## Funding proposal package for FP144

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Costa Rica REDD-plus Results-Based Payments for 2014 and 2015";

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

d) Secretary's assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Report from an accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

### Information on Plantations Used in the GHG Inventory

Information on plantations used in the GHG inventory has been deduced from the 2014 National Agricultural Census.

Costa Rica has enhanced the consistency of the FREL with the GHG inventory, through recalculation of the GHG inventory for the years 1990, 1995 and 2000; to be included in the country's next national communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).

### Forest Definition

Costa Rica has used the definition of FREL and the Technical Annexes on REDD+ results as follows:

- **Minimum forest cover area:** 30%
- **Minimum forest canopy cover:** 30%
- **Minimum height of trees:** 5.0 m

### (b) If a Country is Considered HFLD

Please provide the justification for this classification.

While Costa Rica is not proposing an adjustment to its FREL for being a High Forest Cover and Low Deforestation (HFLD) country, it has been part of the pilot project on HFLD Climate Finance Mobilization®. HFLDs are defined as having more than 50% forest cover and a deforestation rate under 0.22%. Costa Rica can be considered an HFLD country according to the following criteria:

- More than 60% of the total land area is permanently covered by forests.
- According to the Costa Rica Technical Annex on REDD+ results for 2014–2015, the country has 3,193,394 ha of stable forest cover.
- Costa Rica has a long-term trend showing a decreasing trend of average deforestation from primary forest between 1986 to 2015 and a decreasing trend of average deforestation from secondary forest area from 1986 to 2015.

### (c) FREL/FRLR Adjustments for an HFLD Country

If adjustments were made, please provide information that the adjustment is in accordance with Decision 12/CP.17 and does not exceed 10% of the FREL to reflect quantified, documented changes in circumstances during the reference period. The adjustment must be based on a credible assessment of forest carbon dynamics during the eligibility period.

Not applicable: No adjustments have been made.

Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the quantified estimate of the FREL and FRLR. This should be provided according to Decision 12/CP.17, specifically on the modality for FRELR, and whether the raised issues were material or not addressed.

According to the TAR, Costa Rica modified its FREL submission is in overall accordance with the guidelines for the submission of information on FREL/FRLRs (as contained in the annex to decision 12/CP.17).

### Overview of how primary and secondary forests were distinguished in the 1976/1980 map
- Re-analysis of the area classified as "non-forest" and include the main outcome of this verification activity (see Section B.2).
- Providing more robust data in order to support the assumption that secondary forests in 1985/1988 are representative of the forest cover at the time.
- Presenting a comparison of the results of the Cifuentes model (used to predict the rates of biomass accumulation and deforestation) with the field data collected during the verification activity, including issues related to applying IPCC guidance).
- Presenting the results of the analysis of the emissions from deforestation (as an annex).
- Presenting the user's manual for "FREE TOOL CR" - reference-level estimation tool (as an annex).

## Areas of improvement identified by the TAI are being addressed based on the availability of technological and financial resources in the program, so far as can be determined:
1. Identification of primary and secondary forests is not yet addressed due to lack of resources;
2. Classification of forest/non-forest: not addressed yet due to the lack of resources;
3. Aggregation of data at the national level: not yet addressed due to lack of resources;
4. Representativeness of the carbon growth model: Costa Rica has requested funds from the World Bank Land Degradation and Forests (LDCF) Trust Fund for the purchase of a computer to run the Cifuentes (2008) Above-ground Biomass (AGB) growth model in wet and dry forests will be updated and validated using data collected during the verification activity;
5. The 1976/1980 map: 105 topotanical series were measured in different types of secondary forest and ages (See more details in Annex A);
6. Forest inventory: the National Forest Inventory is being updated with new information from the time series information from satellite images, aerial photos, and mosaics of high and medium resolution imagery;
7. The 1985/1988 map: the data used for this map are derived from the Landsat MSS mosaic from the Landscapes 1960 and Sentinel 2015 satellites were used. Based on the information of the pilot project, the TAI is being updated with new information about the areas and conditions of the National Forest Inventory;
8. Accuracy of the carbon growth model: not addressed yet due to lack of resources;
9. Make the "FREE tool" and manual publicly available: "FREE tool" and manual has been made publicly available (see Section B.2);
10. Technical Annex: The Cifuentes model is being validated using data collected during the verification activity.

### Considerations regarding national GHG inventory:
- Significant progress has been made in harmonizing the estimation of forest emissions in the FOLU sector of the GHG Inventory. The methodology for estimating emissions from deforestation and degradation (EDD) was developed by INEGE and is being used as a reference for estimating REDD+ results (see Table 5 in the Technical Annex). Both, Technical Annexes and INGE FOLU inventory are based on the same land use maps. Differences between methodologies are the following:
    - Forest cover: The TAI uses only forested areas while INEGE includes shrubland and ruderal exoise emissions;
    - Dead wood and litter carbon pools are excluded, etc.
- C stocks in above-ground biomass (AGB) of forests lands were estimated using the asymptotic model and validated by Cifuentes (2008).

### Some recommendations considered when presenting this proposal:
- In particular for the REDD+ pilot results (see Section B.2), Costa Rica has an analysis of areas classified as "non-forest", by performing uncertain analysis of "forest" and "non-forest" change categories, and included additional information on the use of the tool to estimate the FREEL and then results FREEL & MINI TOOL CR.

REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL
GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 1 OF 80)

The FREL includes carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions and removals associated to changes in C stocks in the following pools: (For more details see section 2.4 of Modified FREL of Costa Rica and section 7 of ERP of Costa Rica):

- Above-ground biomass (AGB): AGB contains the highest proportion of C stored in forest land, between 80%–95%
- Below-ground biomass (BGB): On average, BGB represents 18% of AGC stocks per hectare. The conversion from land use to forest was included in the FREL for completeness purposes given the availability of high-quality monitoring data.
- Litter (L): Even though litter represents <15% of emissions from forest land conversion and <10% of total C stock, it is included as a separate pool because it is a key component of the carbon cycle and has country-specific data.
- Soil Organic Carbon (SOC): Although potentially a significant carbon pool, organic soil C was excluded from the FREL due to lack of reliable national data to estimate the flux of C in the different land use change categories. The SOC stock is included as a separate pool for estimation of forest land conversion emissions. On the contrary, considering that lands converted to forest land are generally more deforested than its previous land use, it is expected that the net effect on SOC stocks will be negative. Therefore, no data is required for the estimation of C stocks changes.
- Historical emissions from forest land conversion were included based on the limited availability of data.

Regarding ONS and N2O gases, biomass burning and related emissions of methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) were excluded in the estimation of FREL. Before 1997, slash-and-burn was the common practice for land use change in Costa Rica. The country has not yet implemented a national policy to regulate slash-and-burn forestry (Law No. 5840), however, in 1997 conversion of forest became illegal with the Current Forest Law; hence, slash-and-burn emissions are excluded from the FREL.

According to Decision 1/CP.16, paragraph 70) the following activities were included in the FREL: emission reductions from deforestation, and enhancement of forest C stocks. At the moment, sufficient quality data are lacking for the estimation of these two components (For more details see section 2.3 of Modified FREL of Costa Rica and Section 8.3 of ERP of Costa Rica).

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 8 OF 80)

### January 16th, 2014: The "Emission Reduction Project" (ERP) was approved by the GCF Board for the period October 16-17, 2012 including Costa Rica as a participant in the emission reduction program in the period 2010-2020.

#### 2011/12
##### 2013/14

- Sustainable management of forest: Emissions/removals associated with the sustainable management of forests are excluded due to the lack of reliable data. At the same time, it is important to note that total area under forests in Costa Rica has been increasing since 2005 and that most of these areas are not stand-replacing, but rather partial timber volume losses every 15 years. For these reasons, it is very likely that there will be a net increase in forest carbon stocks over the period due to the sustainable management of forests in future revisions of its FREL.

- Degradation of forest: Emission information on forest degradation there were not available during the construction of FREL. The FREL was based on a fraction of the total area under forests, and it is not possible to report on forest carbon stocks. Nonetheless, the FREL did not collect sufficient information on activity data and emissions factors for potential future forest degradation. It is important to note that Costa Rica included forest degradation in its FREL submission as part of the ERP Project of the World Bank Carbon Fund (WBCF) project. The FREL was constructed using a visual assessment of forest cover density which classifies primary forest areas as intact, degraded, and deforested. Forests are considered to be degraded when they have lost more than 50% of their original tree cover density which classifies primary forest areas as intact, degraded, and deforested.

(vi) FREL/FRL consistency: These provide any additional information that supplements the information provided in the TAR. The proposed FREL is based on the 1986-2013 time series used to construct the FREL/FRL, and whether significant issues were raised in the report and whether these issues have been resolved. If not, the FREL should be revised due to time and data restrictions.

In Costa Rica, there are three main milestones regarding the development and implementation of national public policies on forests and climate change: 1986-2005 (first period of enhanced mitigation actions), 1997-2005 (second period of enhanced mitigation actions) and 2010-2023 (third period). The years of each of the periods considered in the FREL milestones have been established based on these milestones.

For the construction of the FREL, a 1986-2013 time series of land use maps was developed. This time series was specifically constructed for the purpose of developing the FREL and is not necessarily representative of the actual historical emissions. The FREL/FRL has been estimated as the sum of the annual average CO₂ net emissions from deforestation and forest degradation during 1986-2013, based on the land use maps developed specifically for this time series when estimating AD. Satellite imagery was collected and analyzed starting for 1985/86, 1991/92, 1997/98, 2003/04, 2008/09, and 2013/14.

Within the FREL, the FRL was based on historical emissions for two contiguous historical reference periods: 1956-1999 and 2009-2013. The proposed FREL/FRL has been estimated as the sum of the annual average CO₂ net emissions from deforestation and forest degradation during 1986-2013, based on the land use maps developed specifically for this time series when estimating AD. Satellite imagery was collected and analyzed starting for 1985/86, 1991/92, 1997/98, 2003/04, 2008/09, and 2013/14.

The TAR considered that additional sampling and the validation of the model developed by Cifuentes would increase the confidence level of FREL submission by Costa Rica (see details in section (ix) issues related to applying IPCC guidance).

**(vii) Sources of emissions: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to whether all activities listed in paragraph 70 of UNFCCC/COP21/INF/4 are significant sources of emissions or not. If they were not, justify whether activities that are significant sources of emissions were not included due to lack of data and/or whether the omission overestimates emissions or underestimates removals. In addition, provide a list of all REDD-plus activities that are significant sources of emissions in future FREL/FRLF development scenarios.**

The national FREL, proposed by Costa Rica for the five contiguous historical reference periods 1986-1998 and 1987-2009 (on the annual average of the carbon dioxide (CO₂) equivalent emissions associated with deforestation), includes the following anthropogenic emissions: a. The inclusion of all anthropogenic emissions associated with deforestation; b. The inclusion of the anthropogenic emissions associated with reforestation and afforestation; c. The inclusion of the anthropogenic emissions associated with orchard activity and meandering, because they are considered to be natural processes. The carbon stocks in forest land for the reference periods 1986-2009 (the five contiguous periods) are considered in Costa Rica's modified submission of 23 May 2016 only for secondary forest; gains and losses from conversion of forest land to other uses, including abandonment, were not included. The carbon stocks in forest land: a. Carbon stock enhancements in forest land remaining forest land were estimated using growth models developed by the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MIDEA) based on data collected between 1986-2009; b. The carbon stocks as a function of age, were applied by determining the age of the forest at the year of the conversion and then applying the appropriate stock enhancement factor for that age.

The TAR acknowledged that Costa Rica included in the FREL the most significant activities, and the most significant sources of emissions relative to forests:

**(ix) Significant pools: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the inclusion of the most significant pools. If they were not, justify whether all other anthropogenic emissions or underestimates removals. In addition, provide a list of all significant pools in future FREL/FRLF development scenarios.**

The carbon pools included in the FREL are: above-ground biomass (trees and non-trees); below-ground biomass (trees and non-trees); dead wood (only above ground); and litter. The soil organic carbon, dead wood (below ground), and litter were not included.

The TAR identified the following additional areas for future technical improvement:

a. The inclusion of the below-ground dead wood in the below-ground biomass pool; and
b. The omission of the soil organic carbon (i.e., the omission of this pool or the provision of more information justifying its omission).

The TAR noted that emissions from deadwood are likely to be insignificant. With regard to emission from the soil organic carbon, the TAR noted that the inclusion of these pools is consistent with the approach used in the emission factors contained in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines. Nevertheless, the TAR noted that the current omission of these pools are unlikely to be leading to an overestimation of emissions.

Costa Rica will consider including these pools in light of the potential inclusion of additional REDD-plus activities such as agroforestry and forest management, in FREL/FRLF submissions.

**(x) Emissions from gases: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the inclusion of all gases that are significant sources of emissions or not. If they were not, justify whether activities that are significant sources of emissions were not included due to lack of data and/or whether the omission overestimates emissions or underestimates removals. In addition, provide a list of all REDD-plus activities that are significant sources of emissions in future FREL/FRLF development scenarios.**

The TAR identified the following additional areas for future technical improvement:

a. The inclusion of CH₄ and N₂O emissions from biomass burning

REDDO-rSBF FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) [PAGE 10 OF 80]

**Background:** Slash-and-burn was the common practice for land use change in Costa Rica, as this was the essential way to convert forests to pastures and crops. Under the old Forest Law, slash-and-burn was legal; however, it became illegal with the current Forest Law; hence, slash-and-burn dramatically decreases after 1998. For this reason, burning, burning and related emissions of methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) were included in conversion of forests to crops and grassland that occurred in the period 1990-1998 and excluded in the post-1998 period.

Nevertheless, the TAR noted that current omission of these gases is unlikely to be leading to an overestimation of emissions from land use change. The FREL/REDDO-rSBF project will not include any of additional REDD+ activities such as forest degraded and sustainable forest management, in future FREL submissions.

For the purpose of this proposal, it is important to recall that the relevant historical period is 1997-2009; when slash-and-burn is not considered.

### (ii) IPCC guidance for FRELLRB. Please indicate if whether the construction of the FREE/LRB (date, location, etc.) was in accordance with the IPCC Guidelines.

The construction of the FREL was guided by the 2006 IPCC Guidelines.

#### [iii] Issues related to applying IPCC Guidance: Please mention any significant issues related to the application of IPCC GLGIP/GSC as noted in the TA report. Include any significant issues that are material to the alignment with the IPCC guidance and/or the FREL project. Any such issues should be described, including how they were raised and resolved. If applicable, provide a plan to address and outcome issues raised in the TA Report that were not addressed or resolved at the time of submission of this proposal, as well as any other relevant restrictions.

The TAR noted that the forest-related carbon stocks used to assess carbon stock changes due to forest land use change and the conversion of land to other land-use categories have been assessed on the basis of a country-specific methodology, and this is not fully in accordance with the 2006 IPCC Guidelines.

Above-ground carbon stocks for secondary forests were estimated using a growth model developed by a national study. The TAR noted that the model used by Costa Rica does not account for the dynamics of above-ground biomass and should be added. In addition, the TAR noted that the model used by Costa Rica does not take into account the carbon stock in dead wood and coarse woody debris (CWD). The TAR also noted that the methodology of the quality of the satellite imagery employed (LandSat) was not sufficient to overcome the special confusion of forest land use change due to the presence of CWD. Therefore, it was not possible to include them as an additional subcategory in the land-use time-change series.

The TAR considered that additional sampling and the validation of the model developed by Osaforest would increase the representativeness of the sample for the assessment of carbon stock changes in secondary forests. In comparison of the results of the Cifuentes model and IPCC default factors, presented by Costa Rica during the review process (which was not included in the TA report), the TAR noted that the Cifuentes model overestimated the biomass and therefore noted that, when estimating carbon stock changes in secondary forests, including all forests (e.g. harvest first, regrowth second) it is necessary to account for the modeling approach embracing the accuracy of the FREL/REDDO-rSBF submission from Costa Rica.

**(ix) Reference period for the FRE/LFL: Please indicate the reference period (number of years) applied for the current proposal.**

The national FRE/LFL used by Costa Rica to assess the results presented in this funding proposal is from 1997-2009, a total of 13 years.

**(ix) If previous reference level submitted: Please indicate whether a previous reference level applied to the project or activity and if so, describe it.**

The FRE/LFL used for Costa Rica's first FRE/LFL was submitted to the UNFCCC and technically assessed through the UNFCCC process. It was submitted and modified in 2016 during the course of the UNFCCC technical assessment process.

**(ix) Uncertainties: Please indicate whether the country has provided information on aggregated uncertainties (of the emissions or removals estimates, taking into account national capabilities and circumstances, and so forth). If applicable, describe the process implemented to minimize systematic and random errors.**

Uncertainty was not estimated for Costa Rica's FRE/LFL. Likewise, uncertainty was not analyzed by the Technical Team of the TAP. The country has provided information on aggregated uncertainties (of the emissions or removals estimates, taking into account national capabilities and circumstances). This was done using Approach 2 of the IPCC 2006 Guidelines, employing Monte Carlo simulations, and the uncertainties are reported in Table 1.

**(ix) Indicate whether different FRE/LFLs have been used for different funding sources or other purposes, and if so, list and describe them.**

Costa Rica has submitted an Emissions Reduction Programs Document (ERPD)[^1] with the aim to receive payments under the REDD+ mechanism.

The UNFCCC FRE/LFL was developed based on historical emissions for 1986-1990 and 1995-2000. However, the reference period 1997-2009 does not comply with indicators 11.1 and 11.2 of the TCFD Methodological Framework.

Indicator 11.1: The end-date for the Reference Period is the most recent date prior to two years before the TAP starts the independent assessment of the draft ER Program Document and for which forest cover data are available. This indicator ensures that the reference period ends at least one year after the country's last major deforestation event, with sufficient time to develop a REDD+ program or climate change strategy.

Indicator 11.2: The start-date for the Reference Period is about ten years before the end-date. An alternative approach may be used only with convincing justification as in Indicator 11.1 and is not more than 15 years behind the end-date.

To establish a reference period consistent with the CFP Carbon Fund requirements, the period between 1998-2011 [^2] was selected for the country's FRE/LFL.

* **End year (2011):** According to Costa Rica’s RPP and PDP[3], in its country’s National REDD+ Strategy being implemented in 2010. However, given that for Costa Rica does not have a major “gap,” the TAP recommended extending the period until 2011 only to comply with the CF-MP. Costa Rica followed the TAP's recommendation.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Base year (1995): 1997 is the year when the current Forest Law was passed, including key forest policy reforms and mechanisms (e.g., PSEA). It also includes a reference level map (please see previous footnote). Selecting 1998 as the base year of the historical reference period would have been more appropriate for the REDD+ Program because it is the year when the current Forest Law became effective. Because this, the reference level can be used as a benchmark to measure emissions reductions and/or forest carbon stock enhancements in Costa Rica under the REDD+ programs. This date was strategically selected to show the impact of the Forest Law and has an important role for REDD+ submissions to the UNFCCC.

### During the Reference Period (2013-2015): In 2016, the Carbon Fund participants decided to provisionally include Costa Rica's Emission Reduction Program Document (ER-PD) into the portfolio of both Tranche A and B. The ER-PD was approved by the UNFCCC in June 2017. The inclusion of the ER-PD into the portfolio of the Carbon Fund was deemed approved upon fulfillment of the update of the reference level, including the assessment of forest degradation and deforestation impacts on emissions with the Carbon Fund's Methodological Framework (CFMF) as described in section 3.3.

#### Indicator 3.3: Emission reduction from forest degradation and deforestation for where such emissions are more than 10% of total forest-related emissions in the Accounting Area, during the Reference Period and during the Term of the ERPA. These estimates are evaluated using the best available data (including proxy activities or data).

To comply with MFJ indicator 3.3, Costa Rica estimated the significance of emissions from forest degradation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks in forests remaining forest land Association (FRLA) areas where forest classified primary forest areas as intact, degraded, and very degraded in the forests remaining forest land Association (FRLA). The estimation was based on the FRLA reference level. Therefore, the emission of forest degradation was included in the FREL prepared for the ER Program of the Carbon Fund.

The CFCP reference level has been estimated as sum of the gross emissions and removals from all REDD-plus activities during 2013-2015. The reference level includes: (i) deforestation; (ii) forest degradation; (iii) enhancement of forest C stocks in forests remaining forest land and regeneration of forest C stocks in secondary forests; (iv) afforestation and reforestation; and (v) the net effect of all REDD-plus activities for 2013-2025. The difference between the UNFCCC-FREL and the CFCP-FRL is the inclusion of degradation in deforestation.

Costa Rica will be able to claim ERPA after the date of unconditional approval of ER-PD. Costa Rica submitted in 2012 the ER-PN, which was approved by the Fund's Donor Committee. With this approval, a letter of intent was signed with the UNFCCC on June 30, 2017. The ER-PD was approved by the UNFCCC on June 29, 2018 for USD $5 million dollars. Costa Rica submitted the final ER-PD on May 24, 2016. The Carbon Fund Participants decided to provisionally include Costa Rica's Emission Reduction Program Document (ER-PD) into the portfolio of both Tranche A and B in 2016.

Therefore, ER-Program's period begins at December 29th, 2017 and ends in December 31st, 2041. During this period, Costa Rica will be able to claim ERPA based on the amount of emissions reductions and/or forest carbon stock enhancements in tons of CO2e based on monitoring events, according to the amounts agreed in the ERPA.

There are no consequence of having different FRELs in the national carbon accounting for Costa Rica because there is only one reference level (FRLA) that includes all REDD-plus activities. The GCF RBP proposal (2014-2015) and the term of the potential ERPA to be signed with the CFCP (2018-2024).

## No major consistency issues over the time series results are expected to be raised.
- Since for the complete time series (1987–2013), images from four different sensors and satellites of the LandSat family were used (LandSat 4, TM, Landsat 5, ETM+, Landsat 6, Thematic Mapper, Landsat 7 Enhanced Thematic Mapper Plus, Landsat 8 Operational Land Imager).
- A mask of the country (in raster format) generated from map MCS 2013/4 of the geo-database was used, to ensure that only land cover classes in the country were included. The mask was created by comparing the number of columns and rows K, Y with the maps of the 1997–2013 time series.
- The MCS 2015/16 map has the same geographical reference system as the previous version (MCS 2013/4) and therefore no transformation is needed. Compare the coordinates of the two maps using the formula: X = 29995(115) in order to compare them geographically with the MCS 2013/4 image to obtain the land use change matrix.

### For the calculation of the activity data
- A cartographic comparison of the wall-to-wall maps MCS 2013/4 and MCS 10/15 is made, to subsequently correct for changes and new pixels in a transition matrix.
- In order to prepare the 2016–2017 time series, the following steps were taken: (i) The wall-to-wall map of the 2013/4 was used as base map. (ii) MCS 2015/16 map, met the following requirements: i. Both maps must be in the same coordinate system; ii. Both maps have the same geographical reference system and not being displaced; iii. Both maps must share the same classification LULC key used for the previous Serra model version.

**Uncertainty of activity data**

The uncertainties of the activity data for land use change activities (deforestation and reforestation) come from the uncertainties associated with the processing of creating land use change maps from which the activity data are obtained. The uncertainty in the land cover map was estimated using a Monte Carlo simulation approach as described by the guidelines from Olsson et al. (2014). The uncertainty estimation for each land cover change class was derived based on the accuracy statistics shown in Table 8.

| Non-stable forest (Forest remaining Non-Forest) | 0.85 | 0.74 |

### Literature
The statistical uncertainty reported in these documents takes into consideration the sampling error. Therefore, the standard deviation estimates only contain this error term.

The uncertainties (the margin of error for a 95% confidence level divided by the estimate) of carbon stocks vary from 1% to 15%. The uncertainty of aboveground biomass (the pool with the largest carbon stock in the different forest types) has the highest uncertainty reaching 152% at the 90th confidence level.

### Appropriate Uncertainty
The uncertainty is estimated by combining the uncertainty of activity data and emission factors as described in the previous section. This combination of uncertainties has been done through Approach 2 of the IPCC 2006 Guidelines, employing Monte Carlo simulations, and the uncertainties are reported in terms of 95% confidence intervals.

### 3. Costa Rica has developed its own domestic offset project to support its national carbon neutrality goal through the REDD+plus RBP. The project is called FONAFIFO (Fondo Nacional de Financiamiento Forestal by the Spanish acronym). This system includes the LULUCF sector and therefore allows FONAFIFO to be valued under the CDM methodology. The project aims at reducing deforestation in Costa Rica, which is currently concentrated on growth forest plantations from the planting year to its last measurement period (2014). The project uses a combination of methodologies for carbon accounting: the CDM and an adapted version of the REDD+ methodology. The project has been designed with the aim of creating fungible units and does not seek to meet the same level of requirements as international standards, but rather to provide a cost-effective mechanism that can be used by Costa Rica to report its results to the UNFCCC in the context of REDD (100% CO2e REDD+ results). Nonetheless, an assessment of the project against the environmental integrity, the legality, the IUCN species, governance and social criteria for 2014 and 2015 have been fully deducted from the volume offered. More specifically, 13,145 US$ and 25,301 US$ where generated in 2014 and 2015 respectively.

Additionally, the following voluntary REDD+ projects have been identified within the country:

### 4. Avoided deforestation through the payment for environmental services program in humid forests located in private lands in Costa Rica. This project is called "Costa Rica: Humid Forests". The project aims to total a 12,000 hectares of privately-owned forest (including some 100 farm owners) and is implemented by the National Association of Community Forestry Cooperatives (ACOCV). This project did not issue any VCS. The project was not implemented because it also required VCS certification.

### 5. Baumholder Mixed Reforestation in Costa Rica: Baumholder has established a reforestation project with native tree species in Costa Rica. The reforestation project comprises a total area of 1,206 ha spread across two sites. The first site is located on the former land of an agricultural farm where the owner had pasturized, previously used for extensive cattle ranching, was reforested. The plantations are managed by Baumholder and are planted with native tree species in order to provide a sustainable source for responsible forestry. To prevent double payments, the ERs achieved by this project for the years 2014 and 2015 were subtracted from the total volume offered to GCF (in 2014 it was 367 CO2e and in 2015 it was 924 CO2e). The project is very small and does not have a significant impact on the size of the GCF proposal (in 2014 it was 502 ICQO and in 2015 it was 5624 ICQO).

### 6. VisionWild - Vizcaya Forest: The VisionWild-Vizcaya project is located in a backward rural region in the central heart of Costa Rica on the edge of the Mayanque Wildlife Refuge. This project is more than a value for money project, it is also an example of how to use the REDD+ methodology to implement new-discovered sustainable land use methods. More than 50% of the EBA (this project area) is covered by diverse tropical forest and the remaining part consists of secondary forests. The VisionWild-Vizcaya project is a former pasturized with autochthonous tree species in close-to-native mixed stands with the aim to restore the natural ecosystem. The project has been designed as a reforestation of Mesoamerican tropical forest. To prevent double payments, the ERs achieved by this project for the years 2014 and 2015 were subtracted from the total volume offered to GCF (in 2014 it was 367 CO2e and in 2015 it was 924 CO2e). The project is very small and does not have a significant impact on the size of the GCF proposal (in 2014 it was 367 ICQO and in 2015 it was 864 ICQO).

A national registry system covering all sectors of the economy is being integrated in the National Climate Change Matrix (NCCM). http://www.greenclimate.fund/ Under the supervision of the Ministry of Environment, the national registry is expected to be fully operational in the second half of 2021.

An interim registry mechanism, covering REDD+ only, will be established. FONAFIFO will develop a simplified spreadsheet to list REDD+ transactions, to be publicly available at FONAFIFO webpage. For the interim registry, the results paid by the GCF will not be transferred offered for future payment or otherwise used (for example offsets). The administrator of the interim registry as well as for the future economy-wide registry will be responsible for ensuring that the results paid by the GCF are accounted for in accordance with the accounting approach towards its NDC.

In order to ensure that the results paid by the GCF will not be transferred, offered for future payment or otherwise used (for example offsets), the administrator of the interim registry as well as for the future economy-wide registry will be responsible for ensuring that the results paid by the GCF are accounted for in accordance with the accounting approach towards its NDC.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Figure 2: Optimization of REDD+ results

- Provide information on how different financing contributed to the achieved results.

The National REDD+ Strategy is a multifaceted initiative to achieve results at the national scale. Costa Rica has and will continue to use many public and private international and domestic sources of financing to support its policies and programs in this area. The most important of these are mentioned in section D.1 on input potential, it is not possible to directly attribute emission reductions to any single source of funding. However, the REDD+ Strategy has been implemented with the financial support of many others alongside many others. The implementation of this complete package of policies and measures has already led to emissions reductions that are greater than those achieved by the individual components.

That being said, the vast majority of the resources that Costa Rica has used are domestic and the contribution of international sources while most welcomed is relatively small in financial terms.

Costa Rica has been a strong proponent of green, sustainable and resilient development, particularly in regard to the protection of its natural environment. This commitment is enshrined in its Political Constitution. Costa Rica has provided for the fundamental right of a "healthy and ecologically balanced environment, and the responsibility of the State to protect it". The country's environmental policies have been implemented by a privileged political party and enjoys popular support, although some areas are recognized to have more progress than others.

Existing forestry policies and programs implemented in the last three decades have played a key role in achieving drivers of deforestation. The main instruments have been the National Conservation Area System (SINAC) by its Spanish acronym, which was established in 1970; the National Forest Service (SINAFOR), created in 1983.

Thanks to the SINAC, Costa Rica has protected a significant portion of its territory (26%) as Protected Conservation Areas since 1970. Ecotourism, a national GDP driver, positively affects rural economies, especially in contrast with other countries where it is often associated with deforestation and environmental degradation.

FONAFIFO's PES program was also instrumental in achieving early REDD+ results. FONAFIFO’s PES program is based on the polluter pays principle. As of 2016, the PES compensated environmental services in 34 municipalities across the country. The program has been implemented for more than a decade and economically depressed rural areas reaching over 18,000 different beneficiaries. For the specific years 2014 and 2015, a total of $7 million was delivered to rural communities as compensation in exchange for carbon credits modality. In 2014 PES of slightly over ZIM was delivered while for 2015, the amount of payment delivered was over 23.1 M USD.


---
**Page 23**
---

The REDD+ plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) (PAGE 1 OF 80)

This TIP is mainly financed by 33% of the national water and taxes for water use. Between 2010 and 2015, 79% of the funding for the Costa Rican PES program came from the government budget, with only 2% coming from private initiatives. The rest has been covered by donations and debt from international financial institutions (IFIs). The main source of financing is the World Bank, which provides grants from World Bank known as Ecomercados I and II. Ecomercados' overarching goal was to secure the conservation of biodiversity in Costa Rica through long-term sustainability by implementing market-based mechanisms for payment of environmental services.

Over the last 5 years the PES program has been fully funded by the national carbon tax and water fee, and international financial institutions (IFIs).

All of the statistics related to the PES programme are publicly accessible online at: http://www.fondoct.org.co/en/servicios/estadisticas-de-pes

The FCPP readiness fund and the UN REDD program have also made a financial contribution to the RBP. This was realized through the provision of US$ 10,000 for the PES readiness (including the SEPA process), US $200,000 for preparing the RPMs; this was provided by U.S.$ 8 million for readiness (including the SEPA process, information and pre-consultation process, and funding). The UN REDD program has also provided US$1.5 million for the PES readiness. As of this date there is no national registry of transactions; however, Costa Rica acknowledges the need for such a tool as laid out above. A national registry system covering all sectors of the economy being integrated into the National System of Environmental Information (SINAE) under the supervision of the Climate Change Directorate of the Ministry of Environment and Energy. This national registry is expected to be fully compliant with the Cancun Safeguards, including the requirement for a transactional registry that covers first transfer, transfer, acquisition, cancellation and use towards NDCs or towards other mitigation purposes, including REDD+ projects. The registry will also include all information on transactions that are necessary that will be defined under Art 5 of the Paris Agreement and include all the sectors that take part in emissions reductions: agriculture, forestry, industry and biodiversity. It is important to note that there are no REDD-plus subnational projects in Costa Rica.

# C.1.1 Compliance with Cancun Safeguards

Please provide any additional information that supplements the information provided in the REDD+ Strategy and/or the National Forest Programme (NFP) document.

Costa Rica considers that the National REDD+ Strategy, adopted in 2010 and that policies and measures under implementation since then have been in line with the available UNFCCC guidance and COP decisions. The country has also implemented a number of other policies and measures related to forests and biodiversity conservation, having implemented since 1997 a scheme administered by FONAFIFO that pays landholders for reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD) and restoring river flows and storing carbon through a PES scheme. Other important policies include the prohibition of commercial logging in primary forests, the creation of the National Forest Fund (FONAFIFO), and the Fire Management Plan enacted in 1997, which set guidelines for planning, monitoring and evaluation of various activities related to fire management.

Reduction of deforestation and increasing forest cover since the 1980s in Costa Rica is explained by a combination of the command-and control measures described above, and the positive effects of incentive programmes. The PES programme was created as an additional measure to complement these efforts. It has been the primary incentive-based program operating during the period for which results-based payments are sought (2014-2015). The PES programme is a voluntary scheme that provides landholders with incentives to create and maintain forest cover on their lands, in accordance with the Forest Law No. 75/78, is consistent with the national forest program and relevant international conventions and agreements.

Costa Rica’s policies and regulations (P&Rs) provisions including fiduciary controls, transparency provisions, a monitoring framework, reporting requirements, and other safeguards are available at FONAFIFO website.

The PES programme was created and has been implemented consistently with Costa Rica's Constitution and policies. It is consistent with the country’s obligations under international law related to forest management, biodiversity conservation, public participation (particularly of indigenous peoples and minorities), among others (see the ESA in Annex XIII) and P&Rs review).

Moreover, the PES programme is aligned with Costa Rica’s climate change objective of becoming a Carbon Neutral Country by 2050. The PES programme has been designed to meet the country's commitments under the Paris Agreement, supporting the implementation of the NDC. Indeed, Costa Rica commits itself to reducing its greenhouse gas emissions by at least 37% below business as usual (BAU) levels in 2030 and carbon neutrality based on the mitigation potential of the Forestry sector. Moreover, it makes specific reference to biodiversity conservation and forest restoration as key elements for achieving these objectives.

The PES programme is also consistent with Costa Rica’s climate change mitigation are reflected in the country's GHG inventories. Furthermore, the PES programme has been designed to meet the criteria established by the UNFCCC for a carbon credit mechanism (ecosystem-based adaptation).

In addition, the PES programme has been demonstrated and supported by thoroughly documented success. Between 2014 and 2015, the period for which the results-based payments are sought, the PES programme:

i. Was a critical contributor at the national level to the registered reduction of emissions of 14,974 TCO2-e for the year.

ii. Signed 1,371 new contracts signed with land titholders of the 17,778 contracts since signed in 1997.

iii. Generated benefits under areas of conservation, reforestation, natural regeneration and agroforestry systems; and

iv. Provided incentives, and therefore benefits to over 1,371 individuals (50% of those beneficiaries being women).

v. Received $141,142,875 Costa Rican colones in incentive payments to beneficiaries.

Such results are consistent with the objectives of national forest programmes and relevant international conventions and agreements.

As required by UNFCCC REDD decisions, a Safeguards information system (SIS) has been developed for providing information on how the activities of the PES and other policies and measures contemplated under the National Forest Policy are consistent with the Carcun Safeguards. The SIS is based on the national forest governance structures, taking into account national legislation and sovereignty.

Transparency and disclosures are required by Costa Rica's national legislation for all government implemented programs and projects. These requirements are also applicable to PES. The PES programme has progressively included PIS provisions including fiduciary contracts, transparency provisions, a monitoring and evaluation framework, and regular performance reports. The PES programme is subject to transparency laws, main statistics, monitoring and audit reports are publicly available in FONAFIFO's website.

FONAFIFO includes in its website up-to date statistics on the following parameters: number of PES contracts disaggregated by type of contract (e.g., conservation, reforestation, afforestation, agroforestry); PES contracts benefiting indigenous peoples; budget and expenditures; and requirements for the program.

In accordance with applicable laws and regulations, the PES programme is subject to fiduciary contracts, transparency provisions, a monitoring and evaluation framework, and a requirement of regular performance reports. As requested by Costa Rica's Comptroller Office for all public offices, FONAFIFO has in place a system to receive, register, investigate and resolve complaints from citizens regarding the PES programme. These grievances are received and resolved. The majority of the grievances were related to delays in processing the contracts and payments, and/or lack of transparency (e.g., not knowing how much money was allocated for each contract, or feeling uncomfortable seating and lack of air conditioning). According to the annual reports of FONAFIFO's grievance mechanism, the number of complaints received has been decreasing over time. In 2014, during the implementation of the PES programme with indigenous peoples (IPs), it was observed that IPs were not always aware of their rights and obligations under the contracts. With implementation of the PES programme were evidenced, once identified, corrective measures were taken to improve the situation. The number of IPs participating in the PES programme has increased from 105 IPs in 2014 to 397 IPs in 2016. In the case of the special PES programmes for indigenous peoples (IPs), the number of IPs participating in these programmes, limited the area for participation to 300 hectares before 2014, allowing up to 600 ha in 2014 and in the case of the special PES programme for indigenous peoples (IPs) in 2015, the limit was increased to 900 ha. In 2016, IPs had interest on submitting larger areas to the Programme. In response, the special PES programmes for indigenous peoples (IPs) were extended to 3,000 hectares. The special PES programme for indigenous peoples (IPs) is known by its Spanish acronym as briefly described below.

The PES programme recognizes the rights of indigenous peoples and members of vulnerable communities, by taking into account relevant international obligations, national circumstances, and the provisions contained in the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

Since the PES programme began, FONAFIFO has been committed to ensuring respect for the rights of indigenous peoples (IPs) and their participation. The PES programme is designed with the objective of promoting IPs' active participation. The PES programme in Costa Rica in its latest phase aims to strengthen and provide positive incentives for IPs to conserve forest ecosystems, while respecting their cultural values and traditions, and consolidating their territories as essential protected areas for the conservation of forest ecosystems. In this regard, FONAFIFO has been committed to ensuring that IPs have existing conditions to access the PES that were not necessarily aligned with specific conditions in IPs territories.

Recognizing that when the program started the conventional PES modality did not contain special provisions for indigenous peoples (IPs), FONAFIFO designed a special PES programme for IPs. The maximum amount of the contract (600 ha) as maximum is opposed to 300 ha for other contract holders). Despite being a larger area for IPs, this does not mean that they have more rights than other contract holders. In fact, it is the opposite: the special PES programme for indigenous peoples was designed in full consultation with indigenous peoples through their ADs (Asociaciones de Derechos) and with the support of FONAFIFO. The special PES programme includes a special provision for allowing the use of 2% of the tenure for subsistence activities. In addition, to renew contracts in the PES programme, IPs have the right to request an extension of their contract.

Moreover, following the constitutional mandates and international Agreements during the period 2014-2018 the government of Costa Rica developed a direct on the consultation mechanisms with IPs to ensure FONAFIFO operational transparency. The result was the development of a special PES modality specifically designed for IPs in order to facilitate their participation in the PES programme and to ensure that they have access to the benefits generated by this mechanism.

Costa Rica carried out multiple participatory processes ensuring that key stakeholders were able to participate effectively, including representatives from indigenous peoples (IPs) and other relevant actors. The process was based on a participatory approach that included the following stages: 1) identification of IPs' needs; 2) design of the special PES modality specifically designed for IPs in order to facilitate their participation in the PES programme and ensure they have access to the benefits generated by this mechanism.

Moreover, the special PES programmes for indigenous peoples recognize indigenous territories as areas of effective conservation and protection of biological diversity, managed by IPs and including sustainable use of

## (v) The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and communities, in decision-making processes

The Constitution in Costa Rica establishes a mandate to ensure an equal and effective participation of citizens in decision making processes, ensuring access to information and justice. As a result, and in the context of environmental policies, IPs are represented by their own organizations (IPs) or through their representatives on environmental councils, regional conservation councils (CORAC), and the National Council on Conservation Areas (CONACAR). The latter is composed of 15 members appointed by the President of the Republic. The IP Committees (COPIRENAS) include that the participation of IPs, local Councils on Biological Corridors and the National Biodiversity Fund.

Incorporately since 1997, the PES design and implementation became highly participatory, involving national regional and local stakeholders, relevant government agencies, the private sector, indigenous peoples and communities. The process was initiated in 2005 with the creation of the National REDD+ Strategy. In July 2013, identifying at groups that should be involved in planning, implementation and monitoring. Participatory processes are carried out through a series of workshops and meetings. IPs are invited to participate as basic requirements are invited to participate. As a result, 17,776 PES agreements have been put in place since 2005.

## The National REDD+ Strategy

The National REDD+ Strategy was consulted at the national level with a methodology that had three phases: i) identification of the actors and their roles (ADs) according to their social characteristics and geographic location, established for four regions: Central, North, South and Pacific. The ADs are organized in regional networks called FONAGA (Fondo Nacional de Agua y Gestión Ambiental). They work via definition of an AD for REDD-plus. The ADs are responsible for the implementation of the strategy at the national level. The FONAGAs are established as a coordination entity for several territories in each region. FONAGA delegated to the ADs all the logistics and financial resources required during the participatory process.

Between 2013 and 2014, the National Agricultural Research and Higher Education Center (CATIE) developed a program of cultural mediators* that spoke native indigenous languages, and which were sent to the communities to carry out activities: i) delivering information about the NBS and PES in culturally appropriate ways ii) gathering recommendations and proposals for pre-consultation as part of the pre-consultation process.

During 2012-2015 and under the coordination of CATIE, pre-consultations were carried out over 180 stakeholders engaged in REDD+ activities taking place in the country, including two-day meetings, dialogues and capacity building workshops, and analysis of PES by the regional consultative groups. In order to review the PES modalities for IPs, a special process was developed with the objective of ensuring that IPs are more active in the special modality for IPs, which has been in implementation since 2016.

In the pre-consultation process in 2015, IPs requested the development of a mechanism for consultation for REDD+ plus. The process to develop this mechanism was carried out with the participation of IPs and other peoples, was carried out under a broad participatory process. FONAGA established partnerships with a large number of organizations that represent IPs in Costa Rica, including COPIRENAS, CONACAR, and others.

(v) That actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity, especially in areas where they are threatened or degraded, and that they do not lead to the conversion of natural forests, but are instead used to incentivize the protection and conservation of existing forests and their ecosystem services, and to enhance other social and environmental benefits.

Costa Rica has a series of instruments to protect priority areas of natural forests and biological diversity. One law of Environmental Conservation (Ley de Conservación del Ambiente) No 740 (1983) established the obligation of the State to conserve, protect and administer forest resources and regulates the use of these resources in accordance with the principles of sustainable development and environmental protection. The PES program was designed under the above-mentioned legal and policy framework, and was designed as a complement to other instruments such as the creation of biodiversity, implementation of the forestry law and strengthening of the national protected area system.

The Forestry Law under which the PES programme prohibits the cutting or use of forest in national parks biological reserves and natural areas. The Forest Code (Ley de Bosques) No 741 (1983) establishes that on the other hand Art. 11 establishes a total prohibition to change the use of forest land: "On forest-covered lands, no changes shall be made in their use or nature without prior authorization from the competent authority". The implementation of the PES programme, procedures to address possible situations where adverse impacts occur in relation to the conservation and protection of natural forests and biological diversity.

For example, in the cases where there is breach of contract or reports relate to possible adverse impacts in PES projects, the Forest Code provides that the competent authority may suspend the use of forest land. If such suspension occurs, either corrective measures are taken, or the permanent suspension of the contract is determined. The suspension of contracts is a measure that can be applied to all types of reforestation and afforestation modalities and are related to low capacities/difficulties to implement reforestation measures. For example, in 2014 there were 35 cases where the suspension of contracts was applied due to adverse impacts associated with cutting down trees in PES forest areas; such cases where as follows: one where 2 trees were cut and another case where 6 trees were cut in a protected area of the Monteverde Cloud Forest Reserve and the Humorito Obras. On another note, for the results period, in 2014 there were 12 cases reported under the conservation and protection of natural forests and biological diversity.

The PES develops instruments for the recognition of environmental services of mitigation of GHG emissions (fixation, reduction, sequestration, storage and absorption), protection of water for urban, rural or hydroelectric use, among others. The PES also provides incentives to promote sustainable land management practices, genetic improvement, protection of ecosystems, ways of life and natural scenic beauty for tourism and scientific purposes, as well as the conservation of biological diversity in areas where it is threatened or degraded by small and medium-scale producers, including IP and local communities. Finally, under the improved PES modality for forests, there are specific provisions to address adverse impacts during the project processes; there are special provisions to ensure equitable benefit sharing and benefit distribution plans.

The forestry law recognizes four of the main ecosystem services including carbon sequestration and storage (biological diversity), water protection (hydroelectric use), soil conservation (agriculture) and timber production (timber industry). The forest is a highly dynamic system, with high levels of variability and opens to a broad number of stakeholders (17,776,330 hectares are in place since 1979/8). The Program

## Green Climate Fund (Page 1 of 80)

Is based on four fundamental pillars: Institutional strengthening, Legal Framework, Financing and Monitoring and Evaluation. The GCF has dedicated operation manuals [1] to each pillar.

Since its creation, more than one million hectares of forest in Costa Rica have been part of the PES programme schemes at one time or another and as a result forest cover has returned to over 50% of the country's land area from a low point of around 23% in 1986. The PES programme was launched in 1997, with the first contracts signed in 1998.

The PES programme has covered 1'262,720 Ha, where 1'134,072 Ha have been under the conservation modality (57% of the total area), 100,000 Ha under the sustainable forest management modality and 98,648 Ha under the agroforestry modality.

The PES programme has covered 24.36% of Na's natural regeneration ("PES"). While deforestation and forest degradation continually increase in Na, the PES programme has been able to maintain a net gain of forest cover since its inception. The PES programme covers Protected Wild Areas (SPA) and its PES program, which together cover approximately 35% of the country and 70% of the protected areas.

The forestry sector is one of the largest sectors in Na's economy, employing more than 120,000 people or about 52% of the country's territorial area (SINAC, 2013). Thanks to this, the forestry sector has become a net emission sink. The PES programme has been able to maintain and even increase forest cover in Na while generating an estimate of around 11 million tons for a result of avoided deforestation by the PES programme between 1998 and 2014.

The PES programme is one of the most successful examples of market-based mechanisms. The results demonstrate that developing countries can reduce emissions in the forestry sector while maintaining vital functions such as biodiversity conservation, water supply, carbon sequestration and other ecosystem services linked to key environmental and economic resources, especially to small and medium producers in rural areas.

The PES programme has been widely cited serving largely as a reference for the design of REDD-plus policy/strategic frameworks at a global level.

According to the PES manual for the implementation of all modalities, records on the status before receiving payments is required. Moreover, to ensure conservation of natural forests and avoid conversion, reforestation or afforestation, the complete conversion of degraded lands and pastures. The latter allows the enhancement of forest carbon stocks.

During 2014-2015, five different types of PES contracts were in place:

* **Forest conservation contracts:** where payments ranged between US$397 and US$16 per hectare in a ten-year period (20 years for reforestation). Payments are made on an annual basis. Contracts have a five-year period, for forest conservation easements. Eight-five percent of contracts in the PES programme support forest conservation in primary and secondary forest areas. Contracts are signed for five years and can be renewed depending on financial performance.
* **Sustainable forest management contracts:** payments ranged between US$232 (2014) and US$477 (2015) per hectare, with a ten-year period (20 years for reforestation). Payments are made on an annual basis. Nine percent of contracts in the PES programme support sustainable forest management. Landowners must maintain at least 30% of their land under natural vegetation and must not convert any primary or secondary forest areas.
* **Reforestation contracts:** payments ranged between US$120 and US$ 196 per hectare with introduced species, and US$45 to US$78 for native species. Payments are made on an annual basis. Contracts have a ten-year or ten-year period (depending on the year of signature), for reforestation easements. Landowners must maintain at least 30% of their land under natural vegetation and must not convert any primary or secondary forest areas. Six percent of contracts in the PES programme support restoration of degraded and abandoned lands.
* **Natural Regeneration:** payments around US$180, disbursed over a five-year period (20 years). This is a contract for reforestation with native species.
* **Agroforestry contracts (newly modified, implemented since 2003)**: Payments ranged between 1.21 and 1.73 US$ per tree, disbursed over a three-year period.

(vi) Actions address the risks of reversals.

In terms of environmental sustainability, the surrounding PLRs and implementation manuals, templates, and guidelines are designed to ensure that the PES programme is a model of deforestation reduction, equity, and inequality, all while maintaining and enhancing natural capital. In fact, Costa Rica is one of the few countries in the world where there has been an increase in forest cover over the past decades attributed to a combination of command and control measures and policy instruments that have been put into place since 1983. The PES programme was established under Law No. 7540, which prohibits the cutting or use of forests in national parks belonging to the National System of Conservation Areas (SINAC) and other protected areas. Article 26 of Law No. 7540 and on the other hand Art. 19 establishes a total prohibition to change the use of forest land. “On forest-covered land, it is prohibited to carry out any activity that results in deforestation or forest plantations.” This itself constitutes one of the key elements that reduce the risk of reversals in Costa Rica’s PES.

Under the PES contracts, the release of economic incentives is tied to regular monitoring and mandatory periodic reporting. The PES programme also includes a number of measures aimed at ensuring long-term environmental sustainability into the PES programme, continue to foster good governance at the national level, and ensure that the PES programme strengthens environmental management and protection for working with the social actors, not just as beneficiaries but as key partners in the implementation of programmes, specially its new modality for indigenous peoples, the monitoring and implementing of objectives.

The primary focus of the PES programme is conservation, reforestation, and sustainable forest management (benefits). The PES programme also includes a number of measures aimed at ensuring long-term environmental sustainability into the PES programme, continue to foster good governance at the national level, and ensure that the PES programme strengthens environmental management and protection for working with the social actors, not just as beneficiaries but as key partners in the implementation of programmes, specially its new modality for indigenous peoples, the monitoring and implementing of objectives.

In addition, two of the PES (conservation, reforestation and sustainable forest management contracts) imply that landowners must commit to maintain forest areas for 15 to 20 years after the contracts ends. These measures evidence that the PES programme contemplates measures that reduce the risk of reversals.

It is also important to note that the PES programme has been strengthened in its long-term goals: the programme stores a significantly larger amount of carbon as compared to unmanaged areas. Sierra and Buscaglia (2013) estimated that the PES programme will store 45 million metric tons of CO2 by 2020, which is equivalent to 8% of Costa Rica’s total emissions in 2010. By the year 2030, a review of several sub-national studies of the PES programme: Denzau et al (2010) highlights that the cost of maintaining forest cover under the PES programme is estimated to be US$5 per hectare per year in this scenario. This highlights the importance of Costa Rica’s long-term commitment to funding its national PES programme over the past 25 years.

Since 2007, the PES has strengthened coordination mechanism to monitor forest cover improving methodologies for the estimation of activity data, achieving more robust information for decision-making processes. As part of this work a early warning system for forest fires has been strengthened, allowing quicker responses and better management.

Additional information on measures taken by Costa Rica to avoid reversals were examined in the ESA of the PES programme found in the Annex X(III).

In line with the minimal risk of reversals, as discussed above, and recognizing the utility of buffers to address the risk of emissions leakage, the National REDD+ Strategy has been adjusted to include a buffer of 10% of the 248,945 CO2e which has been deducted from the volume eligible for result-based payment in this funding proposal.

## (iv) Actions to reduce displacement of emissions

Given that there is a wide range of policies and measures in the National REDD+ Strategy to address the drivers of deforestation on a national scale, the risk of displacement is reduced. The National REDD+ Strategy is a multi-sectoral strategy which includes actions by different actors (e.g., public sector, private sector, international partnerships of different enabling parties) (e.g., inter-institutional coordination) and direct investments made in the areas to be Subsidiary to farming). It is important to recognize that Costa Rica is implementing its National REDD+ Strategy.

A critique of the Costa Rica PES scheme has been that it does not directly address the issue of displacement; that is, it does not explicitly consider whether or not the avoided deforestation in one area is simultaneously deforesting another plot of land (Ross et al. 2006). However, the literature suggests that the threat of leakage in Costa Rica is small—(Page 500).

Furthermore, Costa Rica has an enacted policy on the prohibition of land-use change in forested areas enacted by the Forest Law No. 75/1996 which greatly mitigates the risk of displacement associated with the REDD scheme.

Having a national FREL and national forest monitoring system in place has allowed Costa Rica to monitor possible displacement risks, and to ensure that any displacement is compensated for through the National Fund for Compensation (FNC). The results plus results can be measured, reported and verified at the national scale, in line with UNFCCC requirements of transparency.

Further information on measures taken to avoid displacement can be found in the EIA.

### 2.3.1.2 REDD+ readiness phase

Please describe and provide evidence that the Carbon safeguards information was made transparently available to stakeholders.

Most of the social environmental principles addressed by the Carbon Safeguards have been part of Costa Rica's legislation for the last 20 years, in compliance with the Constitution mandate to ensure a healthy and ecologically balanced environment.

The REDD+ readiness phase process carried out into Costa Rica during the REDD-plus readiness phase (2008-2019) included discussions on social and environmental safeguards. Over 180 participatory stakeholder engagement workshops were held, including two national-level workshops, three regional-level workshops, capacity building workshops, and analysis of proposals by the regional territorial groups (BTR acronym in Spanish).

Costa Rica regulated governance arrangements as well as the stakeholder engagement platforms for REDD-plus in the National Forest Law No. 75/1996, which was amended in 2018 to include provisions on REDD-plus (and onwards). Additional detail on all the different stakeholder engagement platforms, boards and secretariats that were established in both cases is provided below.

The Executive Decree No. 3755-MINEF defined governance for the Readiness phase of plus REDD when FONAFIFO was the responsible party for REDD-plus in Costa Rica, referring to MINEF for the establishment of the National REDD+ strategy, the National REDD+ Strategy Committee and the REDD+ Executive Committee. To ensure the importance of the National REDD+ Strategy, it was defined by an office specifically created within the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINEE) called the National REDD+ Secretariat. The National REDD+ Strategy Committee is composed of representatives from the following sectors: Producers, small and medium Forest Producers, Government, Academic sector and Civil Society. In order to put in place a governance structure for the implementation phase of REDD-plus, the National REDD+ Strategy Committee established that public institutions shall name focal points to address REDD-plus. The aim was to have these focal points as part of the national coordination mechanism for REDD-plus. Representatives from the non-norm government sector that support the National REDD+ Strategy's implementation also participate.

During the implementation of UN-REDD Targeted Support in Costa Rica during 2014-2016 work was carried out to identify and address the country’s safeguards concerns, which were later resolved at the first national clarification of the Carbon Safeguards. The SMC, and environmental NGOs participated in this process.

The National REDD+ Strategy identified and the relationship with social and environmental safeguards was clarified by the National REDD+ Strategy Committee. This resulted in the identification of actions to address potential risks. Such actions were incorporated into the National REDD-CCAD-giz program amongst others.

In 2011, Costa Rica carried out a Strategic and Social Environmental Analysis workshop with over 110 participants from multiple sectors including small and medium producers, IPs, Academia, NGOs, timber industry owners, international organizations, government representatives and civil society. The results of this workshop were used to inform the National REDD+ Strategy and the relationship with social and environmental safeguards was clarified by the National REDD+ Strategy Committee. This resulted in the identification of actions to address potential risks. Such actions were incorporated into the National REDD-CCAD-giz program amongst others.

## C.2. Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits

### C.2.1. General description:

**Purpose:** Describe how the proceeds will be reinvested in activities consistent with the country’s NDC, national strategy, and REDD+ Strategy.

**Description:** The proposed use of funds for this GCF RBP project is focused on improving and expanding public policies that have proven to be successful over the last 25 years for the implementation of the Forestry Law. The GCF RBP project will support the Government of Costa Rica (GoCR) in implementing a new policy framework for REDD+ as a policy instrument to guarantee forest conservation and carbon (C) stock enhancement through reforestation, afforestation, and sustainable forest management activities. This is done so as well to strengthen fire prevention measures which have proven their effectiveness in the recent past.

Costa Rica has put forward ambitious Carbon Neutrality goals in pre-2030 timeframe commitments and its NDC which includes a target of 15% reduction in GHG emissions by 2030. The GCF RBP project contributes to this objective by reinforcing Costa Rica’s political will to reduce emissions, conserve forest carbon stocks, and increase the amount of CO2 sequestered through reforestation and afforestation activities.

Another objective of the GCF RBP project is to further increase participation of all stakeholders in the PES programme, both public and private, including indigenous peoples.

The proposed project fully fits in with the National REDD+ Strategy. Table 12 below highlights the direct relation between the project outputs and activities and the policies and measures identified in the National REDD+ Strategy.

## Output 1 Enabling Conditions are in place for effective REDD+ implementation

### Activity 1.1 Security implementation of safeguards provisions
Costa Rica has made significant progress on national safeguards, including:
- Submission to the UNFCCC of its first SOI on safeguards and establishing a SIS.
- Costa Rica has completed its national classification of forests and is in the process of developing a National Forest Inventory (NFI) for use as input into the national approach to safeguards.

### Activity 1.2 Monitoring and reporting of REDD+ implementation
Costa Rica's Forest National Monitoring System (FNSM) was consolidated in 2019 and is composed by:
- A Terrestrial Satellite Monitoring System (SMST).
- An INF.
- The SMST, national data on changes in use and condition of forests, as well as the estimation of emissions and removals to be reported in the National Inventory of GHG for the AFOLU sector.

### Activity 1.3 Capacity building
The FNSM is also known by its acronym SIMOCOTE (Sistema Nacional de Monitoreo de la Cobertura y el Uso de la Tierra y Ecosistemas en Sistema).

This activity will strengthen national capacities for REDD+ monitoring, reporting and verification.
Furthermore,
- Support will be provided to meet the requirements of emerging market standards such as "The REDD+ Environmental Performance Standard (EPS)" and "The Verified Carbon Standard (VCS) - Artisanal and Pygmy Peoples' Rights (ART) Program".
- Market standards can be combined with Wawaes Framework for REDD+ result-based payments to maximize the potential of REDD+ in Costa Rica.
- The activity will support the development of a diversified strategy for capturing REDD+ result-based payments from market and non-market sources based on international partnerships in line with the San José Declaration.

### Activity 1.4 Updating the FRS
The FRS will be updated to reflect methodological improvements in response to technical assistance provided by GCF, including:
- The National Forest Inventory (NFI) and the NDC monitoring framework.
- Progress towards the development of a national REDD+ inventory.
- Support for participation of Costa Rica in market mechanisms including the REDD+ Environmental Performance Standard (EPS), VCS - ART Program, and other REDD+ transaction programs (MVs).
- Support for validation and verification processes.

### Output 2 - Payment for Environmental Services (PES) and Fighting forest fires

#### Activity 2.1: Expanding and Improving the Payment for Environmental Services Program

The PES program is an instrument created by the Forestry Law, with over 20 years of effective application and has received public and private investments. The basic concept of the PES programme is a voluntary contract through which landowners agree to protect their forest in exchange for financial compensation. This can be a participant if and only if the participant conducts the agreed land-use practices (currently). The PES program covers four main modalities: i) conservation, ii) sustainable management of forest, iii) reforestation iv) natural forest regeneration and v) agroforestry systems.

Figure 4 shows the total area covered by each modality.

## Figure 4 Total # Hectares per Modality in PES Program Historical (1997-2018)

| Modality | Total # Hectares |
| --- | --- |
| Forest Protection (13,562.0 Ha) | 59% |
| Natural Regeneration (28,066.0 Ha) | 24% |
| Post Harvest Protection (7,713.0 Ha) | 11% |
| Reforestation (7,713.0 Ha) | 0% |

Source: FORAFNIGE https://www.forafnige.go.cr

Note: Agroforestry systems are not included as the measure is in of trees rather than Ha; data not comparable.

Through this activity 2.1, the existing PES programme will be expanded to cover approximately 30,500 hectares aiming to cover additional demand to participate in the Program, with an estimated tentative cost per hectare of US $85.00 (US$7.40 for reforestation and US$62.00 for forest protection).

The official regulation covering the PES operations manual can be found [here](#). An English summary of PES operations manual can be found in Annex XIXII.
