The key element of the Indigenous people’s PES modality presented in the description of activity 2.2.

## Requirements for applicants and properties

The basic requirements for all PES applications to the PES Program are the following: copy of the Farm's map in its original scale; duly signed application form; an orderly, complete and readable manner including contacts information; a copy of the land title deed; and a copy of the project plan. For agricultural activities, it is necessary to present a certification by SINAC indicating that the management plan was completed.

The requirements for properties and they must be: officially registered in the National Registry; be located in the country; have no encumbrances or mortgages; and be free from any other legal restrictions. Properties that have constituted mortgages will not be admitted except when in the public deed the mortgages authorization is indicated, and the mortgage has been paid off. The property must be registered in the national registry for the implementation period of the project. The PES operations must include a land title deed or an authorization to use (legal title, under possession) in line with article 8 in Law N 8940, and section 39 in the regulation of Forestry Law.

Requests presented for the SAF modality where the properties have constituted mortgages may enter the PES without the authorization of the nonproperty.

1. At any time throughout the implementation of a RED project areas may be visited by FONAFIFO's Professional Auditors, the School of Agricultural Engineering and/or the National Forest Service to review and verify the status of the area and information included in certifications.

Finaly, FONAFIFO's monitoring and control department carries out internal audits every year. In this process they randomly select 10% of the contracts for audit. All of the selected farms are visited to verify their implementation in order to ensure compliance with the terms and conditions of the contract. The results of these visits are used as a basis for the annual report submitted by FONAFIFO. Additionally, satellite monitoring tools it into the auditing process to identify Farms and territories with FON contracts that may have been incorrectly identified. These can be selected for field visits and audits, making the Monitoring and review process more agile and cost-effective.

# 6. Withdrawals and Revisions

There is a high demand to participate in the Program and strict selection criteria hence, beneficiaries go through important processes of selection, withdrawals from the PES scheme are not common. The majority of contract cancellations to date, are due to lack of compliance, especially under the reforestation modality. It is important that FONAFIFO's monitoring and control department carries out internal audits every year in order to improve potential difficulties to avoid cancelling contracts where possible, when non-compliance is partial and still has some potential for improvement. In 2015, 37% of the total 721 non-compliant events occurred because of lack of compliance with the terms and conditions of the contract. The remaining 63% were to cancel contracts for technical reasons. For example, in years 2014 and 2015 a total of 973 non-compliant events occurred due to technical reasons. In these cases, FONAFIFO's monitoring and control department may decide that the contract is not feasible or it is not possible to make any improvements on the contract. In this case, the contract will be cancelled. For example in years 2014 and 2015 a total of 973 non-compliant events occurred due to technical reasons.

In cases where a contract with an individual farmer or indigenous community are reassigned to a new contract with another individual farmer or indigenous community, the new contracts will be awarded according to the similar criteria.

The program aims to reach as many communities and individuals as possible in each year of operation. This is why some grants are granted at a lower score to the score in the previous year, so that they target other conservation areas. According to FONAFIFO, during the period 2011-2015 between 40-55% of contracts were renewed.

## Activity 2.2: Expanding and Improving the Special Payment for Environmental Services in Indigenous territories

This activity will make payments for environmental services to indigenous communities according to the modalities of the special PES in indigenous territories. The modality operates in a way that is similar to the regular PES program, but with some differences such as the inclusion of an additional component related to the engagement process between indigenous peoples and FONAFIFO. The indigenous people's contact and key differentiators are:

According to the 2011 Census held by the National Institute of Statistics and Census (INEC) in Costa Rica, 104,143 individuals identify themselves as indigenous, representing approximately 5% of the total population where 69.8% are women and 50.3% men. Costa Rica has eight different ethnic groups: Cabécar, Bribrí, Brunca or Boruca, Garífuna, Miskito, Ngäbe, Quinn, and Talamanqueño. The indigenous territories in Costa Rica are divided into 24 indigenous reserves and 10 indigenous communities.

The indigenous population of Costa Rica is settled in "24 indigenous territories" with a total area of 33,447 hectares. These areas are managed by the Autonomous Indigenous Development Councils (CIDEI) or the Autonomous Government of the Territories are the Integral Development Associations (ADIN), created by the regulations of the municipal government. The ADIN and CIDEI have been established in order to provide a framework for those who have embraced ADIs as their governance structure, others keep their traditional structures of governance. It is noteworthy that 19 out of these 24 indigenous reserves are located in the southeast of Costa Rica (provinces of Cartago, Limón and Puntarenas).

## In control state

FONAFIFO uses a form with indicators designed for all included activities. These forms will be included as part of the project documentation to be submitted by FONAFIFO to the Green Climate Fund. FONAFIFO uses its GIS based Project database integrated in the FONAFIFO Information System (SIPS).

REDDO plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 10 OF 80)

It is important to mention that indigenous territories are the only community-owned private areas in Costa Rica. Moreover, according to national legislation, the ACE recognizes and guarantees the free, prior, and informed consent of representation of the indigenous community. The Indigenous Law recognizes the full legal capacity of indigenous communities as a collective entity and as individuals. In addition, it recognizes the reserves for the indigenous communities established by executive decrees.41

In addition to collective ownership, the legal framework guarantees the private ownership of the members of the indigenous communities in their ancestral lands. Article 70 of the Indigenous Law allows the transfer of parcels to indigenous families on a free and proprietary basis, in order to meet their needs.42 The National Commission for the Administration of Indigenous Reserves (CNAIR) is responsible for the administration of these minorities, stating State action in order to guarantee the indigenous individual and collective ownership of their lands.

As a signatory to all the major international conventions on Indigenous peoples including the ILO and the UN Declaration on Indigenous Peoples Rights, Costa Rica has legislation in place that generally recognizes their rights. The country has also developed a national policy for the promotion of the development of indigenous peoples, demonstrated by the regulation of the general mechanism for indigenous peoples consultation (Executive decree 4093/2017) and the creation of the National Commission for the Administration of Indigenous Reserves in a free, prior, and informed manner, through adequate procedures and representative institutions.

Since 1997, the above-mentioned Territorial Territories have voluntarily participated in the PES program research, which has allowed them to generate income from the sale of carbon credits and individual activities. To date, 284 contracts have been established between FONAFIFO and Indigenous peoples under the different modalities of reforestation, afforestation, agroforestry systems, soil conservation, land regeneration and 1,668,780 trees under the Agroforestry systems modality, representing an investment for the period 1997-2019 of approximately $ 59.06 million USD43.

## Source: National Census INEC 2011

In most cases these contracts have been established with the support of the ADI that serve as aggregating entities for several indigenous territories in each region facilitating communication and coordination process with indigenous peoples. The ADIs are also responsible for the implementation of PES agreements, which are signed by FONAFIFO and IPs representatives to reach PES Agreements. PES is one of the main sources of income that indigenous communities receive from their forest resources.

The process for Indigenous peoples to apply to the PES program is very similar to that of other forest owners (see description of activity 2.1). However, to ensure that participation is inclusive and respect indigenous people's worldview, FONAFIFO has developed a specific procedure for the implementation of the PES program with special provisions through executive decree No 3987-MINAE:

- In order to avoid conflicts in the demarcation cadaster map, the project can be processed using existing baseline information. FONAFIFO will use the original map of the reserve's constitution.
- The contract will be signed by the ADI and the IPs. The contract must be approved by the general associator assembly that authorizes the PES project's development. The Minutes must include the list of community members who voted in favor of the project, as well as the number of votes for each option. The PES project will be used by the ADI.
- In the case where the IPs are not present on the project area, traditional and subsistence agriculture activities are allowed, supervised by FONAFIFO Personnel. The contract will establish specific conditions required to execute these activities.

Without exception, in all cases, the documents and requirements to apply the PES Program must be submitted through the ADI. In addition, the ADIs have a formal agreement with FONAFIFO that obliges them to share information on their members' participation in the PES program. This agreement will establish obligations that allow the dissemination of all information on the functioning of the PES program.

The Indigenous Development Associations may present PES Projects with up to 100 hectares for the Forest Protection Program, and up to 250 hectares for reforestation or agroforestry systems per year. In reforestation projects a maximum of 300 hectares per year are allowed.

### REDD+ plus RSB FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) [PAGE 30 OF 80]

#### Activity 2.3: Forest fire prevention

Forest fires in Central America are a threat to forest loss, and one of the main drivers of deforestation in the Region. In Costa Rica forest fires mainly cause forest degradation, as they consume all the undervegetation leaving behind bare soil.

SINAC is responsible for managing the response to forest fires and leads National Commission on Forest Fires (CONIFOR acronym in Spanish). Forest Fire prevention measures as established in the National Strategy for Integrated Forest Fire Management 2012-2031 are implemented by SINAC. The strategy's main objective is to reduce fire incidence, minimize its impact on biodiversity and human communities rely on voluntary firefighters, such as Bomberos de Añasco, as a first line of defense against wildfires.

According to the National Strategy for integrated forest fire management 2012-2031: 99% of forest fires in CR are caused by human activities (voluntary or involuntary), evidencing social inequality, and access to land (where people have no legal title to their land). The strategy's main objective is to reduce fire incidence, minimize its impact on biodiversity and human communities rely on voluntary firefighters, such as Bomberos de Añasco, as a first line of defense against wildfires.

This activity has been prioritized as part of this proposal as the existing forest fire prevention program has demonstrated its effectiveness to both prevent and control forest fires. Enhancing the geographical coverage of fire prevention measures will further contribute to reducing fire incidence in CR.

To achieve the appropriate level of coordination within the national, regional and local action, the country has set up an organizational structure to address the problem of forest fires, allowing the simultaneous integration of different actors and strengthening the overall leadership of the Costa Rica State. It is in this context that the National Strategy for Integrated Forest Fire Management 2012-2031 was developed.

Costa Rica started working on fire management in 1997, through an official country guideline called the National Fire Management Plan. This plan has been updated and revised several times to include various activities that are carried out at national level in this matter. The strategy's main objective is to minimize the impact of fire by strengthening the capacity for prevention and control of forest fires, as well as the implementation of the National Fire Management Plan, in order to contribute to the conservation of the country’s biodiversity.

The national structure for its management, as established by the national strategy and which are fundamental parts in the development of this proposal, is composed of three main actors: the National Commission on Forest Fires (CONIFOR), responsible for the formulation, management, supervision, coordination and monitoring of institutions related to Fire Management in the country.

The Brigades against Forest Fires are made up of forest firefighters, which will be made up of public institutions, private companies, non-governmental organizations or voluntary people belonging to communities, and have been trained and trained for this purpose.

Through this activity the forest fire prevention program will be strengthened by implementing capacity building activities in the brigades against forest fires. This will include training on the use of equipment such as software, hardware and other equipment for monitoring of forest fires; equipment, materials and fuel for maintenance of brigades against forest fires; purchase of communication devices to improve protected areas communications, implementation of an early detection system for forest fires and design and implementation of a fire communication campaign.

#### Project Board (PB)
The Project Board (PB) is responsible for management decisions when guidance is required by the Project Management Unit (PMU). The PB will be responsible for approving project budgets, ensuring financial constraints are adhered to, and addressing any project level grievances. Project Board decisions should be made, by consensus, in accordance with standards that shall ensure best practice, development results, best value money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective international competition.

In case consensus cannot be reached within the Board, the UNDP Resident Representative (or their designates) will make a final decision if it cannot be found, will take the final decision to ensure project implementation is not unduly delayed.

Specific responsibilities of the Project Board include:
- Provide guidance on the direction of the project, ensuring it remains within any specified constraints;
- Address major issues as raised by the project manager and/or the management committee;
- Provide guidance on new project risks, and agree on possible countermeasures and management actions to mitigate them;
- Agree on project manager's tolerances as required, within the parameters set by UNDP-NICE; and
- Provide approval for any changes in scope or budget that are proposed when constraints are exceeded.
- Advise major and minor amendments to the project within the parameters set by UNDP-NICE in line with the GCF policy on restructuring and cancellation;
- Ensure compliance of all projects with the GCF-Awarded projects and programmes;
- Ensure coordination with various government agencies and their participation in project activities;
- Review and approve any changes in scope or budget that are proposed when constraints are exceeded to ensure that the agreed deliverables are produced satisfactorily according to plans;
- Approve the final implementation report, including the quality assessment rating report; make recommendations for the workplan.

Project Assumptions

UNDP provides a three-tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and regional offices, who are independent from the Project Management Unit by carrying out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role is to ensure that the Project Board has the necessary information on which to make decisions.

As the Project Board will be independent of the Project Management function; the Project Board cannot delegate any of its quality assurance responsibilities to the Project Management Unit. The Project Board will have access to all financial data provided by the GCF. As an Accredited Entity to the GCF, UNDP is required to deliver GCF-specific oversight and compliance functions in accordance with the GCF’s requirements for delivery of oversight, reporting (i) through the project completion, (ii) throughout the project reporting.

National Project Director (NPD)

The UNDP National Programme will act as a National Project Director (NPD) and will be responsible at the highest level for providing guidance on the management and technical feasibility of the project and ensuring its implementation. The NPD will also be responsible for coordinating with the GCF, the Technical Committee and advising the Project Manager on Government policy and priorities. The NPD will be supported by the Technical Committee (TC) in the performance of these responsibilities as well as in providing advice to the Project Board and processes. The NPD will set and approve procurement of services and goods corresponding to the project and will determine the terms and conditions for such procurements, including the payment schedule and modalities for payments. The Combined Delivery Report (CDR) will be applied on a quarterly basis and signed by the NPD.

Technical Committee:

The Technical Committee already established for the National REDD+ Strategy, will be expanded to serve as technical committee of the project consisting of high-level technical representatives from the following institutions: Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN), Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fishery (MAAF), Ministry of Health (MINSA), Ministry of Education (MINEDUC), National Forest Institute (INIFOR), the National Center for Environmental Information (CENIEGA) and The National System of Conservation Areas (SINAC). The Technical Committee will be chaired by the NPD. The TC will also include representatives from the Fundación para el Desarrollo Sostenible (FUNDDES), the Centro de Investigaciones y Servicios Ambientales (CISA), and the Fundación Clicco (in Spanish), to ensure coordination with the broader climate change related processes.

Meetings will be arranged when there is a need of technical inputs and coordination with the project’s components. The TC will provide guidance on the implementation of the project, as well as advice for the NPD to provide technical support to the Project Board. Project National Director, Project Technical Expert and Project Manager will attend all meetings of the TC.

The TC will also be responsible for ensuring that indigenous communities, local community and women groups, private sector and other partners will be invited to participate in an ongoing dialogue on project implementation and its results. The TC will provide guidance on the subnational REDD+ processes, as well as those supporting the National REDD+ Strategy, be invited to participate, to ensure adequate coordination as well as knowledge exchange on challenges and best practices.


The Project Management Unit (PMU)

The Project Management Unit (PMU), under supervision of UNDP will run on a day-to-day basis within the framework established by the Project Board. The PMU will be responsible for implementing the project.

The Project Manager function will end once the project is operationally closed, which is decided by the Project Board, and all commitment have been fulfilled such as completion and submission of the final report and project closure process and any other documentation required by the GCF and UNDP.

The Project Manager's prime responsibility is to ensure that the project meets its objectives in terms of quality, time, cost, and scope.

The Finance Unit, as part of its quality assurance role, provides the final approval. The Project Manager is also responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out within the approved budget and time frame.

The Assistants in the fields of administration, finance, logistics and procurement will report to the Project Manager and provide support in management and administration of the project, as well as any logistical support required by the Project Technical Experts.

The Project Technical Experts will produce in a timely fashion inputs for annual work plans and budgets of their components, to be consolidated by the Project Manager and then presented for approval by the Project Board; and an annual progress report on the implementation of the project. The PMU will monitor the progress made, any shortcomings and the necessary adjustments made to achieve project outcomes.

The PMU is designed to support Outputs 1 and 3, which use a conventional upfront financing modality (i.e.- cash advance) from UNDP to cover the costs associated with the implementation of the project. The PMU finances and implements activities using its own staff and processes; while (ii) UNEP as AE will transfer funds through the Project Board to the PMU for the implementation of Output 2 activities, including safeguarding, the design of the PMU does not need to take up Output 2 activities into account.

Upon request by MINAE, UNDP will provide technical backstopping during the implementation of the project. The final report on the project's results and lessons learned will be recovered according to UNDP’s policy.

**Responsible Parties**

For an entity to be engaged as a responsible party, a capacity assessment must be performed. Parties concerned with project formulation and design must review needed capacities. They first determine which tasks each party is able to perform and then identify the gaps in terms of skills and resources that need to be filled. The measures will be documented for follow-up action. This may be done, for example, through a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) or other agreement.

Additionally, parties must confirm that its partners are screened against UN Sanctions and Eligibility through a UN Security Council online system. Parties must also verify that any supplier has been involved in terrorism and corruption. The Bonn Office has a Due Diligence Policy on Suppliers. In addition, a Risk Assessment Tool is applied before any agreement is made. This tool includes the following exclusionary criteria:

- Arms/ammunition or their components, including military supplies.
- Explosives or explosive precursors.
- Tobacco or tobacco products.
- Weapons of UN sanctions, UN ineligibility lists or UNDP vendor sanctions list;
- Pornography.
- Substances subject to international bans or phase-outs, and wildlife or products regulated under the CITES.

*Performance-based payments (PBP)s are a type of agreement between UNDP and a Responsible Party to provide resources for agreed results. Payments are made only upon verified achievement of agreed results provided; rather payments are made only upon the verified achievement of agreed results. This approach gives greater flexibility in how resources are used to achieve result* (UNDP Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures - POPP, see Figure 7).

In this modality, as payments are made only on verified results, "the Responsible Party is fully responsible for achieving the agreed results. The UNDP will provide technical assistance to the Responsible Party in using resources within the parameters stipulated in the project document and performance-based payment agreement. The Responsible Party will be required to submit substantiative and other reporting required in the agreement to trigger payments" (UNDP POPP).

The UNDP Policy on the PBP modality is publicly available in full in UNDP's POPP™.

The overall process and requirements for this PBP modality are as follows:

1. Government defines project objectives, outputs, targets and prices per unit of result.
2. Government & UNDP agree on an independent assessor, who reviews elements from step 1 and defines a validation methodology;
3. A technical committee or project board reviews and approves elements defined in step 1 & 2;
4. A Project document is signed, as well as the Performance-based payment Agreement;
5. Disbursements are made to the Responsible Party upon verification of results, based on the achievement of one or more outcomes verified by the independent assessor (including safeguards).

### GCF RBP Project

Provide information on the non-carbon benefits associated with the implementation of REDD+ activities, highlighting their importance for the long-term success of REDD-plus activities and providing evidence to this regard.

Non-carbon benefits have been discussed with all stakeholders involved in the REDD+ process since its beginning. The GCF RBP project has identified three types of non-carbon benefits: social (carbon benefit), biodiversity conservation (non-carbon benefit) and land use change (carbon benefit). These non-carbon benefits were identified based on different strategic options identified for the GCF RBP project in the Project Proposal.

Given that the GCF RBP project is focused on increasing the number of beneficiaries and offered increased opportunities for indigenous peoples to participate in PES as well as strengthening other existing public policies that recognize the rights of indigenous communities, the social benefits are clearly defined by the current legal framework in Costa Rica and they comprise a wide range of activities such as education, health services, housing, employment, etc. and through the Payment for Environmental Services program.

The environmental services recognized by the Foresty Act include:

1. GHG mitigation and carbon storage (carbon benefit)
2. Soil erosion control (non-carbon benefit)
3. Biodiversity conservation (non-carbon benefit)
4. Land use change (carbon benefit)

A recent academic study[1] specifically quantified three environmental services recognized by Costa Rica's PES program: carbon storage, soil erosion control and habitat suitability for biodiversity as a cultural environmental service. The model used to quantify the carbon sequestration potential of the project area was the Universal Soil Loss Equation (RUSLE) to model soil erosion control and Maxent to model habitat suitability. The additional carbon sink capacity of the project area is estimated at 1,024 tons of CO2 per year. This would be found to store 235 tCO2 of carbon, for 148 Mt·yr-1 of soil erosion and contain 762 ha·yr-1 of suitable habitat for biodiversity. The project area is also part of the PES programs enrolled in both the National Forest Service and the Ministry of Environment and Energy.

Additionally, the social and environmental benefits derived from implementing the Payment for Environmental Services program are not limited to the carbon sequestration potential but include a wide range of the best recognized co-benefits related to the organizational capacity and improved participation due to the implementation of the program. The PES programs in Costa Rica have been designed as a mechanism that allows civil society organizations to actively participate in public policy. Additionally, they promote productive activities in the timber value chain, which are more sustainable than traditional practices such as slash-and-burn agriculture or regeneration, both with commercial and native species. In many cases, these programs are linked to communal conservation areas.

In the case of PES investments in indigenous territories, due to the communal nature of land tenure, the social and economic impact of non-carbon benefits is easily identifiable, since organized communities collectively decide on the use of the resources provided by the program. These decisions can include infrastructure improvements such as roads and bridges, etc. It is not the same case with private owners, who individually decide the use of the payments received.

[1] Herrera, L et al (2020) Spatial quantification to examine the effectiveness of payments for ecosystem services: A case study in Costa Rica. PGS 75th Scientific Assemblies, Ecological Indicators Volume 138

# Introduction

The evidence on the impact of the PSA Program on poverty is mixed. Several studies (Ohl et al., 2013; Mertens et al., 2015; Zondervan et al., 2016) have found that the PSA Program has had a positive effect in terms of increasing access to markets and services, leading to larger and relatively better-off farmers. Conversely, Muñoz (2014) finds that the PSA Program plays an important role in poverty reduction by targeting the poorest households and communities. To maximize their poverty impact by adding particularly disadvantaged districts to the priority areas for the PSA Program, it is recommended that focus on the participation of indigenous peoples in the program seeks to increase its impact on poverty alleviation.

Regarding gender inclusiveness as a non-carbon benefit, please refer to the gender action plan summarized in section D.15 for more information.


## D.13: The REDD-plus RSB model-based programme

### D.13.1: Description of potential

Describe the potential of the programme to contribute to the achievement of the Fund's objectives and results.

#### Identification of policies and measures to curb the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation

All the policies and measures of the National REDD-plus Strategy have been identified through studies and consultations during the readiness phase for their potential to address the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation as well as the barriers to forest carbon stock enhancement, conservation and sustainable forest management.

Deforestation and reforestation were assessed for 1987-2013 at the national and sub-national scale. This assessment was based on a combination of remote sensing data (satellite imagery) and ground truthing at the national level; the patterns of gross deforestation and gross reforestation were analyzed. Deforestation reflects current land use decisions-making by land owners, while reforestation results from longer-term land use planning considerations.

At the regional level, zones of homogeneous deforestation processes have been identified. The zones are distinctive land-use types that share similar characteristics in terms of drivers of deforestation and forest degradation. This stage was to conduct a Two-Step Cluster analysis according to 3 indicators: the intensity of deforestation during 2013; the rate of deforestation between 1987 and 2013, and the proportion of land area affected by deforestation in 2013.

In the second stage, the clusters were manually refined according to expert judgement. Local experts in five consultation workshops were involved.


#### Once the zones were finalized

National statistics on land use and agricultural productive systems were derived for them, based on the maps mentioned above. The statistics on population dynamics, employment and migration were also used as inputs into the analysis of drivers of deforestation and forest degradation. The net emissions and removals in forests remaining forests from forest degradation and forest carbon stock enhancement were estimated.

This analysis was the basis for the development of the National REDD+ Strategy which includes a series of policies and measures to address these drivers. A key measure which is supported by the current proposal is the use of payments in lieu of taxes (PILTs) as an instrument to reduce deforestation, forest degradation and land-use change. The strategy also proposes additional policies and measures to support forest governance and address deforestation and forest degradation at national level.

The REDD+ Strategy includes a series of policies and measures that aim to reduce the drivers of deforestation and impact of forest fires, solving land-tenure conflicts and development of new financing options for areas under social protection.


## Efficacy of the utilization of the proceeds of payments in consideration of drivers of deforestation

Analyses carried out during the readiness process point to the fact that the most important factors driving deforestation are related to the competitiveness of agricultural activities. These factors show that deforestation is mostly driven by a higher profitability from clearing forests for agriculture, which is more likely to be driven by a higher profitability than the one obtained by conserving forests (that includes values for ecosystem services and carbon sequestration). The analysis also shows that the most important factor driving forest degradation is related to the lack of tenure security. This is because it reduces the standing value of forests which in turn increases the forest's relative profitability when compared to alternative land uses thus altering the basic economic equation that determined land-use decisions.


---
**Page 53**

Furthermore, studies suggest that the PES has had an important indirect impact as it served as compensation for the prohibition of forested land uses changes greatly increasing the political acceptability and reducing the enforcement cost of this command and control measure**. This is a critical contribution of the PES which is often overlooked.

### Methodology to assess the impact potential in terms of Co2E

The UNFCCC Warsaw framework for REDD+ does not require, nor provides a methodology, for attribution of emission reductions or removals from forested land uses changes to specific policies and measures. Deforestation to a single policy or measure is flawed for multiple reasons. From a conceptual standpoint, there is no clear-cut distinction between deforestation and degradation. Furthermore, the UNFCCC Warsaw framework does not allow for attribution of emissions reductions or removals to individual policies and measures can pose risk of displacement. For example, even if scaled up, the PES programme would not be able to compensate for all forested land uses changes within Costa Rica that fall outside the areas covered by the program cancelling off the mitigation effects. From a technical standpoint it is extremely difficult to attribute emission reductions or removals from forested land use changes at national scale framework**. Furthermore, It is very challenging to achieve full consistency between GHG estimation approaches at national level and the REDD+ methodology for attribution of emission reductions/removals at project scale. For example, an emission factor based on national scale, may be applied at the local level but not necessarily reflect the actual emissions given that the statistical design to gather that data was designed to ensure significance at the scalescale.

Costa Rica deals with these conceptual and technical issues by implementing REDD+ plus on a national scale. However, Costa Rica has also developed a methodology for attribution of emission reductions/removals within the national territory and avoids consistency issues across scale by focusing on ensuring that REDD-plus results are consistent with the national framework and verified at the national level in line with the requirements set out in the Warsaw Framework.

The National REDD+ Strategy is a multi-sectoral initiative to achieve results within the national requirements Costa Rica has set for itself. It includes both public and private sector actors and resources of different nature to support its policies and measures. With multiple partners supporting multiple activities and due to the challenges mentioned above, it would be impossible to attribute emission reductions/removals from forested land uses changes to a single action/compromisor. Rather, each funding source will have contributed alongside many others. The implementation of REDD+ plus in Costa Rica has led to emission reductions/removals equivalent to 14,794 tCO2e per year during 2015-2014.

As per the UNFCCC Warsaw Framework for REDD+, the output of this project will be the national REDD+ Strategy. During the lifetime of the GEF project (2013-2024), it will be known first as the BIRD funds with the REDD+ technical annexes are submitted to the UNFCCC in 2015 and then as the National REDD+ Strategy. The outputs of this project will also be available on the GEF REDD+ Information Hub on the GEF Web-Platform, in accordance with UNFCCC decision SCP/19.

The extensive international scientific literature available on the impact of public policies of the Forestry Law which greatly supports the PES and other policies supported by the GEF HBP project are those that have proven to be successful over the last 25 years of implementation.

### Impact potential of PES

According to a recent academic study**. PES areas enrolled in the programme were found to store an additional 9 tCO2e/ha/year on average when compared to areas outside the programme. It is also important to note that


---
**Page 54**

Literature also suggests that areas which are committed long-term to the programme score a significantly larger benefit per hectare compared to uncommitted areas. Some studies have shown that forest cover declined the longer payments were in effect, disappearing almost entirely by the fifth year. In a review of several sub-national REDD+ projects, it was found that the most successful projects had a strong focus on securing land tenure for a long-term effect on forest expansion relative to a business-as-usual scenario. This highlights the importance of Costa Rica’s land tenure reform in funding its REDD+ project.

Taking the above point consideration, it is estimated that supporting over 260,000 ha over will lead to emission reductions of 8,560,078 t CO2 when compared to a BAU scenarios where these lands would lose 9t/ha or 330,000,000 t CO2.

Impact potential of preventing and fighting forest fires

Under current climate change scenarios, according to Costa Rica’s GHG inventory, in 2015 over 10,400 ha of forests were affected by forest fires representing 30g of NO₂ & CH₄ emissions and 2,857.165 ton of CO₂, as well as an additional 9t/ha of CO2 equivalent.

Benefits derived from the implementation of the National Strategy for Integrated Fire Management 2012-2021 are harder to estimate due to the absence of a “clear without project” scenario. Nonetheless, the lower forest fire incidence in Costa Rica compared with other countries facing similar climatic conditions is an indication that the strategy has been effective.

Indeed, although El Niño’s drought effect caused the largest number of forest fires in Costa Rica since 2000, rapid and affirmative action by the government and civil society organizations helped to contain them. The National Fire Information for Resource Management System or FISYS (which can be accessed [here]) shows Costa Rica’s reduced fire incidence relative to other countries facing similar climatic conditions.

The proposal is expected to directly benefit over 1000 private landowners of which at least 300 women. Most importantly the project will strive to benefit several indigenous communities with a total population of 154,143 inhabitants, equivalent to 2.4% of the country’s total population where 49.5% are women and 50.5% men.

# D.2 Paradigm shift potential

## Supporting the achievement of one of the World’s most ambitious NDCs to the Paris Climate Agreement

The ultimate objective of Costa Rica's National REDD+ Strategy is to support the national objective of achieving Carbon Neutrality as well as its treaty pre-2020 commitments and its NDC.

The success of the PES in Costa Rica has been a key factor in this achievement. The successful measurement of emissions and reportable results generated a paradigm shift by building confidence in UNFCCC processes by demonstrating that the implementation of REDD+ can be achieved at scale, and that the carbon credits generated from the implementation of the ICs/CDI can indeed be rewarded by international REDD+ plus result-based payments which have long been promised in the sector.

At a sub-national territorial level, Costa Rica’s early implementation of policies and measures to reduce deforestation has also directly and contributed to a paradigm shift of reducing deforestation. Further implementation of these policies and measures will continue to contribute to the achievement of the targets set out in the NDC, while enhancing community and biodiversity co-benefits and contributing to a post-COVID-19 green national recovery plan.

## Potential for scaling up and replication, knowledge and learning

Costa Rica’s progressive policies which led to the REDD+ results achieved in 2014 and 2015 and which will be further enhanced by the implementation of the NDC, are a model for other countries around the world.

The relevance and efficacy of the proposed use of PESs in addressing the drivers of deforestation is explained in section D.1. Noteworthy here, is the fact that the PES makes a meaningful contribution to the required international cooperation as it can be used by developing countries to meet their international obligations as it serves as compensation for the prohibition of forested land use change greatly increasing the political acceptability and feasibility of such prohibitions. The PES also provides an important mechanism for the contribution of the PES which is often overlooked, and which can serve as important lessons to other countries on the role that market-based mechanisms can play in addressing deforestation.

Costa Rica’s innovative policies and measures could be replicated in many other countries currently engaged in REDD+ around the world. However, developing country policy makers have yet to witness the operations of a mechanism such as the PES which has been designed specifically for this purpose and which is different from Costa Rica. Instead, to build confidence that UNFCCC REDD plus results can make a significant contribution to meeting international commitments, it will be necessary to demonstrate (1) compliance with all relevant requirements of the UNFCCC process in order to rapidly obtain and receive RVPs; and (2) for the international community to recognize these results as being credible. This will require both national and international implementation (including the Warsaw Framework for REDD-plus). Furthermore, Costa Rica will also set an example by demonstrating how PESs can be used to meet the requirements of the new market-based standards on non-market based solutions on international partnerships in line with the San Jose principles. This will include engaging with other countries that are implementing the Global Standard for Environmental Excellence Standards (TREES) from the Architecture for REDD+ Transactions (“ART”) initiative.

#### Direct beneficiaries and benefits of the project

The direct beneficiaries of the project are the indigenous peoples. ## Indirect benefits and contribution to the SDGs

The implementation of the National REDD-plus Strategy contributed and will continue to contribute to numerous Sustainable Development Goals beyond the obvious contribution to SDG 13: Climate Action.

By supporting PES in indigenous territories, the RBPs programme are an important contribution towards poverty reduction as they provide a sustainable income for communities living on their lands which is the poorest population in the country. The PES is the only cash transfer programme of the Costa Rican government that provides a direct and cost-effective way to provide basic needs such as food and shelter; (2) a means to recover and rebuild after the crisis, and (3) protection from future shocks.

D.A. Needs of the recipient:

Describe the vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population.
Costa Rica has an old democratic and pacific tradition, respectful of human rights. For instance, education was declared a fundamental right by law in 1948, and universal suffrage for women was granted in 1952. The Americans were enacted back in 1943 and the existence of a rule of law regime and democratic governments have produced a recognized political stability.

During the last decades, rural households improved their living conditions, thanks to the combination of economic growth and a higher social public investment. Revenues were increased in general, within a framework of liberty and equality. The country has been considered as a "middle income" country since 1980 according to UNDP's classification, of "high human development"; however, the country's challenge is to improve the quality of life for its population, due to the legacy of labor markets and environmental unsustainability within the context of a new development model.

According to the Ministry of Planning (MIDEPLAN) indigenous peoples of Costa Rica have the lowest development indicators in the country. The 2015 census indicates that the largest indigenous population is Bureas and Talamanca. The 2011 census includes disaggregated data on indigenous people, which shows that there are 349,876 indigenous people living in Costa Rica. This figure results in a total of 7% of indigenous households have at least one of their basic needs unfulfilled, while it is 24.5 % to the rest of the population.

Moreover 62.8% of indigenous households carry out at least one agricultural activity and only 39.3% of have access to electricity. The majority of these people live in rural areas and are considered as a vulnerable poverty population in the country. The latter, added to the fact that they depend on renewable natural resources (result of their traditional lifestyle) for their livelihoods and activities and livelihoods, places indigenous peoples in a position of vulnerability to climate change (LO 2017).

Between 2014 and 2015, the economy grew at a moderate pace, with acceleration and slowdown cycles. In a low inflation environment, the GDP growth rate was 3.8% for 2014 and 3.9% in 2015 (0.5%). The dynamism in the creation of informal jobs. Health, education and access to public services indexes continued to improve. The country has been a stable democracy since 1948. In terms of political stability, Costa Rica is one of the political arena, the country held free and clear democratic elections for a sixteenth term, the longest sequence of this kind in Latin America.

The country's economic outlook for the year 2018 of the Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC), indicates that Costa Rica has become one of the countries of the Central American region and the Caribbean with the highest growth rates. The country is also among those with the highest GDP of Central America.

Given this, the Government of the Republic made an important effort and on July 17th 2016 Law on Strengthening of Public Finances, which among other aspects includes:

- The change of the old and obsolete General Sales Tax to the Value Added Tax (VAT).
- Cash flow tax for companies that have a turnover greater than 5 million when the value of offering is allowed.
- The rates of the Income Tax and the Salary are modified.

The entry into force of this Law supposes a stop to the uncertainty that has accompanied the country in recent years.

Costa Rica has environmental strengths which are part of its image and historic evolution, and that have positioned it in the world as a responsible and innovative country in ecological issues. Conservation continues to be the main challenge for Costa Rica, which is reflected in the increase in protected areas, which has tripled changes, in four years, the marine area almost tripled. FONAFIP's PES programme is based on the polluter pays principle. The PES is mainly financed by 5.5% of the national fuel tax and from a fee for water use. As of 2013, the PES compensated environmental services in >100,000 ha of degraded forest land in Costa Rica, which has been identified as one of the most economically depressed rural areas. Funding the national PES programme is an absolute priority for Costa Rica and it is supported by the Government of Costa Rica, the World Bank, and other international partners.

In any case, with an ever-increasing demand, FONAFIP's budget, even prior to the COVID-19 crisis, accommodated only 50% of the unfilled demand. This is why this REDD+ RBP project intends to secure additional financial resources to strengthen the PES scheme.

Costa Rica has the potential to achieve even more ambitious PES goals by consolidating the program fully into the mainstream economy. However, the country's biggest challenge is to secure long-term financial sustainability to maintain the PES programme in the future. The current situation of high dependence on fuel revenue makes the program vulnerable to changing political and macroeconomic conditions as well as to fluctuations in international carbon prices.

To this end, Costa Rica is looking to use international REDD+ result-based payments to support the expansion of the PES scheme.

## Contribution of PES to the COVID recovery phase

The proposed use of proceeds is more relevant than ever given the ongoing recovery process from the covid-19 pandemic. The national PES will be an important mechanism to transfer real cash resources directly to indigenous communities in Costa Rica, which are currently the only beneficiaries of the only existing government cash transfer programme that directly targets indigenous people in Costa Rica.

In an emergency situation like the current one, cash transfers offer three different forms of relief: (1) a rapid and cost-effective response to immediate needs such as food and shelter; (2) a means to recover and rebuild after the crisis; and (3) protection from future shocks.

One key requirement for PES is that payments must be conditional upon environmental performance—that is, a unique opportunity to ensure the COVID-19 recovery process takes environmental concerns into account.

This project is fully aligned with Costa Rica’s National REDD+ Strategy. Its Carbon Neutrality goals are set out in this project, and it aligns with the commitments and this NDC and a suite of other national strategies.

The MINAE is the national environment authority in charge of designing environmental policies and coordinating strategies, projects and projects for the conservation of ecosystems and the sustainable use of natural resources. MINAE is also responsible for the administration of the National Fund for Forest Financing (FONAFIFO).

In 1995, the National Fund for Forest Financing (FONAFIFO) was created by the Forestry Law, with the purpose of promoting forest management and reforestation, and to improve the use and industrialization of Costa Rica’s forest resources. FONAFIFO is a public institution that finances projects in favor of the environment and the Environmental Services. It is governed by a Board of Directors that represent different stakeholders of the Forestry Sector.

In 2019 FONAFIFO managed an annual budget of $27,545 thousand (equivalent $36,270,728.86 at January 2020 exchange rate) (the GCF project will be on FONAFIFO’s endowment in payment for environmental services). The National REDD+ Secretariat is a public institution with REDD+ having managed the national REDD+ secretariat which oversee the REDD+ readiness process.

### D. Efficiency and effectiveness

Describe the economic, and / or apparent, financial outcomes of the programme.

There are two main sources of information on the impacts of Costa Rica’s payment for environmental services scheme, which can be drawn on to assess the expected efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project:

Costa Rica’s successful results in reducing deforestation and increasing forest cover since the 1980s, are explained by a combination of factors. These include: (i) the creation of protected areas; (ii) the agricultural frontier, coupled with incentive-based programs including the PES; and (iii) active support for environmental policies.

While studies on a national scale conclude that PES alone has had a low direct impact on deforestation rates and the forest cover of Costa Rica, sub-national studies provide evidence of additivity for PES-related deforestation reductions in specific areas.

In addition, studies show that land enrolled in the programme was found to store an additional 9 ton C/ha-on average when compared to areas outside the programme, hence the program has had documented impact in promoting carbon sequestration. The PES programme is also considered a cost-effective mechanism for climate action as it serves as compensation for the forested land uses change greatly increasing the political acceptance of the programme and its implementation.

Furthermore, the PES appears to have a better impact at a lower cost than the protected area network, the main alternative as a conservation tool. According to Sage (2000) and Harshman et al. (2005) the protection cost of the forested land uses change is 14 times more expensive in terms of GDP per hectare compared with PES and State protection through a national park (from 1.4 to 4.6 times less expensive depending on the methodology used).

Despite the substantial cash transfers to voluntary participants in this program, most studies do not document evidence of significant impacts on wealth or self-reported well-being. Some have even claimed that voluntary PES does not harm participants, but they beg the question of why landowners participate if they do not gain from it. The literature also suggests that many participants are motivated by a desire to protect their environmental assets and to secure their property rights and benefits. This is especially true for forest owners who see their forests as an asset similar to cattle or hired labor, since both decreased as a result of participation. Nor does the literature find evidence that participants have underestimated their costs of participation, and requests for participation keeping increasing beyond the initial period of the project. The literature also suggests that the willingness-to-participate is higher than the willingness-to-terminate, which is especially true in the case of forest owners who see their forests as an asset similar to cattle or hired labor.

## 1.2.3. For the use of this document

The following sections describe how environmental safeguards risks and impacts will be identified, screened, assessed and managed in a manner consistent with the GCF’s ESS standards, including the development of an appropriate monitoring framework to track project compliance against approved activities and the appropriate environmental and social assessment tools and management plans.

UNDP's Social and Environmental Standards (SES) were reviewed by the GCF accreditation panel and deemed sufficient for the purposes of this project. The UNDP SES is a set of standards that are applicable in all categories; however, for this project, the category for this project is Moderate. The ESFM provides an assessment of the social and environmental risks as well as the mitigation measures to be implemented under the Social and Environmental Screening Process (SESIP) and on the consultative process realized to date.

As a Moderate Risk Project, further impact assessment and management measures will be needed in order to manage these risks effectively. Based on the findings of the ESFM, further assessment and, where needed, elaborated management measures prior to project implementation are required. The following sections describe how environmental safeguards risks and impacts will be identified, screened, assessed and managed in a manner consistent with the GCF’s ESS standards.

The requirements of the UNDP SES may be incorporated into an updated ESMP and/or by elaborating as an activity-specific plan (if PES or IPES) PS modality policy and guidelines could be updated to incorporate key safeguards management measures.

To ensure full compliance of the rights of IPs and on UNDP’s Standard on indigenous peoples during the implementation of the project and the IPES specific PS modality, this ESFM recommends the development of a project-specific gender action plan (GAP) that will be integrated into the overall project management structure. The governance structure of each of the Indigenous communities that may participate in the project, including whether they are represented by an elected representative or through other means, should also be considered as part of the structures of governance. It shall provide inputs for strengthening the gender dimensions of the IPES Specific PS.

In order to promote and ensure the full and effective participation and support of stakeholders during the REDD+ readiness process and later on during the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy, the Government through FONAFIFO has established a series of stakeholder engagement platforms.

An extensive stakeholder engagement process was carried out in Costa Rica during this first REDD+ readiness phase (2011-2015), with funds from the FCFS and an investment of approximately US$840,000. Over 180 stakeholders were engaged through a series of workshops, roundtable discussions, focus group meetings, information & capacity building workshops, and analysis of proposals by the regional territorial groups (BTRs) according to the National REDD+ Strategy. The results of these consultations have been incorporated into the implementation plan; the RBPS project will support implementation of three of the main action lines of the strategy.

During the implementation phase of the project actions will be held to assist in the development and implementation of a stakeholder engagement platform for REDD+. This will include legal provisions for FCFS, of indigenous communities and other stakeholders. ## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 18 OF 20)

To enable operational, logistical, programmatic, technical and financial conditions for the design and implementation of the REDD+ Strategy.

The REDD+ Executive Committee was also created to ensure governance of the National REDD+ Strategy. Formed by an official member and a deputy for each one of the main stakeholder groups or Relevant Interested Parties (RIPs) as defined in Article 18, it is composed of representatives from the Government, Indigenous Academic sector and Civil Society. The role of this committee is to provide technical and political recommendations on the design and implementation phases of the National REDD+ Strategy, ensuring inter-institutionality within the REDD+ Strategy; the decree established that public institutions shall name focal points to address these issues. It also ensures coordination with other stakeholders in the government sector as well as where other stakeholders from the non-government sector that support the National REDD+ Strategy's design and implementation phases.

The above-mentioned arrangements were operational during the REDD+ Readiness phase and supported the design of the National REDD+ Strategy. The lessons learned from this process were considered in the elaboration of the new arrangements for the implementation phase.

### Governance during the implementation phase

Encouraged by the success of the governance during the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy, including the key institutional arrangements, Article 7 creates the Executive Secretary for the National REDD+ Strategy and its Secretariat.

The National REDD+ Secretariat is composed by two public servants from the National Protected Areas System (SNAC) and two from the National Forest Finance Fund (FONAFIFO), one of them is designated as its coordinator. The Secretariat will be responsible for: i) ensuring compliance with the National REDD+ Strategy; ii) ensure compliance social and environmental safeguards for the National REDD+ Strategy; iii) establish and manage a national registry of forest carbon projects; iv) prepare and present quarterly reports on progress of the National REDD+ Strategy (strategic plan); v) provide technical support to the REDD+ Steering Committee in its meetings; vi) serve as secretariat committee) vii) supervise financial resources administration processes from the National REDD+ Strategy; viii) participate in the design and implementation phases of the National REDD+ Strategy; ix) any other action required during the design, implementation or evaluation phases of the National REDD+ Strategy.

The REDD+ Executive Council is made up by the Director of the SNAC, the director of FONAFIFO, two VICE ministerial representatives (one from the council and oversight), one representative of the executive REDD+ secretariat, the regulation of Emission Reductions and to ensure compliance of the National REDD+ Strategy.

The main role of the REDD+ Steering Committee (established in Article 18) is to ensure compliance of the National REDD+ Strategy with the National Forest Code, the Law on Indigenous Peoples, two small forest producers (according to the National Forest Law), two representatives from NGOs from the indigenous sector and one representative each from the following sectors: a representative from those that have Forestry Science centers, one representative from the School of Agronomy Engineers and a Representative of the Ministry of Environment.

The committee will be coordinated by the REDD+ Secretariat providing necessary collaboration for its operation. Representatives will be chosen via independent townhall meetings that will be promoted, coordinated and supervised by the REDD+ Executive Committee. The first meeting was held on 20th July 2018 with a 30 days notice; end date will be advertised in the national and regional level media. Representatives are chosen by their respective organizations and must be present at all meetings of the committee, which started in January 2019 and since then has convened every 2 months, during the first meetings its own operations manual was agreed.

The REDD+ Steering Committee is responsible for ensuring compliance with the National REDD+ Strategy as per the law. The committee will be informed about any project that is being developed or implemented in accordance with the National REDD+ strategy as long as financing is available. May request information from the implementing entity that participate in the committee as considered necessary, including a request for grievance/complaint notes as relevant when relevant where there is non-compliance of the National REDD+ Strategy.

### National Consultation Plan for Indigenous Peoples

The national consultation plan for indigenous peoples developed at the national level was a result of the participatory process with indigenous communities at the local level. This process aimed to ensure that the voices and perspectives of indigenous peoples are heard through different organizational levels as described below and illustrated in Figure 10.

#### First Level: Formed by organizations at the local level (OTs) in Acornum or by the indigenous communities themselves, this level is responsible for identifying representatives from each BTR. The OTs confirm the Townhall for the BTR and each BTR designates a representative.
- **Second Level:** Composed of the Townhalls for the Atlantic, Central Pacific, and Central sector. This level has characteristics and geographic location. It defines four blocks for the following geographical areas: Atlantic, Central Pacific, and central sector. The role of the BTRs is to maintain coordination at the national and local levels.
- **Third Level:** Composed by two representatives from each BTR (48 leaders approximately).
- **Fourth Level:** Formed by one representative per BTR. Its role is to provide technical advice to all territorial blocks.
- **Fifth Level:** Composed of a National Coordinator, a national official and a deputy who will represent indigenous peoples in the National level discussions. National REDD+ Executive Committee (during the readiness phase) and receiving the REDD+ Steering Committee.

Regarding articulation with small farmers and rural communities, who represent an important area of Costa Rican families involved in agriculture and forestry or potential to develop forest-related activities, the REDD+ plus RFB funding proposal outlines several key points:

* The National Foresty Union (UNAFOR), a third level organization formed by five regional organizations and over 160 local organizations including producers, cooperatives, women's organizations, and indigenous peoples.
* Regional references for the small farmer sector and civil society: this consultative group elected by participating organizations in each region is composed of representatives from different regions in the country. The Forestry Chamber (CFN) represents small medium and large forest producers and from the forestry/forestry industry.
* Other groups, their members, and their representatives organized under different groups and organizations of potential beneficiaries on REDD+ or interested in REDD and that do not form part of UNAFOR.

To ensure that small farmers and rural communities were able to participate adequately in the readiness process and the designation of their representatives the Indigenous and Small Farmers coordinating association for community participation (ASOPRODECO) was created. This group is composed by representatives from specific groups of stakeholders. This work was carried out jointly with the National Foresty Union (UNAFOR) who have now responsibility to represent small farmers in the REDD+ process, as well as the Forestry Chamber (CFN), point to create UNAFOR in Costa Rica. During the implementation phase, articulation with this group of stakeholders will be maintained and strengthened through the participation of ASOPRODECO.

The CFN represents the forestry/forestry industry as well as small farmers, in terms of participating in decision making processes regarding REDD+ in Costa Rica they have two different options: on one hand CFN is responsible to represent the forestry/forestry industry in the REDD+ process and on the other hand it is the responsible government institution in charge of REDD+. On the other hand, are members of the REDD+ Steering Committee who will be part of the national coordination group for the implementation of REDD+.

As part of the National REDD+ Strategy and as a result of the ERM carried out in the context of the Carbon Fund ERPA project in Costa Rica, an Indigenous People's plan was developed that responds to all needs regarding their participation in the process. This plan is based on the results obtained from the indigenous peoples' heritage. The Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) aims to avoid potential adverse effects or risks on indigenous peoples and communities as a result of the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy, and where these cannot be avoided, reduced, mitigated or compensated for. In addition, provisions are included in the IPPF that allow for the participation of indigenous peoples in the process through free, prior and informed consent to obtain broad community support. The IPPF will be updated to ensure full implementation of the REDD+ Strategy.

Costa Rica is committed to implement IPPC as demonstrated by the regulation of the general mechanism for indigenous peoples consultation (Executive Decree 40352-MP-APRIL 2018) which regulates the obligation to consult with indigenous communities and other affected parties in cases where there are administrative measures, new legislation or private projects that may affect their rights. The decree also establishes a general application for public administration which sets out a series of general procedures for consultation, defines when the consultation is required and provides the necessary information to be provided by the consulted party.

The ERM carried out as part of the National REDD+ Strategy with IPs in the country were results included in the IPPF. The ERM was carried out through a participatory process that involved 10 IPs from different regions of the country, which allowed for the identification and analysis of risks and opportunities related to the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy. These provisions include the need to submit minutes and the internal agreement of each community to access the information provided by the ERM. The minutes will be signed by the representative of the IPs in Costa Rica and must be signed by the president of each community acting as the local government. Moreover, provisions allow for the use of the results from the project to be used for subsistence agriculture, reforestation or the sale of the outputs supported by the current project.

Under the scope of REDD+ actions, there have been multiple spaces for the indigenous sector to voluntarily participate in decision making processes and access information related to international & national safeguards provisions as well as with international agreements ratified by the country. # Grievance Redress Mechanism (MRIS)

In Costa Rica, general grievances about projects and programs implemented by the government, including the PES are processed and managed through the Office of the Comptroller (Contraloría in Spanish). Since 1997, and improved in 2005, this office has been responsible for managing complaints related to the implementation of their programs, including the Payment for Environmental Services Program (PES). Since 2013 all grievances are managed through the MRIS. The Office of the Comptroller is a public entity that reports directly to Congress and is subject to its control.

In Costa Rica, general grievances about projects and programs implemented by the government, including the PES are processed and managed through the Office of the Comptroller (Contraloría in Spanish). Since 1997, and improved in 2005, this office has been responsible for managing complaints related to the implementation of their programs, including the Payment for Environmental Services Program (PES). Since 2013 all grievances are managed through the MRIS. The Office of the Comptroller is a public entity that reports directly to Congress and is subject to its control.

The grievance mechanism (MRIS) aims to facilitate a communication channel between the Government, through the Comptroller of Services as a neutral entity and functionally independent of the entities in which they are housed, and the citizens who have grievances. The MRIS allows citizens to express their disagreements and generate contributions that give feedback to the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy and its Implementation Plan.

The MRIS is designed with the objective of ensuring that all groups are addressed and that the greatest possible inclusion is guaranteed.

In accordance with Executive Decree No. 404/2015, the Secretariat of the National REDD+ Strategy is integrated by SGRAC and FONAFIFO. However, the Comptroller of Services in FONAFIFO has generated a mechanism to receive complaints from citizens regarding the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy and the rational use of public resources. In the case of REDD+ actions that are not under the competence of FONAFIFO, citizens can submit their grievances through the MRIS or by contacting the corresponding Comptroller Services.

For the purposes of the MRIS, any social actor whether a natural, legal, state or private person, individual or community, national or foreign; or any that constitutes a Relevant Stakeholder in the National REDD+ Strategy (according to the criteria established for this purpose) can submit their grievances through the MRIS. Any interested stakeholder may require information, submit suggestions, grievances or claims on non-compliance in relation to the REDD+ Strategy and its Implementation Plan.

Costa Rica has a comprehensive anti-corruption and money laundering legal framework. The Ombudsman's Office created the Inter-Institutional Transparency Network to facilitate the access to information related to the administration of public resources through its publication on the Internet. The network was created in 2013 with the purpose of providing citizens with easy access to information, relating to the correct administration of public resources and to prevent acts of corruption throughout Costa Rica. The National Anti-Corruption System (SNC) is a set of laws that regulate the prevention of corruption interest such as: budgets, income, expenses, investments, payment, tenders, contracts, purchases, suppliers, operations, etc., and the SNC also includes the Code of Conduct for Public Officials, which establishes principles that support the network are justice, equity, legality, accountability, citizen participation and transparency.

Transparency and disclosure are required by Costa Rica's national legislation for all government implemented programs and projects. In line with this law, FONAFICADO has in its website up to date statistics (including the years 2013-2015) of the PES contracts that have been implemented in the country. The National Strategy for the Implementation of PES contracts for the different modalities of PES (hydrological resources, conservation, biodiversity, agroforestry and forest) includes information on the total number of hectares, financial expenditures, etc., on the program.

General provisions to projects and programs implemented by the Ombudsman's Office include that the FONAFICADO is responsible for the implementation of PES contracts. The National Strategy for the Implementation of PES contracts was in place during the result period and continues to be in place to date.

**UNDP**, as accredited entity will support the implementation of this project following its program and operations policies and procedures which include provisions for procurement, financial management, and reporting.

The project's specific environmental and social risks described in detail in the SEIS (included as Annex 1 of the ESIP).

Use of proceeds from this project will be directed to contribute and enhance the implementation of Costa Rica’s National REDD+ Strategy. In particular: POLICY 2: Strengthen the existing programs to prevent and control land-use change, deforestation, forest degradation, and forest carbon stock loss; POLICY 3: Promote sustainable forest management and conservation; and POLICY 5: Promoting the participation of indigenous peoples.

Overall, Costa Rica’s National REDD+ Strategy has a clearly defined mitigation focus as it aims to address drivers of deforestation and forest degradation. The project will contribute to this goal by supporting the implementation of policies and enforcement of forest carbon stock loss. Hence, its implementation is clearly aligned with the QCF’s mitigation objective.

The project will be implemented by UNDP as accredited and executing entity, and FONAFIFO as responsible party and it has the full policy and regulatory support from the Government of Costa Rica. Costa Rica's National REDD+ Strategy is supported by the Ministry of Environment and Energy and the Ministry of Public Works and Transport and Energy and supported by other government institutions within it in particular the National Protected Area System, the Climate Change Fund, the Mesoamerican Biological Corridor, etc., as well as by national and international stakeholders.

UNDP as accredited entity has demonstrated technical and institutional capability to implement this project. UNDP has implemented Costa Rica’s forest project portfolio that has exceeded around US$10 million between 2015 to 2019.

The country office has specialized technical personnel on climate change and forests including a national program officer, a regional program officer, and a technical advisor with extensive experience in the field of REDD+.

In terms of monitoring and evaluation, UNDP applies UNDD’s guidance for monitoring and evaluation outlined in the SBM Handbook, which is oriented to results-based management (See detailed description of M&E procedures in Annex 2). The project will be monitored against the following criteria: avoid deforestation and forest degradation; avoid environmental impacts; avoid impacts to people and the environment; minimize, mitigate, and manage adverse impacts; ensure that all stakeholders are involved in decision-making processes; ensure that the project addresses the environmental risk; and ensure full and effective stakeholder engagement, including through a mechanism to respond to concerns and complaints. UNDP will also apply the criteria for monitoring and evaluation for all UNDP projects under eligibility criteria and as published in two UNDP platforms: CARES and IATI.

The National REDD+ Strategy was developed following a participatory process including extensive consultations described above, with representatives of the Government of Costa Rica, civil society organizations, Indigenous Peoples, two small forest producers (according to the National Forestry Forests), two representatives from NGOs from the region, one representative from the Ministry of Environment and Energy, three representatives that have Forestry Science careers, one representative from the School of Agronomy Engineering and a representative from the University of Costa Rica. The Strategy was approved by the Government in 2015 for its implementation and the use of proceeds. This will guarantee an oversight mechanism in place from different stakeholders.

- Following the presidential elections, the next presidential elections are in 2022 and the next elections will be in 2027 when the project will be in full implementation. - Costa Rica was categorized as one of the countries of the Central American region with one of the highest deforestation rates. The government has been implementing a series of reforms since 2019 under the Law on Strengthening of Public Finances. The entry into force of this law expresses the commitment to reduce public spending and increase efficiency in the use of resources. These reforms include: The change of the old and obsolete General Sales Tax for the Value Added Tax (VAT) and the VAT can be applied to a good or service when the value of equity is altered.
- The current situation of the COVID-19 pandemic is currently the risk with higher probability of occurrence and highest impact of this project. While some impacts have been occurring during 2020, uncertainty of the circumstances in the future makes it a challenge for planning the activities and identifying and putting in place mitigation measures.
