## E.3 Gender considerations

### E.3.1 For the period of the achieved results

Provide a summary of relevant information in the assessment describing the extent to which the measures undertaken complied with the GCP gender policy.

In 1990, Costa Rica approved the Law for the Promotion of Women's Social Equality, whose article 7 states that "It is the responsibility of the State to guarantee equal rights between men and women in the political, economic, social and cultural fields". Likewise, the National Policy for Gender Equality and Equity (PEG) 2015-2030, which sets out the objectives and strategies to be implemented by the Government to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). One of its axes of action promotes the distribution of wealth, which recognizes that actions must be generated in favor of women. The PES program is a mechanism for generating income through the governance of tenure, because land, fisheries and forests are essential for realization of human rights, food security, environmental sustainability, biodiversity, housing quality, rural development and social and economic growth.

To enhance women's participation in the implementation of the ENSRED+, FONAFIFO establishes as its local Funds for the Promotion of Women (FOPM), which allows women to participate in the conservation activities they promote in their productive spaces of agroforestry or silvopastoral systems (PES SAF). The implementation of these programs is carried out by women, who are responsible for the design and execution of the projects that these women can be involved in new initiatives that have the potential to increase forest cover and reduce the degradation of natural resources.

Access to the traditional PES scheme in Costa Rica is granted based on land-tenure rights. Given that 84.3% of land is owned by men, only 15% of farms are owned by women, and most of them are small farmers (under 10 ha). Women's participation in the PES program has been low because they have not had access to land or credit for conservation activities. This situation has led to gender discrimination against women, especially regarding participation in design and implementation or access to opportunities generated through the PES program. The lack of women's participation is reflected in the distribution of benefits, negatively affecting women. Recognizing these realities, the PES programme since 2015 included a specific measure for women's empowerment, which increased their participation from 3% in 2017 to 49% compared to 1997.

From 1997 to 1997 15.1% of PES contracts were signed with women owners. This equates to a total of 2,502 women owners and 38,612 hectares under contract. Of the 16,712 contracts signed in the Program between 1997 and 2017. The number of women owners with PES contracts increased considerably between 2004 and 2013 (Figure 1). This increase corresponds to a 58% increase in the number of women participating in the PES program, which is partly due to the efforts made by FONAFIFO to increase the number of women owners receiving PES. However, as of 2014, the data does not allow us to determine who receives the PES payment; therefore, it is not possible to determine who receives the PES payment; therefore, there is a decrease in the contracts signed with both men and women.

Provide adequate and sufficient information on how the AE will undertake activity-level gender assessments and participatory action plans.

UNDP is committed to reducing gender inequalities in access to and control over resources and within the benefits of development. Thus, it will ensure this project will not discriminate against women or girls or reinforce gender-based discrimination and inequality. UNDP will also ensure that women have equal opportunities for participation meaningfully and equitably, have equitable access to resources, and receive comparable social and economic benefits from the project.

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a key component of the REDD+ Strategy. It provides guidance on how to integrate gender equality into all components; UNDP ensures inclusive and participatory activity-level gender assessments and action plans are undertaken.

Costa Rica has developed a comprehensive gender assessment and action plan that covers the whole National REDD+ Strategy, including the activities to be supported by this funding proposal: The Gender Action Plan (GAP). Among other things, it includes a review of the institutional framework related to gender equality; an analysis of the current situation regarding women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels and in all sectors; and a review of the existing policies and programs that promote gender equality. At the same time, the country has a specific and robust regulatory framework to promote gender equality: it is a signatory and has ratified several international conventions on gender equality (CEDAW, CEDIP, ILO Convention 105, etc.). The National Institute of Women (INAMU) has had a major impact on environmental, forest and climate change policies which in the last decade have been increasingly focused on gender equality. In this context, the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINAE), the Secretariat, made up of FONAFIFO and SINAC, has been preparing a Gender and REDD+ Road Map since 2013. The National Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation in Developing Countries (REDD+) in collaboration with gender experts, State institutions, civil society organizations, and diverse private sector actors have developed the Gender Action Plan (GAP).

The GAP report summarizes the processes for developing the GAP, the results found, and the proposed actions to address gender gaps and enhance gender-differentiated opportunities by implementing the National REDD+ Strategy. The document includes a review of the institutional framework related to gender equality; an analysis of the current situation regarding women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels and in all sectors; and a review of the existing policies and programs that promote gender equality.

The Gender Analysis (aka Gender Assessment) allowed for a better understanding of the context, including the main challenges and opportunities. ## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 12 OF 80)

### Procedures

- Gender-specific contributions and knowledge related to forest resources are underrepresented in policy formulation and management.
- Women find it more difficult to participate in forestry activities and decision-making processes related to forests.
- Gender stereotypes limit women's participation in forestry activities and decision-making.
- Fewer women participate in decision-making processes related to land use, including the development of agroforestry systems.
- Women producers have less access to information and their farms are smaller than those of men.
- There is a lower percentage of professional women doing technical work in forest-related areas.

### Distribution

- Women who live closer to forests show higher poverty rates.
- The farms of women producers receive less financial support from the government compared with those of men. This trend has been decreasing in recent years.

At the same time, there is great potential to increase the participation of women from different regions of the country in technical and decision-making processes related to forest resources. Women are increasingly involved in a wide range of activities aligned with the National REDD+ Strategy; in Costa Rica there are about 12,389 women producers who own 106,563 ha hectares of land used for agriculture, livestock production, and agroforestry systems. These women have an important role to play in natural persons in the country. Prioritized activities include reforestation, ecotourism, cocoa cultivation, plans (agriculture, livestock), and the development of agroforestry systems. The National REDD+ Strategy also includes actions for construction) and the development of agroforestry systems. Most of these activities can be carried out close to women's homes.

The activities included in the CAP generate significant rural development opportunities that generate resources for women, reduce poverty, and contribute to reducing deforestation, forest degradation and increasing carbon stocks. The gender analysis found that many of the areas with a high percentage of women producers are also those where there is a greater need for reforestation, agroforestry systems, as well as priority areas for forest conservation and management, for forest landscape and ecosystem restoration, and for the development of agroforestry systems. The CAP includes actions that support these activities prioritized by women during the development of the GAP coincides with or can be strengthened with activities included in the Territorial Rural Development Plans of the country's rural areas.

The GAP is aligned with the National Action Plan (PANAM) of the National REDD+ Strategy and is composed of 67 gender objectives (one for each PAM) and 20 expected results, together with the design of a Gender Action Plan (GAP). The implementation of these actions will be carried out in three phases. The GAP proposes a range of actions that encompass: (a) policy changes at the national level; (b) institutional strengthening to ensure gender equality in forest management and other related areas; and (c) capacity building for women. Through these actions, it will be possible to address priority gender considerations in the forest sector and establish strategies that are more inclusive of women's needs.

The design of the GAP was based on a bottom-up participatory approach with the objective of allowing a process to propose concrete actions that reflect the reality of the country and to validate the ideas and contributions of women, as well as to identify gender gaps in the forest sector. The process included a series of meetings with stakeholders publicly consulted, turning it into a proposal for concrete social and environmental transformation based on the needs identified by women producers. This process also allowed the identification of areas where there is a need for management of Costa Rican forests. In addition, the REDD+ Secretariat has achieved an important achievement in the design of this GAP: the inclusion of gender considerations as part of the National Strategy for Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC). Through the development of this GAP, Costa Rica becomes one of the few countries that have developed a Gender Action Plan for its REDD+ Strategy.

This GAP researches Costa Rica's commitment to human rights and gender and makes a clear path for continuing work on gender and the environment in the country. The National REDD+ Strategy GAP is the country's first gender action plan, which will be implemented through the NDC process.

### E.4. Interim report on prohibited practices

#### E.4.1. For the period of the achieved results

Provide evidence that Costa Rica has taken steps to demonstrate that no Prohibited Practices occurred during the implementation of the activities that lead to the REDD-plus results, such as: unaddressed Prohibited Practices; inclusion of areas with high levels of biodiversity or cultural heritage in the project area; and/or failure to follow the based actions; and double payment or financing for the same results achieved.

The National Forest Financing Fund (FONAFIFO), created by Act No. 7575 in 1996, is the governmental institution responsible for the administration of the Fondo de Financiamiento para la Conservación del Bosque de Costa Rica. The general objective of FONAFIFO is to finance small and medium producers for conducting reforestation activities, afforestation, forest conservation/management forest restoration, and establishing sustainable systems.

FONAFIFO also has the responsibility to raise funds for financing the payment of government services provided by forestry operations and contributions from the private sector to support the development of natural resources sectors. These services are defined in the Forest Act. The PES is mainly financed by 3.5% of the national fuel tax and 0.25% of the income tax, which are collected by the National Tax Service (SAT). FONAFIFO finances 14% of forest (120,000 hectares in indigenous territories), investing more than $400,000 in economically depressed areas.

The PES was expanded thanks to two loans from World Bank known as Economides I y II as well as some support from German Development Bank (KfW). In this context FONAFIFO has undergone numerous financial audits and evaluations by the World Bank, the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB), the European Investment Bank (EIB) and the World Bank. These audits have all concluded that FONAFIFO's internal control mechanisms were adequate to ensure compliance with the terms of the loans.

Through the Ecomercados project, Costa Rica also gained significant experience in complying with the World Bank's Environmental and Social Framework (ESF). The ESF is a set of policies and procedures designed as an important step to define a management framework to follow-up REDD+ safeguards under the UNFCCC as well as in the context of this project.

Additionally, Costa Rica has implemented Anti Money Laundering (AML) regulatory regime in place since the adoption of No. 178 of 30 April 1998 on narcotics, psychotropic substances, drugs of unauthorised use and related offences. The Law No. 2456 of 2000 on money laundering and financing terrorism was amended to modify Law 738 which is referred to as the "Drug, Money Laundering and Financing of Terrorism Law" (Law 198). This law was further modified by Law 2456 in order to include the term "terrorist acts" ("delitos de capitalización en actividades terroristas"). The modifications to the law further expanded the circle of those individuals who are subject to money laundering.

Costa Rica has also implemented the UNODC's (United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime) list of countries that have been identified as having significant anti-money laundering (AML) deficiencies. The last Mutual Evaluation Report follow-up relating to the implementation of AML measures in Costa Rica was published by the UNODC in 2018, which concluded that Costa Rica was compliant for 9 out of 13 criteria and was deemed Highly Effective for 0 out of 5 Substantially Effective for 1 of the Effectiveness & Technical Compliance ratings.

## F. Legal arrangements

### REDD+ results

Provide an analysis with respect to legal title to REDD+ results in the country. This should include an explanation of how these results are accounted for and reported under the UNFCCC.

According to the terms of reference of the REDD+ RBP pilot programme, there is no transfer of ownership to the GCF of the emission reductions paid for by the Fund. Payments will be recorded in the UNFCC web portal (List Information System) as financial flows, and corresponding results will not be eligible for REDD+ under the GCF or in any other arrangement.

Costa Rica can consider, at its own discretion, to use the emission reductions towards the achievement of its NDCs. However, it should be noted that these emissions reductions cannot be transferred and/or used for any other purposes (e.g. offsetting).

There is currently not one internationally recognized legal definition of carbon credits or emissions reductions. REDD+ results are considered science-inventorship-approving credit, which means they are issued for reductions of GHG emissions or enhancement of forest carbon stocks against a historical or projected reference level. Costa Rica has no legislation that defines the concept of carbon credits or emissions reductions and the effect of refraining from an activity (i.e. it cannot be the result of a purely natural phenomenon) – either as a result of these activities, or as a result of other actions taken by the country in its territory, such as the purchase of licenses, etc. In Costa Rica, the reduction of deforestation and the enhancement of the forest cover since the 1980s has been regulated through different laws and decrees, including Law No. 7543 of 26 May 1986, as well as the National Strategy for Fire Management Executive Decree 2639/1997 later on replaced by Decree 10-2008, and the positive effects of reforestation including the purchase of environmental services (PES) scheme.

The right to receive negative emissions payments derives, in this case, from the Constitution, the General Environmental Law No. 7543/1986 and the Forest Law No. 250/1992, which establish that Costa Rica has the mandate to manage its forests. The General Environmental Law No. 48 establishes the "obligation of the State to protect and preserve the environment" as a fundamental principle of public policy, with priority function of the Costa Rican State "to ensure the conservation, protection and administration of natural forests". Article 10 of the Forest Law No. 250/1992 states that the forest is a common property in case of Costa Rica; these functions are under the mandate of the Ministry of the Environment and Energy.

The Government of Costa Rica, through the General Law No. 7543/1992, article 2, lists the functions among the Ministry of the Environment and Energy to "promote the conservation and rational use of natural resources with the objective to promote sustainable development", which includes the protection and management of forests. Article 8 of the same law establishes that the Ministry attributes to the Ministry of the Environment and Energy, the responsibility for issuing environmental policies and regulations related to the forest sector in order to ensure compliance with the objectives and compliance with the objects and goals proposed in the ministerial programs and the National Development Plan.

The Ministry of the Environment and Energy encompasses forests in areas both in public and private properties. Article 19 of the Forest Law No. 250/1992, which states that "the use of land in private property will not be allowed to establish forest plantations", nor to establish forest plantations by the owner of Costa Rican rivers have no impact an effect that may lead to a loss or decrease of these ecosystems. Article 34 of the same law establishes that the Forest Law No. 250/1992 does not derogate from the existing provisions the principle of forest intractability and ordered the restoration of the affected forest areas, according to the principles of environmental protection, conservation and balance settlement (Tribunal de Casación Penal, sentences numbers 366-2003, 398-2014 y 450-2015).

The government of Costa Rica therefore, through the Ministry of the Environment and Energy, has the legal authority to issue regulations that allow for the payment of REDD+ results in relation to the UNFCCC results offered by the Government of Costa Rica to the GCF for payments.

REDD+ results-based payments as reviewed by the GCF reflect the agreement reached between the Parties to the UNFCCC in the context of the Warsaw Framework for REDD+, which foresees the provision of financial climate change payments in return for measured CERD reductions and reforestation. No other entity in Costa Rica is


---
**Page 79**
---

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) (PAGE 18 OF 80)

part of that agreement or to pass a claim to the GHG reductions and removals pledged to the GCF. This is different from other carbon markets, such as voluntary carbon market over which the Government has no control, oversight, and which create and certify units that are defined under private standards. This is also different from credits generated under carbon offset programs of other countries or subnational programs. The REDD+ RBP project will generate GHG reductions and removals as defined by the GCF but not interfere with other national or subnational programs, payments, or projects. The REDD+ RBP project will be implemented in collaboration with the Government of Costa Rica and the Government of Costa Rica Such efforts and successes and results-based payments under the Warsaw Framework for REDD+ will meet the criteria of measurable carbon benefit.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP144

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Project background

The project presents Costa Rica's REDD+ results for the period 2014–2015, calculating a total emission reduction of 1.4 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO₂eq) in emission reductions (ERs) from reducing emissions from deforestation have been offered to the GCF. The reported ERs have undergone technical assessment.

- The project is fully compliant with the requirements for results-based payment (RBP) under the REDD+ RBP pilot programme and are fully compliant with the eligibility criteria set out in the terms of reference (TOR) for the pilot programme (decision 18/03/17). The presented results, which cover the period from January to December 2015, have been assessed against the TOR and all the TOIRs have been analysed based on the scorecard and are presented in the annexes to this assessment.

- Costa Rica will use the proceeds from the RBP to invest in the implementation of its Forestry Law, to be complemented by the country's longstanding Payments for Environmental Services (PES) programme. The project is also designed with a strong focus on ensuring the participation of relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples. The proposed use of proceeds is consistent with Costa Rica’s National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) and carbon neutrality goals in its 2020 voluntary commitments.

- Output 1: Enabling conditions are in place for effective REDD+ plus implementation; and
- Output 2: Payment for environmental services (PES) and fighting forest fires.

#### Strengths and points of caution

6. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks.

**Strengths**

- Costa Rica's initiatives operating during the result-based payments period are consistent with the GCF's vision of forest programming globally.
- Costa Rica's Forestry Law has been in implementation for almost 20 years and provides both carbon and non-carbon benefits.

**Points of clarification**

- Especially with COVID-19 recovery, it is essential to have the strategic use of funds for the most vulnerable groups in society, which have been hit hard by the pandemic, in addition to the usual vulnerabilities.
- The proposed use of proceeds aims to improve and scale up efforts in Costa Rica's forest policy and programmes especially the Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) national programme. With more than 20 years of implementation,


---
**Page 90**
---

programme seeks to use the proceeds to include indigenous people’s groups in the PES programme and further expand the scope of the programme.

The Board wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XIII (titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations").

## Assessment performance against investment criteria

### 2.1 Impact potential Scale: High

* Costa Rica has a long history of conserving and sustainably managing its forest resources.
* Through flagship programmes such as the PES programme, Costa Rica has managed to protect a large part of its forest areas and restore degraded areas.

In parallel to the many sustainable interventions applied in forests, Costa Rica has developed several REDD+ strategies over the years under the umbrella of their national REDD-plus strategy. These efforts, similar interventions and other supportive measures have resulted in the establishment of 663,445 ha (257,019 acres) of forest areas that are now equivalent to 663,445 tCO2e has been established to address risk of reversals.

The proposed use of proceeds intends to further support the main interventions applied during the period 2014-2015. These include: the implementation and continuation of safeguard provisions; improved monitoring of REDD-plus related efforts; the expansion of the PES programme in key forest biodiversity hotspots areas of the country.

The project will allow for especially poor and vulnerable communities to manage their natural resources more sustainably and will enable them and other participants in the PES programme to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation.

### 2.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

* Costa Rica is a leading country, the National REDD+ Strategy provides sustainable governance for forest management.
* Many of Costa Rica’s policies and efforts have resulted in the REDD-plus achievements in 2014-2015.

The use of the proceeds from Costa Rica's national PES efforts will help to expand and further transform this national pillar of success. The proceeds will support the Government of Costa Rica in its commitment to provide greater benefits for indigenous communities, in particular indigenous peoples in key forest and biodiversity hotspot areas of the country.

The use of proceeds will allow vulnerable and poor communities in key biodiversity hotspots in Costa Rica to participate in the PES programme and improve their management of the natural resources on which they are highly dependent.

### 2.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High


---
**Page 91**
---

19. REED-plus investments and initiatives in Costa Rica have contributed to the sustainable management of forests and the promotion of non-carbon benefits, which are aligned with Sustainable Development Goals 1, 5 and 15.
20. Through the use of proceeds, the provisioning and supporting of ecosystem services provide co-benefits for the environment and target beneficiaries and, in particular, indigenous peoples and poor communities. The economic, social and environmental co-benefits are sustainable and contribute to poverty reduction, food security, income generation and diversification of local livelihoods, and the positive impact on the management of natural resources.
21. The use of proceeds to expand and help transform the national PES programme is very innovative and will help secure full focus on poor and vulnerable communities and their opportunities for sustainable and long-term gain from their land-use practices, in particular for indigenous peoples.

### 2.4 Needs of the recipient
Scale: High

18. Costa Rica has an established structure to manage and finance REED-plus efforts. With this proposal, there is potential to support even more ambitious goals and underserved populations. With the PES programme as the local instrument, local communities, and especially indigenous peoples will be supported in their efforts to improve their livelihoods through investments via the REDD proceeds.

### 2.5 Country ownership
Scale: High

19. The proposed use of proceeds is fully aligned with national policies, strategies and NDC priorities, and it is consistent with ongoing GCF investments in Costa Rica.
20. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has a strong track record in REDD-plus projects in Costa Rica, including the successful implementation of forest health and biodiversity issues in Costa Rica as both an accredited entity (AE) and an executing entity (EE).
21. For the last 20 years, Costa Rica has been leading in forest conservation efforts and has continued to be a leader in this area with the creation of the National Forest Service (SINAFOR) [1996] and other related policies (i.e., the PES programme and the REDD+ Emission Reduction Programme). Since 2015, Costa Rica has been committed to the sustainable management of forests. This is in line with the use of proceeds and the country's objective to have a carbon-neutral economy by 2021.

### 2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
Scale: High

23. The REED-plus results for 2014-2015 presented to GCF and the suggested budget clearly highlight that a fixed price of USD 5 per tCO2eq will be applied to the project, which is


---
**Page 92**
---

cost-effectiveness and in accordance with the TDR of the REDD plus programme. The proposed use of proceeds, as discussed in the previous sections, is in line with the GCF Investment Criteria.

24. Costa Rica's efforts in the preservation of forests has resulted in demonstrable impacts on forest conservation. Non-carbon benefits also include the buy-in of participants in the PES program and the creation of a new social safety net for indigenous communities. In addition to REDD plus recovery, the use of proceeds highlights the potential for a more effective response for indigenous communities by providing them with greater control over their own natural resource system to distribute resources, contributing to resilience for undererved communities.

### III. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 3.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project requests RBP for Costa Rica's REDD plus Program. The payment claims will cover Emission Reductions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (ERDD) program, amounting to a total volume of 14,079,777 tCO2e. The proceeds of the RBP will be reinvested in the PES programme to continue to implement and to further strengthen the programme.

- **Costa Rica's REDD plus Programme**: This programme provides incentives to small and medium-sized producers and landowners to conserve, protect and better manage their forests. It is based on three main components: (i) a payment for ecosystem services scheme; (ii) forest conservation contracts, which support forest conservation in primary and secondary forest areas and constitute 10 per cent of the contracts under the programme in the period; (iii) sustainable land management activities, which include reforestation and agroforestry. These are carried out by landowners who commit to maintain the forest for 35 years and continue 9 per cent of the total contracted area as a buffer zone. The remaining 81 per cent is used to implement activities to reforest degraded and abandoned agricultural lands and maintain them for up to 25 years; and (iv) sustainable land management, reforestation or agroforestry area in exchange for annual incentive payments.

The activities to be funded by the proceeds will have three component outputs. Under Output 1: Strengthening of the PES programme, there are two activities: Activity 1.2 – implementation of safeguards provisions under REDD plus and Activity 1.2 – monitoring and reporting on the environmental and social impacts of the project. Under Output 2: Strengthening of the ESMF, there will be three activities: Activity 2.1 – strengthening the PES programme; Activity 2.2 – special PES in previous territories and Activity 2.3 – forest fire prevention output 3 is a project management.

Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instruments. The AE has provided an assessment of the environmental and social risks associated with the enhanced management framework (EMF) with an indigenous peoples planning framework; and the results of this assessment will be used to inform the proposed safeguards. The AE is the main instrument for the AE's due diligence of the residual period activities. The ESMF provides a general assessment of the environmental and social impacts and risks of the proposed use of proceeds, including the potential for adverse effects on indigenous peoples and other social risks and impacts.

The AE has assessed the project to be of moderate risk, which is equivalent to category B under the GCF environmental and social risk categorization. The Secretariat agrees with this categorization, given that both the activities that generated the results payments and the proposed use of proceeds are consistent with the approved safeguards for forest conservation activities and would not involve any construction or significant water or earth-moving works.


---
**Page 93**
---

## Key risks and impacts and how they are addressed

The main issues/risks of the project include: (i) risks relating to the IS standards on indigenous peoples' IPs in areas where they are present; (ii) risk of displacements of internal land occupants; and (iii) the risk of material changes to forest management practices under Sustainable Forest Management contracts and Reforestation contracts as follows.

### Indigenous peoples

About 100,000 or 2 per cent of Costa Rica's population are considered indigenous. The area of approximately 35% of the country's territory is designated as indigenous peoples' lands and most of these lands are within the boundaries of the proposed PES programme. To ensure that indigenous peoples and their access to benefits, the REDD+ programme allowed group contracts in indigenous peoples' territories. In 2014–2015, out of the total of 1,971 contracts approved by the AE, 38% were group contracts for indigenous peoples.

The country then conducted nationwide consultations with leaders and representatives of indigenous peoples on the proposed PES programme. The AE concluded that the results had been generally positive. In the SSA, the AE has assessed that the PES activities in 2014–2015 fell short of the expected level of participation by indigenous peoples' IPs.

### Displacement

The risk of displacement is not considered a key issue in the PES programme is voluntary, hence the risk of involuntary resettlement due to any government land acquisition is ruled out. However, lands enrolled into the PES by their owners or legitimate occupants may be subject to eviction if they do not have adequate recourse for formal claims; they would be compelled to leave the land. This could happen in both indigenous peoples' territories and other areas where the programme operates.

Lands enrolled within contracted areas may be voluntary on the part of the integrated indigenous development associations (AIBs), but the AE has assessed that the PES activities in 2014–2015 fell short of the expected level of participation by indigenous peoples' IPs. The AIBs can opt out of the contract and utilize their land for other purposes, including agriculture or livestock production. However, if they choose to occupy parcels of land within declared indigenous peoples' reserve may at risk of being displaced. Finally, as part of the PES+ programme, PES was linked to other programmes such as the National Forest and Recreation Areas and the programme to purchase lands in protected wilderness areas. The proposal states that these projects should be adapted to address the social and environmental impacts of forestry resettlement impacts of these programmes. The Secretariat considers this should be adopted by all parties involved in the PES+ programme, including AIBs, as it would also address all the issues raised above. These issues have been partly addressed under the new PES+ programme, which includes a clause that states: "No activities will be undertaken in indigenous peoples' territories; up to 2 per cent of the areas under the contract can be used for subsistence agriculture production."

### Indirect impacts

Although one of the main objectives of the programme is to enhance the ecological functions of the forests, there is always the possibility that these functions could be negatively affected. This would in most cases be with human intervention. The risks would come from the PES measures that include reforestation or introduction of new species into the contracted land (i.e., under the reforestation contracts). These activities may have negative impacts on soil and water quality and displace native species. They could also promote new pests and diseases. This risk


---
**Page 94**
---

has been adequately addressed in the ESMP through the requirement for approval of PES implementation plans and monitoring and verification protocols.

The statement summarizes how the RBP activities and the proposed use of proceeds activities have addressed the risks and impacts in relation to the GCF environmental and social safeguards, and the requirements for stakeholder engagement and a grievance redress mechanism.


### Consistency of the design and implementation of PES-plus activities with the Country Strategy and the environmental and social safeguards of the GCF and the accredited entity

#### 3.1.1 The AEF completed an ESA that based on (i) a basic assessment of Costa Rica's national policies, laws and regulations to check whether they are aligned with the UNDP Social and Environmental Safeguards (SES) for projects applied during the period results to check whether they were also aligned with the GCF environmental and social safeguards; (ii) an assessment of project alignment, in line with key objectives of UNDP SES, without significant shortcomings. The report stated that the project activities did not pose any risk to cultural heritage or endangered species, nor did they give rise to communities or workers due to construction or other interventions. The report also stated that the project activities did not pose any risks to safe drinking water and wastewater outcomes.

Regarding the environmental efficiency of the project, the ESA concluded that the project activities did not pose any risks related to efficiency in the use and consumption of inputs or outputs, nor did they give rise to significant emissions of greenhouse gases, air pollutants, wastes or hazardous materials. The ESA also found that project activities did not pose any health and safety risks to communities.

Regarding involuntary resettlement, the ESA concluded that this standard does not apply because participation in the programme is voluntary. It also found that as part of the project implementation, Costa Rica has been undertaking an assessment of the potential presence of informal occupants along with other stakeholders, including experts for resettlement, and will update/revise the ESA based on the results of this additional analysis.

The AEF found that Costa Rica's policies, laws and regulations are consistent with the UNDP SES. The report states that the implementation of the PES programme did not result in any adverse impacts to natural forests, critical habitats or endangered species.


#### 3.1.2 Regarding indigenous peoples, the ESA found that the 2014-2015 PES activities were generally consistent with the UNDP SES and GCF environmental and social safeguards, which originally did not have special provisions for indigenous people, but since 2010 indigenous people have participated in the capacity building aspects of the programme through the AEFs. Free, prior and informed consent was obtained from indigenous communities before participation in the PES by the AEF was voluntary. The ESA concludes that the activities for which payments were sought during the results period did not pose any risk to cultural heritage or endangered species. The ESA also concluded that the PES activities do not pose any risk to cultural heritage.


The AEF has prepared an ESMP that provides background information of the environmental and social context, including a description of the biodiversity and serial issues in the project areas; a detailed stakeholder engagement plan; and a grievance redress mechanism. It identified some environmental impacts and risks relating to the UNDP SES.


---
**Page 95**
---

However, as the baseline conditions of the targeted project areas and other issues surrounding the implementation of the PES programmes are not fully known at this point, there may be a need to look in further detail at issues such as the potential for displacements of informal land occupiers and indigenous peoples from their customary lands; risks relating to labour working conditions in the large PES contracts; and potential re-emergence of latent diseases due to changes in vegetative cover. Assessments and relevant management plans should be updated accordingly, taking into account the specific objectives of project activities. The ESMF provides for (i) undertaking further assessment at project inception and/or during implementation as appropriate; (ii) preparing or updating relevant management plans; and (iii) preparing/updating various aforementioned management plans on the basis of the assessments.

The ESMP has likewise identified labour and working conditions-related risks for workers and volunteers engaged in the fire prevention activities. Project implementation assessments will be undertaken to identify and mitigate these risks, particularly in terms of labour hiring standards, including in relation to not hiring minors and providing adequate training and supervision. The ESMP also identifies potential exposure to vector-borne diseases and other hazards in the forests. Risks and impacts relating to re-emergence of certain diseases, including vector-borne diseases, are identified for communities living on indigenous territories. Community health and safety risks include the potential for re-emergence of certain diseases, including vector-borne diseases triggered by changes in the vegetation and forest cover.

The ESMP also identifies risks related to access to water resources and access to forests. The ESMP has indicated that it will revise the ESMP and other safeguards instruments based on lessons learned from the project implementation process with respect to the status of indigenous territories, informal occupiers and planned efforts for resettlement. The assessment will also review the applicability of the resettlement policy framework to this PES programme. Further assessments may be undertaken in order to identify risks related to access by indigenous peoples and people in contracted lands and the potential impacts to livelihoods as a result of these access issues.

Potential risks and impacts on biodiversity and natural resources comes from: (a) the PES modalities that involve reforestation or introduction of new species into the contracted land; (b) the possibility that some introduced species may be invasive and have the ability to be invasive and displace native species. They could also promote new pests and diseases. This risk, however, is considered low as it will be addressed through the ESMP and the approval of PES implementation plan provisions and monitoring and verification protocols.

The risks identified by the ESMP will continue to involve indigenous peoples communities in the project areas if the project activities are implemented without taking into account their views and concerns. These risks include: (a) failure of the programme to reflect the collective rights of the indigenous peoples on the development, utilization and control of natural resources; (b) failure of the programme to provide for the full enjoyment by the indigenous peoples of the benefits provided by the programme; (c) potential involuntary restrictions of access to agricultural land and other natural resources; (d) possible negative impacts on traditional livelihoods; and [e] long-term viability. PES contracts may negatively impact on indigenous peoples' traditional land use rights, including their customary tenure systems which are not always considered previously in the design of the programme, or the special PES for indigenous peoples modality, being a new approach to PES that is currently under development. The ESMP will also consider the indigenous peoples planning framework used for the REDD+ programme, which seeks out the requirements and needs of indigenous peoples communities with respect to their participation in the preparation of indigenous people plans; with guidance on how to prepare an indigenous peoples plan. This will be further addressed through the ESMP and the approval of PES implementation plans by the country's indigenous peoples policy, particularly the increasing recognition of customary laws and better representation mechanisms through the AIR.

### 3.2 Gender policy

The AF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan. The gender assessment is an important tool for the country’s commitment to gender equality and women's empowerment. It also provides a comprehensive analysis of existing gender disparities and inequalities.

- Costa Rica has a strong regulatory framework to promote gender equity. It is a signatory to and has ratified the main declarations and conventions to promote women's rights.
- The National Gender Equity Commission was developed through the National Gender Equity and Equality (PGEQ) 2018–2030 and the Action Plan for Implementing the PEG 2018–2030 Action Plan. The PGEQ Secretariat has been preparing a State Policy for Costa Rican Territorial Rural Development, which is part of the National Gender Equity Strategy.
- In the last decade, forest and climate change policies have evolved from Afforestation and Reafforestation (AFR) to Forests and Biodiversity. Gender equity and inclusion of the population in territorial rural development are recognized as one of the main objectives for achieving poverty reduction and sustainable territorial development policy for Costa Rica, the Política de Estado para el Desarrollo Rural Territorial Costarricense (PEDRT).


---
**Page 97**
---

### Gender-responsive, recognizing women as a priority group for attention and addressing gender considerations in its principles as well as in its actions and indicators.

#### The gender assessment illustrates that, despite the strong legal and regulatory framework, Costa Rican women continue to face a number of challenges and gender gaps at the local and national levels. Women are underrepresented in decision-making positions, particularly in decision-making, training and access to information, and access to control over economic resources. Women’s contributions to agriculture, forestry, livestock production, and environment is generally not recognized and undervalued. Women have fewer farms, and when they do own a farm it tends to be smaller than men’s farms. The role of women in agriculture is generally not recognized. Women find it more difficult to participate in forestry activities and projects because of gender stereotypes, time poverty and household responsibilities. Fewer women are involved in the production of forest products such as timber, firewood, and non-timber forest products (NTFPs). Furthermore, women producers have less access to information and their farms receive less technical support and extension services than men’s farms. Women also face gender-based discrimination at work and extension work in the agriculture and forestry sector. In addition to these gaps and inequalities, women are underrepresented in decision-making positions within government institutions. They receive less financial support and the number of women-owned farms benefiting from PS has been decreasing over time.

#### The GAP analysis indicates that there is a lack of gender mainstreaming at all levels of relevant government institutions, including with regard to their respective current gender mainstreaming; this reveals that officials of environmental institutions continue to have limited capacity in terms of gender equality and the inclusion of women’s perspectives. 

#### In accordance with Costa Rica’s GAP, specific gender activities have been identified and will be implemented as part of the project. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the project fulfills the requirements of the GCP Gender Policy. The GAP contains activities that address the challenges faced by women that are identified in the gender assessment, including baselines, indicators, and targets. The GAP also includes a gender focal point who will be responsible to mainstream gender throughout the project and support the implementation of the GAP. In addition, the gender focal point is expected to participate as an equal member of part of the senior decision-making structure. The gender focal point has actively participated in the development of the project’s Gender Action Plan (GAP) and will continue to be involved in overall management of the project. The GAP includes targeted activities that are linked to the gender analysis, including capacity building for women and other vulnerable groups such as indigenous members and particularly women, women-headed households, indigenous women, youth and the elderly. It includes measures to mitigate gender-based violence and provides grievance mechanisms.

#### GAP activities, taking into consideration the disadvantaged position of women, will prioritize women in all aspects of project implementation. Women applicants will be given a higher score in the PES evaluation scorers, thereby increasing their participation as well as their benefits from the national PES programme. The rollout of a new credit system for small farmers is expected to provide women with access to credit that is expected to enable them to have additional resources to purchase or legalise their landholdings. Women will be given priority in accessing training and extension services. One percent of the net payment received by FORAFIFO (the Rural Development Institute) from the Sistema Nacional de Áreas de Conservación Sustentable (SINAC) will be allocated to Costa Rica’s Green Business Fund. The inclusion of women as beneficiaries is also expected through the implementation of one of the GAP items to create and financially support an agroforestry PES modality that takes into consideration gaps in tenure and the characteristics of women’s farms, and that can be implemented only by women.

#### Further engagement that is expected to enhance the participation of women in the partnership that will be established between FORAFIFO, the Rural Development Institute

### Risk

#### 3.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

##### 32. Under the GEF pilot agreement, GCF is required to provide a payment of around USD 45 million for up to 14 million US$EQ achieved by Costa Rica in the period 2018–2015.

The volume of EqUs for which GCF funding is sought is within 30 per cent of the total payable volume as required under the agreement.

#### 3.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability (low risk)

##### 33. The AE, UNDP, will also serve as an EE for this project in close coordination with the FONAFIFO. The AE has had an agreement with Costa Rica since 1973 and is considered a reliable executing entity.

The GCF Secretariat proposes to use a performance-based payment modality for this project (Component 2) to ensure easier execution and faster disbursements.

#### 3.3.3 Programme operational execution risks (medium risk)

##### 54. Risk of double payments: The EqUs achieved by the country will be registered in the Lima PES registry, but there is no current registry managed by FONAFIFO. There were a few REDD+ plus projects implemented in Costa Rica. The Eqs achieved by these projects in 2014 and 2015 are not yet registered with the Lima PES registry. The project proposal offered to GCF to avoid the risk of double payments.

The funding proposal clarifies that the volume of EqUs for which GCF funding is sought will be based on the size of this project submitted to GCF. In the meanwhile, the national registry covering all sectors of the economy is being integrated in the National Climate Change Metric System and is expected to be operational by 2017.

##### 55. Use of proceeds: The proceeds from GCF will be used to strengthen the existing legal and institutional framework for REDD+, as well as the PES scheme for forest conservation and carbon stock enhancement. This will directly contribute towards achieving the NDC goal and the NDC Reward by the country. The end-use of the proceeds is as permitted under the REDD+ plus TDR.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

The UNDP, by virtue of being an organ of the United Nations and therefore being part of the same entity, is required to abide by all the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) Considerations List. The UNSC Considerations List includes a number of sanctions regimes that the sanctions regime is embodied in all aspects of UNDP operations and that UNDP uses the List as screens entities under consideration for contract or partnership with UNDP (including donors).

UNDP indicates that, as an integral part of its due diligence process for each project, it adheres to the UNSC Considerations List. The UNSC Considerations List was referenced in the proposal for the project.

PCAT requires UNDP to undertake a thorough due diligence of the risks in relation to the programme/project and the partners involved. This includes an assessment of any potential partner who will be involved in the implementation of such programmes/projects. Such capacity assessments are undertaken as part of the due diligence process for contract or partnership with UNDP against the UNSC Considerations List, an assessment of any history of sanctions violations, including a screening of terrorists or other fraudulent practices, and/or potential conflicts of interest.

Based on the PCAT, UNDP then builds an internal control component into the design of the project to ensure that adequate safeguards are put in place to prevent, monitor and address any risks and acts of fraud, corruption, money laundering and terrorist financing.

UNDP also undertakes a Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers (HACT) assessment, which represents a common operational (harmonized) framework for transferring cash to government or non-governmental partners (including NGOs) in the context of development cooperation. The common objective of the HACT framework is to support a closer alignment of development cooperation with the principles and practices of good governance, financial management and accountability. It serves as a set of procedures for requesting, disbursing, providing assurance on and reporting on funds to effectively manage risks, reduce transaction costs and promote transparency.

Cash transfers (above the threshold of USD 100,000 in a programme/cycle or otherwise in a context where there is no other UNDP cash transfer activity with that partner, based on your experience with the partner) are presumed to present a material risk to UNDP and will require a HACT micro-assessment and risk rating by qualified third party service providers. Spot checks.

## Section B: Carbon elements

### B.1. Forest reference emission level/Forest reference level (FREL/FRL)

| Criteria | Score |
| --- | --- |
| Is the FREL/FRL consistent with the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory, including the definition of forest used? | 1 |

**Remarks:** The AV notes that overall, the FREL/FRL maintains partial consistency, in terms of sources for the AB and the emission factors, with the GHG inventory included in Costa Rica's BUR. The AV noted some inconsistencies between the information provided by Costa Rica and the GHG inventory included in the BUR and the information used to assess the FREL/FRL. Costa Rica stated that a harmonization process with the GCF will be carried out for the years 1990, 1995 and 2000 and a GCF inventory for the years 1996, 1997 and 2000 is a planned improvement.


---
**Page 104**
---

The AT found that the definition of forest used for the FREL/FRL is consistent with the definition in the context of national GHG inventory and under the clean development mechanism. The forest definition is not consistent with the forest definition used under the Global Forest Resources Assessment (FRA) of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations. The AT encouraged Costa Rica to explore the possibility of adopting a unique forest definition, consistent with the FRA Forest Resources Assessment definition, to be used in all contexts.

In the TAR of the BIIR, the land use, land cover change and forestry (LULUCF) sector is based on full accounting between the GHG inventory and the estimated results of the implementation of forest carbon projects. The AT noted that the FREL/FRL has not yet been achieved.

Core Based FREL/FRL are based on the average annual historical net carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions associated with the activities reducing emissions from deforestation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks.

The TAR notes that a modified FREL/FRL improved the clarity and transparency in relation to the approach used for the construction of the FREL, except for the removal of the harvested wood products (HWP) pool. All notes that have been identified by the AT are related to the consistency assessment in relation to some assumptions made in the FREL/FRL assessment (e.g., forest reference level and land use change emissions estimation), although in the context of GCF it is noted that some of these issues were already addressed.

Is the FREL/FRL, based on historical data and is it equal to or below the average annual historical emissions during the reference period, unless a country is an HFLD country?

Is the FREL/FRL in accordance with the guidelines in decision 12/CPC.17?

(iv) Are the data and information provided for the FREL/FRL transparent? (Has information been provided to allow of understanding of how the FREL/FRL was or will be submitted on reference levels has been addressed?)

Is the FREL/FRL complete? (Has information been provided that allows for the reconstruction of the FREL/FRL?)

(vi) Is the FREL/FRL consistent? (Were similar methodologies applied consistently over the time series used for the construction of the FREL/FRL?)

No issues on consistency were identified in the TAR.


---
**Page 105**
---

The AT noted several issues and areas for improvement in relation to accuracy:

- Regarding the robustness of the models used to predict the rates of biomass accumulation in the different life zones of Costa Rica, the AT considers that additional sampling data from the Cifuentes Forest Carbon Partnership would increase the accuracy of future FREL/FRL.

The AT also noted that the data from Cifuentes do not take into account the carbon stock losses due to residual mortality and other disturbances (e.g., secondary forests) and may be underestimating carbon stocks in these areas, which could bias the growth model accordingly.

The AT also recommends that Costa Rica include in the estimation of forest biomass stock changes in secondary forests the losses currently not taken into account (i.e., those due to natural mortality) as this will enhance the accuracy of future FREL/FRL.

The FREL/FRL covers the activities "reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation" and "conservation." The AT acknowledges that these are the most significant activities. The AT notes that other activities could also be significant, in particular, sustainable management of forests. According to the modified FREL/FRL submission, Costa Rica currently does not have a baseline for the activities related to the aforementioned activities, although some better information is available from remote sensing data collected after 2013. Therefore, the AT notes that the current exclusion of forest degradation, conservation of forest carbon stocks and sustainable management of forests in FREL/FRL does not represent an overestimation of emissions.

The provisional baseline includes above-ground biomass, below-ground biomass, dead wood (only above-ground) and litter pools. The soil organic carbon and HWP pools were excluded, as indicated by Costa Rica.


---
**Page 106**
---

The TMR notes that efforts are ongoing to obtain better information in order to also include the dead wood in the below-ground pool. The AT notes that emissions from dead wood are likely to be insignificant.

With regard to emissions from the soil organic carbon pool, Costa Rica explained that its estimate of 10 Gg CO2e was based on the consideration that insufficient Tier 2 data were available for this pool and that it was not possible to apply a consistent treatment of emissions from soil organic carbon (i.e. the inclusion of this pool in the national GHG inventory is subject to omission) as an area for future technical improvement.

In its original FREL/FRL submission, Costa Rica estimated emissions from HWP using a country-specific methodology. The AT notes that this methodology was not consistent with the 2013 Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories and is also not comparable data with Costa Rica's national GHG inventory. The FREL/FRL submission regarded from the AF, Costa Rica excluded the HWP carbon pool in its modified methodology.

The FREL/FRL submission includes CO2 methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions related to land use change and deforestation. The exclusion of biomass burning and related emissions of CH4 and N2O is included for the period 1986–1996, on the basis that conversion of forest became illegal in 1997 with the passing of that Forest Law. Biomass burning was not considered significant after 1996. The AT noted that not including non-CO2 emissions from biomass burning in forest land remaining forest land in the period post-1996 is a departure from the national GHG inventory estimates for 2012 included in the Party's BUR in response. Costa Rica explained that the national GHG inventory includes GHG emissions from biomass burning in forest land remaining forest land

During the TA, the LULUCF experts identified some technical issues related to the accuracy of estimates of emission reductions from deforestation:
- Referring back to the TAR of the FREL/FRL, the experts noted that the carbon stock of the pre-1986 secondary forest was not considered in the calculation. This means that areas with different age class had equal proportions of areas. Although this was used as a simplification for the purpose of the study and the results, the LULUCF experts noted that this method could cause an overestimation of emission reductions.
- Plantation forest is included in the secondary forest category. The LULUCF experts explained that using for calculating emissions and removals may not be appropriate for this type of forest because it does not include subsequent deforestation area and the associated emissions and subsequent reforestation or afforestation area with forest expansion and the associated removals.
- Costa Rica provided some information related to the areas covered by cloud cover. The LULUCF experts noted that the presence of cloud cover and shadow in the remote-sensing images could lead to errors in the results.

The LULUCF experts, Costa Rica explained that there are differences between the period for which the results were calculated of the FREL and the period for which the results can be corrected. The LULUCF experts noted that addressing the error is not possible with the available remote sensing land use maps by Costa Rica will further enhance the accuracy of the results, which would be an important part of the stepwise approach.

### The following paragraphs describe the main findings in those sections of the scorecard where the country has scored below its target (see Table 1 for the full scorecard and further information on the scoring criteria) (further details are provided in the annex).

#### B.1.(Q) Conformity with the forest reference emissions levels (FREL) (forest reference level (FRL)) and the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory.

The Assessment Team [AT] noted some inconsistencies between the activity data and methodologies used to assess the FREL/FRL, namely: (a) the national GHG inventory includes CO2 emissions from biomass burning while non-CO2 emissions from biomass burning are included in the FREL/FRL for the period 1990–2014; (b) the national GHG inventory includes carbon stock change estimates for plantations but not for primary and secondary forests, whereas the FREL/FRL includes both primary and secondary forests.

FREL/FRL includes both primary and secondary forests, along with an observation that plantations are included under secondary forest and that the information on plantations used in the FREL/FRL is based on data from the National Forest Inventory (NFI) and the Census. In the funding proposal, Costa Rica pointed out that it has enhanced the consistency of its national GHG inventory with the FREL/FRL by updating the national GHG inventory for 1990, 1995 and 2000 and that this would be reflected in the country's next national GHG inventory. The AT notes that these inconsistencies are not material to the consistency of the FREL/FRL but that they will be resolved as part of the next NFI (inventory or FREL/RL, if justified).

#### B.1.(Q) Transparency of the FREL/FRL (data and information provided). The AT identified a number of areas for improvement in terms of transparency of data and information, including by describing the description of how primary and secondary forests were defined in the national GHG inventory and the methodology used to estimate emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (including this work as a data repository), providing more robust data to support the assessment of the FREL/FRL. The AT also noted that there was no information on the age classes, up to 400 years old, with their proportions in the presentation or comparison of the results of the Cifor model [used to predict the rates of human occupation in the different forest types] and the national GHG inventory.

The AT notes that the FREL/FRL is based on the (FREL) default factsheet; presenting carbon stock factors used to assess the emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, including those for primary forests, as well as the FREL/RL CR. In its funding proposal, Costa Rica pointed out progress in a number of these areas, including by providing more robust data to support the assessment of the FREL/FRL manual, improving the consistency of the FREL/FRL with the national GHG inventory and updating the national GHG inventory. Generally speaking, the score is 1 (significant issues were raised and were resolved), although there were not resolved due to the limitation of time and data but were not material to the consistency of the FREL/FRL and the country has provided a plan on how it will seek to overcome them).

### Proposed results-based payments: USD $4,119,143

# B.1.e) Accuracy of the FREL

The Costa Rica technical assessment report (TAR) identified several areas for future technical improvement that would improve the accuracy of the FREL, including: (a) identification of primary and secondary forests; it was noted that the subnational forest inventory data used in the FREL is not consistent with the reference map distinguished in the 1978/1980 map; (b) classification of forest/non-forest; the AT encouraged Costa Rica to reanalyze the area classified as "non-forest" and include the main forest types, including secondary forests; (c) data quality; it was noted that the relevant information: (c) age-class distribution in secondary forests: more robust data should be provided on the age structure of secondary forests. The TAR recommended that this data is representative of all possible age classes, up to 600 years old, with equal proportions of areas at each age class; (d) sampling design and sample size; the AT noted that the current sampling plan on which the carbon growth model is based may result in a partial representation of the forest structure. The TAR recommended that the results from the Costa Rica review and increase the sampling plans on which the carbon growth model is based in order to improve its accuracy, particularly for secondary forests; (e) data quality; (f) accuracy of the carbon growth model: the AT noted that the data from the Gibeonite model do not take into account the carbon stock losses due to rotations in plantations, which are currently included as part of the forest area. The TAR recommended including carbon losses, and pointed out the need to revise the growth model accordingly; the AT also recommended that the FREL should include the carbon stock losses associated with stock changes in secondary forests the losses currently not taken into account by the modeling process. The AT noted that the inclusion of these losses in the FREL should improve the accuracy of future FREE/FRL submissions. Consequently, the score to 1 could be increased if Costa Rica provided a plan on how it will overcome the problems observed due to the limitation of time and data but were not material to the accuracy of the FREE/FRL and the country has provided a plan on how it will use to overcome them.

### B.1.e) Inclusion of the most significant pools in the FREL

The TAR acknowledged that Costa Rica included the most significant activities (reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, and increasing carbon stock) of the five activities identified as paragraph 70 of United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) decision J.13/6, in accordance with national capabilities and existing data. The TAR noted that Costa Rica has not included the following: (a) particular sustainable management of forests and reducing emissions from forest degradation; (b) afforestation and reforestation activities; (c) land use change and forestry (LUCF); (d) soil organic carbon, degraded (below ground) and harvested wood products (HWP).

### B.1.q) Inclusion of the most significant pools in the FREL

The carbon pools included in the FREL are above-ground biomass (trees and non-trees), below-ground biomass (soil organic carbon, degraded (below ground)) and harvested wood products (HWP).


---
**Page 121**
---

The AT noted that emissions from deadwood are likely to be insignificant.* With regard to emissions from the soil organic carbon pool, Costa Rica explained that it had not included this pool or the provision of more information justifying its omission as an area for future technical development. The AT considered that this omission was justified by the fact that Costa Rica estimated emissions from HWP using a country-specific methodology assuming that only 10% of the fraction of HWP does not oxidize, which is consistent with the fact that this methodology is in line with the methodologies used in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines and is also not consistent with Costa Rica's GIC inventory. In response to a question from the AT regarding the inclusion of emissions from biomass burning in the FRES, Costa Rica noted its funding proposal. Costa Rica noted that it will consider including this pool in future FRES. The AT considered that the omission of biomass burning and related land use activities, such as reducing emissions from forest degradation and sustainable management of forests (FSDM), is justified by the lack of sufficient quality data or/and the omission does not overestimate emissions or underestimate removals, noting that this could change in a positive way with better data in the future.

### L.1(c) Inclusion of all significant sources of emissions

The AT noted that the inclusion of biomass burning and related emissions of methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) from land use activities is justified by the fact that these emissions occurred on cropland and grassland that occurred in the period 1980–1996, and were excluded in the post-1996 national law No. 7575, leading to a dramatic decrease in slash and burn agriculture after 1996. However, Costa Rica included emissions of these gases in its national GHG inventory for the period 2000–2004. The AT considered that Costa Rica explained that the national GHI inventory includes GHG emissions from biomass burning because it is a significant source of emissions and that these emissions were not included in the FRES because they were not considered to be significant sources of emissions. The AT noted that the national GHI inventory does include land use change associated with forest conversion for agriculture, forestry and other land use sector. Costa Rica also explained that recalculation for the inventories for 1990, 1995 and 2000 are a planted improvement. It continued to be consistent with the national law No. 7575. The AT noted that data for secondary forests and tree plantations, as reported by SFRSFOR. No data are available for primary forest conversion. Costa Rica explained that it has not included emissions from biomass burning in these areas does not incur land-use change. The AT noted that even though biomass burning does not incur land-use change, accounting in full for these losses may result in an overestimation of the emissions from deforestation in preliminary data.


---

---

forest areas. The AT considered that the treatment of CH4 and N2O emissions from biomass burning (i.e., the inclusion of this pool or the provision of more information justifying its omission) as an area for future technical improvement to ensure consistency with other FREL submissions.

The LULUCF experts noted that Costa Rica has indicated in its submission that it will consider including these gases in future FREL submissions in the light of the potential inclusion of additional REDD-plus activities, such as reducing emissions from forest degradation and deforestation (R-EDD) under Article 6.4 of the Paris Agreement over a 50-year period. However, the AT noted that this is not reflected in the submission itself (i.e., it is not justified due to lack of data and/or the omission does not overstimate emissions). The LULUCF experts also noted that Costa Rica has indicated that it will consider including these gases in future FREL submissions.

### B.1.(iii) Significant issues related to the application of IPCC guidelines/good practice guidance in the construction of the FREL/FRL

The AT noted that the forest-related emissions and removals associated with land-use change (including deforestation and the conversion of forest land to other land-use categories have been assessed on the basis of a coupled model approach, which is not consistent with the IPCC Guidelines (see para. 9, 16 and 17 of the TAT). The reason is therefore 1 significant issue related to the application of IPCC guidelines/good practice guidance in the construction of the FREL/FRL (the AT has provided a plan on how it will seek to overcome them).

### B.1.(iv) How does the reference level for the results included in the proposed compensation approach compare with the reference level used by Costa Rica before its previous REDD-plus reference level had been submitted from Costa Rica to the UNFCCC secretariat, thus the FREL/FRL?

The LULUCF experts noted that Costa Rica did not provide information on aggregate uncertainties related to the FREL (which was submitted in 2013) and therefore could not be compared with the proposed compensation approach.

### B.1.(v) Accuracy of the data and information provided in the technical annex

The LULUCF experts concluded that the data and information provided in the technical annex are complete, accurate to the extent possible (see para. 27 of the technical report on the technical analysis of Costa Rica's proposed FREL/FRL). In addition, with regard to the results of the TATR, the LULUCF experts noted that because Costa Rica used a consistent methodology for calculating emissions and removals in the context of both the FREL/FRL and the results of implementing the activities for 2014 and 2015, the net effect on the reference level is not affected by the differences between the two. The related observations of the LULUCF experts are as follows: (a) as noted in paragraph 15(c) of the report on the technical assessment of Costa Rica's proposed FREL/FRL submitted to the TATR, the AT considered that the reference level for this forest was established on the basis of the assumption that each age class up to 40 years old had equal proportional representation. The LULUCF experts noted that this is not consistent with the current state of the forest. Although this carbon stock value was used consistently to estimate both the FREL/FRL and the data for the technical annex, it could be considered that there may have been an overestimation of emission reductions and could therefore be considered by Costa Rica as one of its significant issues related to the construction of the FREL/FRL. (b) In line with the stoppage approach; and (c) plantation forest is included in the secondary forest category. However, the assumptions used for calculating emissions and removals may not be consistent with the current state of the forest. ## Impact potential

### Scale: High

**Costa Rica is a Central American country with a population of around 5 million people and a land area of approximately 21,850 km². In the 1940s, 76% of Costa Rica was choked in humid rainforests. Then a long process of deforestation in order to grow crops and raise livestock took place, resulting in the country losing between a half and a third of forest cover by 1982.**

However, in 1996, through the enactment of the Forest Law, the government made it illegal to clear forests for agriculture or livestock production.

**Ecosystem Services (PES), administered by the National Forestry Emission Fund (FONAFIFO), pays landowners to protect forests in return for the benefits they provide, such as conserving biodiversity and carbon sequestration.**

The country shows a decreasing trend of average deforestation of primary forest between 2014-2015 compared with 2008-2009 (1,000,000 hectares [ha]) forest cover. Today almost 60 per cent of the land is once again forest.

* **The REDD+ pilot project is in its final phase for 2014 and 2015. The total volume of REDD+ plans resulting from this pilot project is 3,789,749 tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) as the result of Costa Rica's second BUR to 16,794,749 tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) as the sum of 7,049,744 and 7,350,555 tCO2e for 2014 and 2015 respectively.**

* **The total volume of results offered in 2014 is 6,850,000 tCO2e and 2015 are being offered to the domestic market. The total volume of results offered in 2014 is 7,350,555 tCO2e and 6,946,111 tCO2e for 2015, a total of 14,697,771 tCO2e.**

According to the technical annex to Costa Rica's second BUR for 2014–2015, the country has a LUCF target of 38,000,000 tCO2e. The REDD+ pilot project is one of several voluntary incentive-based programme operating during the period for which REDD+ are sought and is one of six policies and measures of the National REDD+ Strategy.

For Costa Rica, a time series of land cover and land use maps was developed. This time series was specifically designed for REDD+ plus with the goal of ensuring consistency between the two historical reference periods 1980–1996 and 1997–2009. The proposed FREL will be estimated as the sum of the annual average CO2 net emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (DRFG) for the period 1980-1996 and of forest carbon stock during these two historical reference periods. Carbon stock enhancements in forest lands are not included in the calculation of the FREL, which is consistent with the methodology for Costa Rica's national conditions by CIFORs in 2008. The TAR acknowledged that Costa Rican forests have experienced significant increases, and the total significant periods of emissions related to forests.

10. Costa Rica has improved the FREL/FRL by including more information on the representation of the carbon growth model, publishing the FREE TOOLS CR manual, improving the consistency of the FREL/FRL with the national GHG inventory and reanalyzing the role of the forest sector in the country’s emissions.
13. Even though Costa Rica has fewer forest fires than other Central American countries, these still account for important emissions. According to Costa Rica's GHG inventory, under current land use change scenarios, the total emissions from forest fires are estimated at 5 Gg CO2 and CH4 emissions and 2,857,165 tCO2e, with all of the loss over 0.005 ha being attributed to forest fires.
14. The National Strategy for Integrated Fire Management 2012-2021 has been effective, showing the commitment of the country to act with concrete measures.
