The PES scheme in Costa Rica is a key element in ensuring conditions for effective REDD+ plus implementation through securing implementation of the safeguards provisions and strengthening the institutional framework for forest conservation, management, and use; and (ii) expanding and improving the PES scheme with emphasis on the Special Payment for Environmental Services in Indigenous Territories and supporting the National Strategy for Integrated Fire Management.
16. The impact can be judged not only by the results of the past but also by the continuous progress made over time. In fact, Costa Rica has proved that it has successful policies in place and maintained the trend of reducing deforestation and increasing forest cover over the last 20 years, with a combination of effective forest law enforcement, land use planning, and the implementation of PES schemes.
17. For 2016 and 2017 show no evidence of reversals of emission reductions in the years following the introduction of the scheme. The deforestation rate (ha/year) continued to be reduced over the period 2016-2018, which provides strong evidence of a climate change mitigation effect.
18. In response to a question by the TAP about the risk of reversals relating to the possibility of a funding reduction for the PES scheme, the presenters stated that the scenario seems unlikely and that the public sector (associated with the state forest service) and private forest reserves (associated with ecotourism) play a key role in avoiding land-use change and becoming more resilient. The government has also been able to use the proceeds from international resources to increase the current level of investment in the scheme.
19. The impact on climate change of the Costa Rica model has proved to be effective for the purpose of reducing deforestation, increasing forest cover, improving the quality of control and the number of beneficiaries and amount of hectares involved in the PES scheme has allowed good results that could be scaled up within the country with the proceeds from the GCF RFP.
14. **Paradigm shift potential**  
   *Scale: High*  
20. The PES scheme of Costa Rica has proved to be effective in reversing the trend of deforestation and increasing forest cover over the last 20 years, while also ensuring that Costa Rica also created FONAFIFO as the main fund to manage the PES scheme with cross subsidies from the energy sector to the environmental sector. The PES scheme is mainly financed by 3.5 per cent from the fuel tax and 1.5 per cent from the water fee. In addition, a significant part of the financing for the Costa Rican PES scheme came from the fuel tax and 6 per cent from the water fee. The rest has been covered by donations and loans from international financial organizations.
21. Costa Rica was also a pioneer country in successfully implementing a National REDD+ Strategy and has demonstrated that it is possible to implement such strategies while fulfilling the requirements of the Warsaw Framework for REDD+ giving confidence to the


---
**Page 125**
---

UNESCO and the REDD+ scheme is feasible. The ultimate objective of Costa Rica’s National REDD+ Strategy is to support the national objective of achieving carbon neutrality by setting it in its voluntary pre-2030 commitments and NDCs.

At the national and territorial level, Costa Rica's early implementation of payment and metering systems for forest conservation has directly contributed to a paradigm shift in this area. Furthermore, there has been a trend of maintaining this pathway with successful policies among the country’s political leaders, which has led to the creation of a new generation of carbon credits that are both economically viable and contributing to a green national recovery plan following the conservative target of 2030.

The prioritization criteria in the PES scheme were established by the Ministry of the Environment and Energy, involving different modalities for forest protection, reforestation, natural regeneration, and sustainable use. The main requirements that must be met by applicants include the presentation of a copy of the farm map at its original scale. On a first come, first served basis, the application is submitted to the regional office or to FONAFIFO and an evaluation scorecard is assigned, assigning a score to each item. Once all items have been evaluated, the beneficiary will be informed by letter that they are eligible for PES funds. Once beneficiaries are selected, they proceed to signing an agreement. It should be noted that the PES funds are deposited in a special account at the National Treasury of the Ministry of Finance directly to the bank account of each beneficiary.

In the case of the indigenous peoples, their local governments or Indigenous Development Associations, the procedure is similar but with some differences. The proceeds are decided by their Indigenous Development Association, respecting the indigenous world view.

The project proposes to use the proceeds in supporting indigenous communities, even though deforestation is not being driven by them. The proposal states that since 1997, there have been no new forest concessions granted for timber production or other different PES modalities, but mainly for protecting forests, representing an investment for the period 1997-2030.

PES is the main source of government cash transfers for indigenous peoples and as such it plays a critical role in the economy of indigenous territories. In response to a question from the Tafirón community, which had been granted forest concessions but later disappeared, the proponents argued that even though deforestation would not increase, there could be an increase in illegal logging.

It is important to note that participation in the PES scheme is voluntary and the choice of the modality is the free, prior and informed decision of the indigenous communities through consultation with their representatives.

In terms of the risk of reversals, the country has provisions that ensure non-reversals from REDD+ plus actions, including an updated National Forest Development Plan, which is issued every five years. The plan includes a commitment to maintaining the current forest economic path towards carbon neutrality in 2031 as part of its voluntary actions pre-2030. It also proposes that the country will continue to be committed to this goal and will adhere to the 2012 level.

The implementation of the Forestry Law, including the ban on the conversion of forests to other uses is of great importance. Furthermore, the National Strategy for Integrated Forest Management has been effective and the proposed project will reinforce the implementation of the strategy by strengthening the capacity of communities in forest resource prevention and communications and early warning systems.


---
**Page 126**
---

28\. The government has a long-term vision involving the National Decarbonization Plan and the Biodiversity Strategy, which together propose an 8 per cent increase in forest cover and maintaining the cover at 60 per cent by 2030. The aim to transition to a zero-emission economy is expected to be achieved through the implementation of innovative financial options such as those being proposed by the biodiversity finance project BIOFIN.

29\. The PES scheme in Costa Rica was well established and has been a source of inspiration for many PES schemes around the world. It has also provided an opportunity to explore other policy financial options with a view to ensuring that the NDC and REDD-plus targets are met. The proposed project will use the potential to evolve and to scale up innovations, with innovative policies and measures that could be replicated in many other countries currently engaged in REED-plus.

1\.4\.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

30\. Costa Rica has been able to create a policy framework for reducing emissions by early implementation of REDD-plus. It has been able to reverse the trend of one of the highest deforestation rates in the world. Moreover, the country has been able to increase forest cover with the help of the PES scheme.

31\. The country has been able to create and consolidate a series of policy instruments such as the National System of Conservation Areas (SINAC), the national PES scheme, the National Strategy for Integrated First Measures (SNIFM) and the National Biodiversity Strategy.

32\. The overall framework has served to enable the country to become an example of successful implementation of a PES scheme and to evolve into exploring the transition to a zero-emissions economy.

1\.4\.2 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

33\. The use of the proceeds will support the implementation of the provisions of the Cancun Safeguards with a robust legal and institutional framework. The country has already fulfilled the Warsaw Framework for Action on REDD-plus, as evidenced by the letter sent by the UNFCCC secretariat to its first summary of information on safeguards and establishing a safeguard information system. Costa Rica has completed its national clarification of the Cancun Safeguards and has identified the relevant legal and institutional framework to apply a national approach to safeguards.

34\. The proposed project will further support the development of an enhanced safeguard information system as well as further strengthen Costa Rica's capacity for monitoring compliance with REDD-plus safeguards in line with the requirements of market and non-market sources of finance.

35\. The proposed project will further support additional activities that need to be carried out related to policy, legislation, capacity-building, communications, governance and addressing grievances, among others.

1\.4\.3 Scalability and replicability

36\. The proposed project will scale the PES scheme to reach new beneficiaries in order to meet the ambitious 2030 targets. Moreover, it will scale up the National Strategy for Integrated First Measures (SNIFM) to cover all of Costa Rica's territory.

37\. Costa Rica's innovative policies and measures around REDD-plus have built the confidence of other countries that have witnessed the results and are finding options to comply with the UNFCCC process. With the use of the proceeds, the country will be able to scale up the

# Sustainable development potential

## Environmental co-benefits

### Economic benefits

The project supports private forest owners, including individuals (emphasizing women owners and co-owners of the forests) and forest owner organizations, as well as indigenous communities that are willing to conserve and regenerate forest resources.

The proposed project includes a pilot programme for the implementation of the National Strategy for Integrated Fire Management, as well as SINAC. Both institutions are critical to ensuring economic benefits for the country, as they effectively manage the forests and provide services such as fire management, which plays a major part in the tourism industry, providing the country with important economic revenues.

The project also supports indigenous communities through the social programmes that are currently financed by the Indigenous Development Associations using PES.


---
**Page 128**
---

### Gender-sensitive development

1. In 1998, Costa Rica approved the Law for the Promotion of Women’s Social Equality which aims to ensure that women have equal access to all areas of life and to promote gender-equal rights between men and women in the political, economic, social and cultural fields. Likewise, the National Policy for Gender Equality and Equity 2018-2030 considers the framework of commitment to gender equality.
2. Funding from FONAFIFO allows women to receive payment for the ecosystem services that they provide through their participation in conservation activities. The funding provides support and incentives for women to get involved in conservation activities.
3. Access to the PES scheme in Costa Rica is granted based on land-tenure rights. Given that 41 per cent of all agricultural land in Costa Rica is owned by indigenous peoples, there was a risk of unequal distribution in the PES scheme. In Costa Rica there are about 12,598 women property owners and 30,760 men property owners. Women own or co-own, directly or indirectly, 15.6 per cent of all farms and 8.1 per cent of the total agricultural area belonging to natural persons in the country.
4. Recognizing these realities, the PES scheme has since 2010 included an objective to increase the number of women beneficiaries of the scheme. From 1997 to 2015 13.1 per cent of PES contracts were signed with women owners. This equates to a total of 48,600 women owners out of a total of 16,712 contract parties in Costa Rica between 1997 and 2015.
5. The National REDD+ Secretariat, with technical support from the WSSD project, developed a workshop on “Testing the design of the credit pool for addressing gender in the REDD+ process in 2016”. The overall objective of the workshop was to explore the gender


---
**Page 129**
---

### Needs of the recipient

**Scale: Medium**

1. **Costa Rica is a success story in terms of development:** It is considered an upper-middle-income country which has shown a steady economic growth over the past 25 years. This growth resulted from an outward-oriented strategy, based on openness to foreign investment and gradual liberalization of trade policies.
2. **The country's gross domestic product (GDP) per capita has grown at an average rate:** This rapid growth has resulted in one of the lowest poverty rates in Latin America and the Caribbean.
3. **As with all other countries,** as a result of COVID-19, Costa Rica’s gross domestic product is projected to decrease by 2% in 2020 compared to 2019. The country's GDP is expected to increase, partly owing to the impact of the health emergency on tourism and trade, plus a contraction in the sectors agriculture and construction. However, a rebound is expected by 2021 as restrictions are lifted.
4. **Two pressing development challenges stand out:** the fiscal situation and persistent inequality. The government has committed itself to reducing poverty, promoting social inclusion, growth and sustainability. To respond to the fiscal challenge the country enacted a new tax law in April 2020 which includes an additional added tax, a capital gains tax and revised income tax.

5. **In terms of vulnerable groups,** the proposed project is targeting the indigenous communities living on the territory since the 2011 census, undertaken by the National Institute of Statistics and Census. As of that date, there was a total of 104,143 inhabitants who define themselves as indigenous people in Costa Rica. Of these, 58% are men and 42% women. The indigenous population settled in the "reserve" is 35,943 inhabitants (34.5 per cent) and is located in a total area of 107 km². This reserve represents only 0.6% of the country's territory and is distributed in different regions of the country, and occupied by eight ethnic groups. Of these, nine live on reserves having the lowest development indices and the highest poverty rates.

*See https://www.worldbank.org/en/region/costarica/overview.*


---
**Page 130**
---

## 1.7 Country ownership

### Alignment with national climate strategy

**Scale: High**

Costa Rica has been a strong proponent of green, sustainable and resilient development, particularly in regard to the protection of natural resources, forests and their environmental services. The country is committed to achieving its climate change objective by becoming a carbon-neutral economy by 2021, as a culmination of its voluntary pre-2020 action and its post-2020 commitments under the Paris Agreement, supporting the implementation of the NDC.

* **Costa Rica’s NDC:** The country’s NDC (Annex I) is 45.9 million tonnes CO₂ equivalent (MtCO₂e). It recognizes that carbon neutrality is based on the mitigation potential of the forestry sector. Moreover, it includes a commitment to enhance forest sinks and other terrestrial ecosystems through actions such as afforestation and reforestation, and actions on mitigation options (enhancing carbon sinks). The contributions of the NDC to Costa Rica’s climate change mitigation are reflected in the country's GGRF inventories. Furthermore, the PES scheme is one of several instruments that can contribute to achieving the country’s climate change mitigation (ecosystem-based adaptation).

* **Forest Law:** The 1997 Forest Law prohibits land use change in forested areas enacted by the Forest Law of 1996, and the National Strategy for Integrated Fire Management, enacted in 1997, set guidelines for the planning, monitoring and evaluation of various activities related to fire management.

The country has a law on biodiversity, enacted in 1998. This established two entities: the National System of Biodiversity (SINAB). This law establishes a series of criteria, including the preservation of biodiversity loss or in those cases where it is not possible of not postponing the adoption of effective measures to protect biodiversity when there is scientific evidence.

The implementation of the Forestry Law works in parallel with SINAC, which has a robust institution endowed with an illegal clearing control strategy and functional geographical information systems to allow the continued monitoring of the forest coverage.


---
**Page 131**
---

Finally, the country created the Citizens' Consultative Council on Climate Change and a participatory platform for citizens founded under the National Policy of Government Openness. It aims to strengthen the accountability mechanisms and to make information available and accessible to all citizens in order to promote their participation in national climate change strategies, including Costa Rica's commitments under the Paris Agreement.

### 5.7.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

The proposed project will be implemented under the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) Direct Implementation Modality. UNDP will be the executing entity/Implementing Partner for this project. The Ministry of Environment and Energy is responsible for managing this project, including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions and achieving results. The Ministry of Environment and Energy has a long history and experience in developing REDD+ plus proposals for GCF as well as projects related to forestry.

The Ministry of Environment and Energy is the national designated authority for GCF. It oversees the implementation of all GCF-financed projects, including those related to the conservation of ecosystems and the sustainable use of natural resources.

FONAFIFO, which was created in 1995, will manage the payment for the PSR scheme under the REDD+ Strategy. FONAFIFO is a public entity that manages forest resources. FONAFIFO was created by the Forestry Law, with the purpose of promoting forest management and reforestation as well as the conservation of biodiversity and natural resource resilience. In 2019 FONAFIFO managed an annual budget of around USD 36 million. It also managed the National Forest Fund (FONAFIFO) which finances the PSR scheme.

The proposed project will have three levels of management for its implementation: (i) a decision-making level, which includes a Project Board in charge of strategic decision-making; with a Ministry of Environment and Energy representative as chairperson; (ii) an operational level of the UNDP in its role as accredited entity to GCF, and a Project Director, who will be the Resident Representative of UNDP; and (iii) a Project Management Unit in charge of the overall implementation of the project.

The Project Board will include the Resident Representative of UNDP and will have the Ministry of Environment and Energy as beneficial representative to ensure the realization of project components. The Project Board will also include an independent expert, who is a development partner selected from academia to provide guidance with regard to the technical feasibility of the project.

The Technical Committee, which was established for the National REDD+ Strategy, will be expanded to serve as Technical Committee of the project, consisting of high-level technical representatives from: i) the Ministry of Environment and Energy; ii) the Costa Rican Academy Institute; iii) the National Center for Environmental Information; and iv) SINAC. This Committee will be expanded to include the Climate Change Directorate to ensure coordination with the broader REDD+ Strategy.

Costa Rica has shown that it has good institutions in place to manage conservation and climate change schemes, with an evident track record showing ability to manage the proposed REDD project.


### 5.7.3 Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

Costa Rica developed a broad consultation process to develop the National REDD+ Strategy and implementation plan. An extensive stakeholder engagement process was carried out in Costa Rica during the first REDD+ plus readiness phase (2011-2019), with funds from the


---
**Page 132**
---

Forest Carbon Partnership Facility and an investment of approximately USD 140,000 over 180 participatory stakeholder engagement activities were carried out in the country, including town hall meetings, information and capacity-building workshops and analysis of proposals by the regional REDD+ focal point. The GCF RFP project will support the implementation of three of the main action lines of the strategy.

### 1.2 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

- The total volume of REDD-plus results offered by Costa Rica to the GCF RFP programme is estimated at approximately 4,459,737 tCO2eq., corresponding to the average emissions in the historical period 1997–2009. From this volume, the GCF volume of emission reductions is equivalent to about USD 68 million.

- It is important to note that for this period over 99 per cent of results are being offered to GCF. There is small volume allocated for REDD-plus offset units which comes from the domestic market and is not significant in terms of the total volume. The volume of emission reductions has a significant impact on the size of GCF’s proposal (in 2014 it was 19,438 tCO2eq and in 2015 it was 32,089 tCO2eq). This is equivalent to about USD 6.7 million for the volume for 2014 and 2015.

- Payments are expected to a fixed price of USD 6 per tonne of CO2eq in 2014 and 2015, and in accordance with the terms of reference of the RFP pilot programme. Costa Rica will receive a total of USD 38 million for this period.

- Between 2014 and 2019, Costa Rica is expected to achieve a total reduction in emissions from deforestation of about 36,985,874 tCO2eq. The indicative volume expected to be offered to GCF for the period 2014–2019 is approximately 3,000,000 tCO2eq. This represents a total of payments per country of 30 MUSD during the entire duration of the GCF RFP pilot programme.

- It is important to note that some uncertainty remains regarding the eligibility of REDD-plus results for payment under different schemes. In this context, Costa Rica seeks to diversify its sources of funding and will continue to explore opportunities to access both market and non-market sources based on international partnerships in line with the San Jose Principles.


---
**Page 133**
---

## PES Scheme

### Background

- **60% of proceeds from Costa Rica’s REDD+ and BIP will be reinvested to scale up and strengthen the existing PES scheme**, which has been in operation for over 20 years.
- The current PES scheme covers only 10 per cent of Costa Rica's forest, expanding the scheme is a priority for the country.

### Challenges

- **The FONAFIFO budget has accommodated only 42% of PES applicants**, with GCF resources enabling more forests to be included in the PES scheme.
- In the short term, GCF resources are critical to increasing current level of investment in the PES scheme and to cover the costs associated with the transition from gasoline consumption sharply in Costa Rica during a government-imposed "lockdown" designed to stop the spread of COVID-19.

### Future Outlook

- The Comptroller General of the Republic estimates that the FONAFIFO budget will be reduced by 20% by the end of 2020.
- Therefore, for 2020, the FONAFIFO budget is estimated to decrease by US$ 2.6 to US$ 13 million.

### Decision

- Costa Rica has decided to engage in a process of "decoupling" of its economy and has put forward a proposal to reduce fossil fuel consumption voluntarily committed and in the NDC.
- Therefore, if consumption of fuel decreases, so will the current sources of income for FONAFIFO.

### Alternative Financing

- The BIP from GCF will support the scheme in the short and medium term, but it is important to begin exploring other international cooperation or with national alternatives to finance the PES scheme.


---
**Page 134**
---

Finally, the use of the proceeds will be also used to prevent forest fires, which is always a more cost-effective measure than dealing with the consequences of forests fires for the economy of Costa Rica.

The TAP concludes that the use of the proceeds will be well spent and will ensure the continuation of Costa Rica's success in avoiding deforestation and promoting regeneration.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project.

- Presenting carbon stock factors used to assess the potential for forest restoration and reforestation.

(D) Identification of forest/non-forest: The AT encourages Costa Rica to reclassify the area classified as "non-forest" and include in the main outcome of this verification activity a data repository of all FREL/FRL relevant information.

(c) Age structure of forests in secondary forests: provide more robust data in order to support the assumption that secondary forests in 1982/1990 are representative of all possible age classes, up to 600 years old.

(d) Representativeness of the carbon growth model: The AT noted that the sampling process on which the carbon growth model is based may not be representative of the entire forest area included in the FREL/FRL and recommends that Costa Rica revise and reclassify the data used for the estimation of the carbon growth model in order to ensure the representativeness of all the forest included in the FREL/FRL.

(e) Accuracy of the carbon growth model: The AT noted that the data from Ciforest do not take into account the carbon stock losses due to natural causes and deforestation in secondary forests, including primary forests and may thus be underestimating carbon losses, and pointed out the need for a more accurate estimation. The AT also recommends that Costa Rica include in the estimation process of carbon stock changes in secondary forests the losses currently not taken into account.

As noted above, Costa Rica is working to improve these issues

# GCF/8.27(02)/A/004

## sustainable management of forests and reducing emissions from forest degradation. According to the modified FRL/FRL submission, Costa Rica currently does not have sufficient good quality information on the extent of forest cover and associated activities. During the assessment week, the Party provided the AT with national forestry statistics available from the national information system (SINIFOR) as well as other relevant information on forest management areas. Costa Rica further noted that their current reporting is based on the five activities identified in the FRL/FRL submission: afforestation and reforestation, forest degradation, conservation of forest carbon stocks and sustainable management of forests (from) the FRL/FRL does not result in an overestimation of emissions.

## Have all of the most significant pools been included?

The carbon pools included in the FRL/FRE are above-ground biomass (AGB), below-ground biomass (BGB), deadwood (if non-tree), deadwood (only above ground) and litter. The soil organic matter pool, as well as the harvested wood products (HWP) pools were not included.

Costa Rica is currently working on a project to obtain better information in order to include deadwood in the below-ground pool. The AT commended Costa Rica for its efforts to address questions related to the inclusion of deadwood and with the aim of improving the accuracy of the reported information as part of the implementation of the Tiers approach. However, the impacts are likely to be insignificant (para. 27 of the TAR).

With regard to the harvested wood products pool, the AT requested clarification on the reasons for the omission of this pool. In response, Costa Rica noted that the rationale was based on the consideration that insufficient Tier 2 data were available to estimate emissions from the HWP pool and that the domestic carbon stock could be included using the default emission factors contained in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas


---
**Page 142**
---

### Inventory (2006 IPCC Guidelines)

The AF considered the treatment of emissions from soil organic carbon (i.e., the inclusion of this pool or the provision of more information justifying its omission) as a basis for future technical improvement of the FREL/FRL (para. 28 of the TAE).

In its original FREL/FRL submission, Costa Rica estimated emissions from land use change and forestry (LUCF) assuming that the same wood fraction of HWP does not emit GHG. This is inconsistent with methods in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines and is also not consistent with Costa Rica’s national inventory system.

In regard from the AF, Costa Rica excluded the HWP carbon pool in its modified FREL (para. 29 of the TAE). In its final proposal, Costa Rica noted that it would consider including information on FREL submissions in the light of the potential inclusion of additional land use change and forestry activities related to forest degradation and sustainable management of forests.

### Have all gases that are a significant source of emissions been included?

The AF considered that greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from deforestation and related emissions of methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) were included in the national GHG inventory. However, emissions from cropland and grassland that occurred in the period 1986–1996, and were excluded in the post-1996 portion of the basis for the first commitment period, are not included with those of the first LULUCF activity after 1996. This is inconsistent with methods used as its national GHG inventory. In response to a question on this issue from the AF, Costa Rica noted that the national GHG inventory includes GHG emissions from biomass burning in forest land resources and other land uses occurring before 1996. These emissions were not included in the FREL because they were not considered significant sources of emissions. The modified FREL includes GHG emissions from biomass burning pre-1996, although the national GHG inventory does not include this information given


---
**Page 143**
---

that only 2005, 2010 and 2012 have been recalculated for the agriculture, forestry and other land-use sector. Costa Rica also explained that recalculation for the inventories for 1990, 1995 and 2000 is a planned initiative. It was confirmed that, for its pre-1990 inventories (i.e., 1989 and 1995), the methodology for forest conversion will be kept consistent. During the assessment visit, Costa Rica also explained that it has not yet finalized data in the period 1998–2011 for secondary forests and tree plantations, as reported by the country in its second national communication because these forests are considered unmanaged, and it is assumed that human activities will not lead to land-use change. The AT noted that even if humans burning does not incur land-use change, not accounting for this for these issues may result in an overestimation of forest carbon stocks in secondary forest areas. The AT considered that the treatment of CERs and N2O emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (and the provision of more information justifying its omission) as an area for further discussion with Costa Rica, particularly with future GHG emissions inventories. In its funding proposal, Costa Rica noted that it will provide additional information on its GEL submissions in the light of the potential inclusion of additional N2O/CH4/CO2 emission factors related to forest degradation and sustainable management of forests

#### (vi) How many years are there between the last year of the FREL period and the year corresponding to the results being proposed for payment?

There are five years between the last year of the FREL period (i.e., 2009) and the year corresponding to the results being proposed for payment (i.e., 2014).

#### (vii) Has the country provided information on aggregate uncertainties, taking into consideration national capabilities and circumstances?

Costa Rica provided aggregate uncertainty estimates of the results, amounting to ±3.5 per cent. The uncertainty was calculated by combining the individual project-level uncertainties using a combination of uncertainties was done through approach 2 of the 2008 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories. The uncertainties were reported in terms of 90 per cent confidence intervals.

#### (viii) Has information been provided on payments that have been (or are expected to be) received from other national or subnational sources for areas during the period for which a country is proposing to receive payments from GCF?

And has any of these amounts been deducted from the total volume paid by other sources having been excluded from the total volume offered to GCF?

The funding proposal acknowledges that other payments have been (or are expected to be) received from other national or subnational sources for emission reduction/forestation for the same period and area as which Costa Rica is applying for payments from GCF, namely a clean development mechanism project under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change Agreement with the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility Carbon Fund (FCPF CF) and two REDD-plus projects under the World Bank's Forest Carbon Partnership Program. Moreover, the funding proposal indicates that, in the REDD-plus projects, payments are made to the communities where the REDD-plus project is being implemented. There is a very small volume allocated for REDD-plus projects in Costa Rica compared with the volume of many REDD-plus projects. This does not have a significant impact on the size of this GCF proposal (in 2014 it was 5924 ± 367 tCO₂ and in 2015, which is the year for which payments are proposed to be made, has been deducted from the offered volume for 2014 and 2015 for which payments have already been received). In view of the small amount of results/carbon credits from these projects, it may be considered that there will not be any significant impact on the GCF proposal.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP 144)

## Proposal name: Costa Rica REDD-plus Results-Based Payments for 2014 and 2015

### Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Country/ies):
Costa Rica

### Project/programme size:
Medium

---

### Impact potential

UNDP fully concurs with the ITAP assessment that the impact potential is high. UNDP agrees with this conclusion and notes that Costa Rica has proven to have successful policies in place and maintain the trend of reducing deforestation and increasing forest cover in the last 20 years, with a combination of effective forest law implementation and the implantation of the PES program. Furthermore, the results presented show that the average annual rate of reduction of these emissions reductions in the years following the results period presented. On the contrary, the average annual rate of increase was 1% per year for the same period over the period 2016-2018, which provides strong evidence of a minimal risk of reversals.

### Paradigm shift potential

UNDP fully concurs with the ITAP assessment that the paradigm shift potential is high. UNDP fully supports the observation that Costa Rica’s early implementation of policies and measures to reduce deforestation has led to a significant reduction in emissions, resulting in a shift of reducing deforestation. Furthermore, the trend has been to continue in this pathway with a combination of effective forest law implementation and the implantation of the PES program while enhancing community and biodiversity co-benefits and contributing to a post-COVID19 green recovery.

### Sustainable development potential

UNDP fully concurs with the ITAP assessment that the sustainable development potential is high. UNDP supports the observation that Costa Rica is showing the world that preserving 60% of the forests in benefit to their sustainable pathway and that they can ensure a long-term essential services to support their green economy.

### Needs of the recipient

UNDP agrees with the ITAP assessment rating of medium for the needs of the recipient. FORAFI/O’s budget, currently accommodates only 42% of approved. The GCF resources will serve to scale the program and to strengthen this PES scheme. The PES will be an important mechanism to channel GCF funds directly into empowered groups to support a green COVID-19 recovery phase


---
**Page 156**
---

# Country ownership

UNDP fully concurs with the ITAP assessment that country ownership is high. The project is fully aligned with Costa Rica’s climate change objective of becoming a Carbon Neutral economy by 2050 and the 2019-2023 National Development Plan, as well as its post-2020 commitments under the Paris Agreement, supporting the implementation of the NDC.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

UNDP fully concurs with the ITAP assessment that efficiency and effectiveness of the proposal is high. In both short and medium term, GCF resources are critical to support the PES scheme. In the long run UNDP fully concurs with the statement that the country will need to look for additional sources of financing in order to ensure the sustainability of the project and finance the PES scheme. UNDP will provide technical advice to Costa Rica as it faces this endeavor.

# Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

As noted by ITAP, the PES project in Costa Rica is well established and has been a source of inspiration of many PES projects in the world. This mechanism can now play a critical role in supporting a green COVID-19 recovery phase by allowing the transfer of needed cash resources to communities that are most vulnerable and have historically been excluded from Indigenous Peoples given that the PES is one of the main sources of governmental cash transfers.

Over the medium term, UNDP will continue to support Costa Rica and explore further ideas to diversify the financial options to ensure the NDC and the REDD+ targets. The project has the potential to evolve and to scale innovations with innovative policies and mechanisms that could be replicated in many other countries currently engaged in PES pilot around the world.

For all other uses, send an email to: Publishing@worldbank.org

### Acknowledgements

This document was prepared by a team of expert consultants (Andrea Gutiérrez Aguilar and Tania Aguilar) staff from the World Bank Secretariat in Costa Rica (María Elena Herrera, Elena Flores, and Héctor Acosta), and World Bank staff (Stavros Papageorgiou, Cynthia Flora, Tamara Bah, Katharina Siegmann, Patricia Kristjánsson and Elana Matulovich). The graphic design was carried out by Estudio Relativo. The team thanks the REDD+ Secretariat of Costa Rica for its support in the preparation of this document as well as the Climate Partnership Facility (CPF) and no donor countries for funding the report.

The Gender Action Plan of Costa Rica's National REDD+ Strategy is part of the efforts that the REDD+ Secretariat has been implementing to ensure gender equality and women’s empowerment in REDD+ processes. These processes are based on the Gender and REDD+ Roadmap developed by the REDD+ Secretariat in 2016. Through this publication, we aim at providing a snapshot of Costa Rica's current situation in terms of forests, gender and climate change mitigation, including this publication.

To obtain the information for this publication, several field visits were organized during 2018 and interviews were conducted with government officials, members of local communities and other relevant stakeholders; all information included in this report had the informed consent of their protagonists which was provided during the field visits.

The team gratefully acknowledges the contributions of women and men from local communities who shared their experiences as part of this publication.

# Executive Summary

## Key Messages

- This report summarizes the process for developing the Gender Action Plan (GAP) of the Costa Rica National REDD+ Strategy, the results found, and the actions proposed to address gender gaps and enhance opportunities differentiated by gender when implementing it.
- The Gender Action Plan of the National REDD+ Strategy is the country's first gender action plan on climate issues and marks a clear path for continuing work on gender and environment in Costa Rica.
- In Costa Rica there are about 12,598 women producers who own 106,564 hectares of land and forest. Women producers represent 37% of all farms and 8.1% of the total agricultural area belonging to natural persons in the country.

Many of the areas with a high percentage of farms owned by women producers coincide with areas which have a lower social development index, as well as with priority areas for conservation and sustainable management of forest landscape and ecosystems, and for the promotion of low-carbon production systems.
- There is therefore great potential to increase the participation of these women in sustainable productive landscape initiatives and other land-use related activities, which can contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions while contributing to the conservation and sustainable management of forest priority areas in the country.

However, rural Costa Rican women face a number of gender gaps related to the recognition, procedures and distribution in natural resource management that prevent them from fully participating.
- The gender analysis carried out as part of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) allowed for a better understanding of the reality of Costa Rican women and men and for obtaining data that show that there are gender-differentiated roles, gaps and opportunities in Costa Rica's rural areas.

Thus, the GAP proposes a range of activities for each of the National REDD+ Strategy Policies, Actions and Measures (PAMs) that can generate important rural development opportunities for a wide range of women, while reducing deforestation and forest degradation and increasing carbon stocks.


---
**Page 164**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The design of the GAP was based on a bottom-up participatory approach, which allowed for the proposal of concrete actions that reflect the reality of the country and the validation of women's ideas and contributions, as well as greater ownership of the GAP development process by the women and groups consulted.

Costa Rica shows great advances in the creation of public policies in the environmental sector that have resulted in a clear need towards the recovery of the country’s forest cover in recent decades. This is due to the country’s efforts to design early forest policies that allowed it to reduce emissions by the sector and maintain the vital functions of critical ecosystems, improving its resilience to climate change and providing access opportunities to key environmental and economic resources, especially in rural areas.

On the subject of gender, environmental policies show a positive evolution over time. The country has signed and ratified the main declarations and conventions to promote women’s rights; it has the National Women's Institute (INAMU). This has had a major impact on environmental policies, which have evolved from a gender-neutral approach to a gender-sensitive or responsive one.

Since 2016, the REDD+ Secretariat, made up of FONAFIFO and SINAC, has been preparing a Gender and REDD Road Map that concludes with the development of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) of the Costa Rica Strategy (EN-REDD+), in collaboration with gender experts, civil society organizations, indigenous groups, Afro-descendant communities, women and small rural producers. This report summarizes the process for developing the GAP, the results found, and the proposed actions to address gender gaps and enhance gender equality.

At the end of this document, the REDD+ Secretariat conducted the country’s first general analysis on forests and climate change, which included a review of the regulatory, institutional, academic and social framework related to gender and related to REDD+, complemented by field visits and participatory processes to identify gaps in opportunities, case studies and lessons learned.

Gender equality is an essential condition for sustainable development results at the national level and men in relation to forest management and in relation to obtaining quantitative and qualitative data on gender-differentiated roles, gaps and opportunities. Costa Rica remains face a number of gender gaps related to the recognition, procedures and distribution in natural


---
**Page 165**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### Recognition
- Women are not visible in the agricultural and environmental sector.
- Women have fewer farms, and these are smaller in size.
- Gender-specific contributions and knowledge related to forest conservation and management are not recognized.

### Procedures
- Women participate less frequently in forestry activities and projects because they have more care responsibilities.
- Social stereotypes limit women's participation in forestry activities and projects.
- Fewer women participate in decision-making processes related to natural resource management and the protection of forests.
- Women producers have less access to information and their farms receive less technical support and extension services.
- Many women are not included as project beneficiaries, with women doing technical work and extension work.
- Most environmental institutions have limited capacities to implement gender-sensitive or responsive initiatives.

### Distribution
- Women producers show higher poverty rates.
- Women producers have lower levels of financial support.
- The number of woman-owned farms, included in the PES has been decreasing in recent years.

At the same time, there is great potential to increase the participation of women from different regions of the country in sustainable production landscape initiatives as they are interested in a wide range of activities proposed by the National REDD+ Strategy. In Costa Rica there are about 12,598 women producers who own 106,563.6 hectares of agricultural land. This represents 15% of the farms and 8.1% of the total agricultural area belonging to national farmers. Women in Costa Rica are interested in activities such as: cocoa cultivation, plant nurseries, home garden improvement, collection of non-timber forest products (medicinal plants, seeds or species for construction) and the development of agroforestry systems. They also have a great interest in the use of solar panels to power homes allowing them to be part of the activities proposed in the National REDD+ Strategy.

The activities included in the GAP can generate significant rural development opportunities that generate resources and improve the livelihoods of a wide range of women while reducing deforestation and forest degradation and increasing carbon stocks. The gender analysis found that many of these areas have large percentage of the farms that belong to


---
**Page 166**
---

Annex XIII (c)

Gender Assessment and Action Plan

female producers coincide with areas with a lower social development index, as well as with priority areas for forest conservation and management, for forest landscape and ecosystem restoration, and for the promotion of low-carbon production systems. The analysis also found that many of the activities prioritized by women during the development of the Gender Assessment and Action Plan are included in the activities included in the Territorial Rural Development Plans of the country's rural territories.

The GAP is structured on the basis of the 6 Policies, Actions and Measures (PAMs) of the National REDD+ Strategy and is composed of 6 gender objectives (one for each PAM) and 20 expected results, together with the definition of specific actions for the achievement of results. The design of the GAP was based on a bottom-up participatory approach that encompasses a range of actions that encompass: (a) policy changes at the national level; (b) institutional strengthening; and (c) changes at the local level through gender-responsive forestry projects, which include women's participation in decision-making processes.

The design of the GAP was based on a bottom-up participatory approach. This made it possible to propose concrete actions that reflect the reality of the country and to validate the ideas generated by women and men from different sectors of society. The process of development of the GAP is the work and women and groups that were publicly consulted, turning into a proposal for concrete social and environmental transformation based on the needs and priorities identified in the Gender Assessment and Action Plan. This includes the sustainable management of Costa Rican forests.

This GAP reasserts Costa Rica's commitment to human rights and gender and marks a clear path for continuing work on gender and the environment in the country. The National REDD+ Strategy GAP is the country's first gender action plan on climate and an important step that contributes to the commitment made in its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC). Through the development of this GAP, Costa Rica becomes one of a few countries that have developed a Gender Action Plan for its REDD+ Strategy.

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### 2. INTERNATIONAL CONTEXT

The most relevant international agreements on gender equality that create an enabling setting for the project objectives are:

1- International references ratified by Costa Rica in relation to the human rights of women: the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) [1979], the Vienna Declaration [UN, 1993], and the Inter-American Convention against Impunity for Crimes Violating the Rights of Women [1994]. The “Convention of Belém do Pará” [1996], the Declaration and the Platform for Action of Beijing (UN, 1995), and the Declaration of the Millennium Development Goals (UN, 2000).

2- The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, in specific Goal 5 that calls for achieving gender equality and empowering all women and girls. It is recognized that empowering women and promoting gender equality is crucial to accelerating sustainable development. As well as ending all forms of discrimination against women and girls is not only a human right, but it also has a multiplier effect across all other development areas.

3- The 70+ mandates on gender equality and women empowerment under the UNFCCC, in specific those related to REDD+. Since COP13, in 2007, REDD+ decisions under the UNFCCC were adopted progressively, with subsequent decisions (guidance, rules, and modalities) that guide how REDD+ is designed and implemented today.

Several key MUD decisions informed the update of general response policy and actions on gender equality during COP16 in Cancún 2010. Parties adopted Decision 1/CP.16 [Paragraph 72] that “When developing and implementing their national strategies or action plans, to address, inter alia, drivers of deforestation and forest degradation, including the social, economic, environmental issues, gender considerations and the safeguards... ensuring the full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, inter alia, indigenous peoples and local communities.”


---
**Page 168**
---

Annex XIII (c)

Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Building on this guidance, the 2011 UNFCCC Durban Outcomes (Decision 12/CF.17, Paragraph 2) further guided countries that when providing information on how safeguards are addressed what is now commonly referred to as safeguard information systems (SIS), gender considerations should also be respected in this process.*

Additional human rights treaties also form the basis and rationale for utilizing a human rights-based approach (HRBA) and integrating gender equality and women's empowerment into climate change policies, plans, strategies, and actions. These decisions help provide an effective compliance framework, which illustrates the need and rationale for incorporating gender equality principles across the REDD+ thematic areas, including national actions plans/strategies and safeguards, among others.

4- UNFCCC Gender Action Plan (GAP)

At COP19 of the UNFCCC, the Parties approved the second GAP. The enhanced gender action plan sets out objectives and activities under five priority areas that aim to advance knowledge on gender equality in climate change policies, plans, strategies, and actions; mainstreaming in the implementation of the UNFCCC and the work of Parties, the secretariat, United Nations entities and all stakeholders at all levels, as well as women's full, equal and meaningful participation in this process.

Of the five priority areas, the present proposals acknowledge and respond to the mandates contained under "Priority Area D: gender-responsive implementation and means of implementation" with special attention to:

- B.3 Promote the deployment of gender-responsive technological solutions to address climate change, including strengthening, protecting and preserving local, indigenous and traditional knowledge and practices in different sectors and for implementing adaptation actions; and promote women's full participation and leadership in science, technology, research and development.
- B.5 Engage women’s groups and national women and gender institutions in the process of developing, implementing and updating climate policies, plans, strategies and action, as appropriate, at all levels.

#### 5- Secretary General Climate Summit from 2019 in relation to gender and climate change

In September of 2019, the office of the SG organized the climate summit. The summit's goal was to further climate action to reduce greenhouse gas emissions to prevent the mean global temperature from rising by more than 1.5 °C (2.7 °F) above preindustrial levels. Some of the key goals designed for this summit were to ensure a fair and just transition, gender-responsive jobs and clear air for better health, and protect the most vulnerable, as well as new initiatives on adaptation, agriculture and early warning systems that will protect 300 million additional people against the impacts of climate change.

Under the gender track signatures[1] countries commit to implementing climate actions that contribute to gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls. This commitment recognizes the differentiated impact of climate change by gender. Ensuring women's full participation in decision-making can make climate action more effective, contributing to increased ambition in all sectors. Four concrete commitments were proposed, four of them relevant to the present proposal:

1) Adopt and implement gender-responsive climate change action plans, policies or strategies.
2) Promote and enhance innovative tools that demonstrate and measure the transformative power of women's and girls' leadership in modifying patterns of consumption and production.
3) Support and promote initiatives that foster women's and girls' full participation and leadership in mitigation and adaptation measures, including in science, technology, research and development.

4) Support and promote initiatives that foster women's and girls' full participation and leadership in mitigation and adaptation measures, including in science, technology, research and development.

It is essential to point out that Costa Rica has played a leadership role in the development of the two GAPs under the UNFCCC and was one of the conveners of the gender track under the Gender Summit. Costa Rica is the lead Party convening the discussions on gender and climate change under the Beijing+25 process.

Therefore, the present proposal embraces and seeks to advance Costa Rica's commitments at the internationally agreed upon instruments and declarations.

## 3. National Regulatory Context

### Gender analysis of the gender equality policy framework

Costa Rica has a specific and robust regulatory framework to promote gender equality. In 1990, the Act for the Promotion of Social Equality for Women was approved. Article 1 of this law emphasizes that "It is the obligation of the State to promote and guarantee equal rights between men and women in the political, economic and cultural spheres" and reiterates their commitment to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The Social Equality for Women Act complements other laws like the Law Against Sexual Harassment in Employment and Teaching (1995), the Law Against Domestic Violence (1995), the Law Against Domestic Violence (1996) and the Alimony law (1996), among others. The following table summarizes several of the articles in the Law for the Promotion of Social Equality for Women and which are relevant to the REDD+ Strategy (Table 1).

| Article and Summary | Relevant to REDD+ Costa Rica |
|---|---|

All real estate property granted through social development programs must be registered in the name of both spouses, in the case of a married couple; in the name of the woman in the case of a common-law marriage or in the name of the beneficiary in any other case, in the name of the woman.

#### FUNAFITO and SINAC could establish a partnership with the INA to provide training for women to enable them to become entrepreneurs and business owners. This would be consistent with the goals of the Gender Action Plan for the REDD+ Strategy.

### In 1998, Costa Rica created the National Women's Institute (INAMU) by passing the Act establishing the National Women's Institute. The Act elevated the National Centre for the Development of Women and the Family to a decentralized institution with a wide range of functions and powers. The creation of INAMU raised the administrative status of the main national mechanism for the empowerment of women, which was created in 1985 as an autonomous public entity under public law with its own legal personality and assets. In addition, within this Institute, the rank of a woman Minister for the Status of Women was created, who


---
**Page 172**
---

Annex XIII (c)

Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The Executive Presidency of the Institute. The Act that created INAMU includes as its mandate the drafting of national policy on equality.

As part of its actions and duties, INAMU developed the National Policy for Gender Equality and Equity (PIEG) 2018-2030 and is currently developing the PIEG 2018-2030 Action Plan. The policy has four main lines of action, focusing on the culture of rights to equality, time distribution, access to productive resources, and wealth creation. These actions are based on the framework of compliance with Agenda 2030 and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), recently approved by Costa Rica. In particular, the theme on the distribution of wealth creates conditions for women's empowerment in sectors related to conservation, climate change, sustainable management of resources, and rural development.

The policy also aims at "promoting gender equality and women's empowerment as a means to allow the generation of wealth, as well as 'ensure responsible governance of ownership, because land, fisheries and forests are fundamental to the realization of human rights, food security, environmental sustainability, poverty reduction, social inclusion, public security, rural development and social and economic growth'". It is important to emphasize that several of the expected results mentioned in the policy are relevant to the National REDD+ Strategy. The Action Plan for the PIEG 2018-2030 includes a series of objectives, measures and actions that promote gender equality and women's empowerment in sectors related to conservation, climate change and sustainable management of resources.

Implementation of the REDD+ Strategy may contribute to achieving some of the expected results proposed in the PIEG 2018-2030 (Table 2). For example, a contribution to time distribution may be made if budgets for implementing proposed activities in the PAMs include a contribution to the cost of childcare. In this way, women can participate more fully and even to engage in employment opportunities that may arise from the implementation of the PAMs. The Strategy can also contribute to the distribution of wealth if measures are implemented to provide women with access to productive resources as part of the implementation of the PAMs. For example, there are currently several women who work as volunteers in the fire brigades in the Nicoya Peninsula, and the Strategy could contribute to their professionalization.

Finally, the Gender Action Plan can contribute to achieving some of the expected results proposed in the PIEG; some of these themes and expected results are summarized below.

The 2019-2022 National Development Plan is based on a gender rights and equality approach. The plan defines the human rights approach and mentions that the national objectives and strategic interventions will promote respect for the principles of equity, recognizing the attention to population groups that, due to their conditions, are subject to discrimination or exclusion.

The 2019-2022 National Development Plan is based on a gender rights and equality approach. The plan defines the human rights approach and mentions that the national objectives and strategic interventions will promote respect for the principles of equity, recognizing the attention to population groups that, due to their conditions, are subject to discrimination or exclusion.

The development and implementation of the Gender Action Plan for REDD+ contributes to the fulfillment of this strategic intervention. Costa Rica is a signatory and has ratified the main declarations and conventions to promote women's rights. Among these agreements are: the Convention on Civil and Political Rights; the Convention on the Political Rights of Women (approved in 1952 by the UN and ratified by Costa Rica in 1970); and the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) (ratified by Costa Rica in September 2001). ### Article 3

States Parties shall take in all fields, in particular social, economic and cultural fields, all necessary measures to promote full development and advancement of women with a view to achieving the purpose of guaranteeing them the exercise and enjoyment of human rights and fundamental freedoms on a basis of equality with men.

In identifying and implementing the concrete measures for promoting gender equality, States Parties should consider the full range of factors that may affect women's access considering the national and local social context, including their needs, capacities, and preferences differentiating by gender.

### Gender Analysis of the Environmental Regulatory Framework

Costa Rica has an environmental regulatory framework that guarantees its inhabitants the right to a healthy environment. Through the Constitution and through the application of the Organic Law on the Environment No. 7554, the State defends and preserves this right, in search of greater welfare for all inhabitants of the Republic. This law is considered a milestone in the environmental legislation for Costa Ricans, and having these rights has generated a significant change in the behavior and culture of Costa Ricans, since it has allowed them to take ownership of the conservation and sustainable management of resources.

The regulatory framework pays special attention to the participation of inhabitants in conservation and sustainable resource management activities (Table 4). The active participation of all social sectors is one of the main mandates of Costa Rican environmental regulations. In spite of the fact that the regulations do not specify the social sectors, this allows for an interpretation that considers women and relevant women's groups or associations.

### Table 4: Articles in Costa Rican Environmental Legislation That Focus on the Participation of Inhabitants in Conservation and Sustainable Resource Management Activities

| Organic Law on the Environment | Art. 57: The State and the municipalities shall promote the active and organized participation of the inhabitants of the Republic in decision-making processes and in actions aimed at protecting and improving the environment |
| --- | --- |
| Art. 60: Promote the active participation of all social sectors in the conservation |

### Forestry Law

On the subject of gender, environmental policies show a positive evolution over time. The policies of the 1980s, such as the Organic Law on the Environment and the Forestry Law, do not mention the gender approach; nevertheless, the policies and plans proposed in the last decade address gender considerations and recognize the importance of developing conservation and sustainable resource management activities with a gender approach. This path is exemplified by the Biodiversity Law, which includes gender as an essential principle.

The National Biodiversity Strategy is developed based on a rights and gender approach and includes gender as a principle and as part of its goals and indicators. The following summarizes this transition in a timeline and includes the relevant legislation.

Figure 1: Gender timeline of biodiversity-related policies

| Year | Policy | Principle | Gender Sensitive |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1990 | Biodiversity Law | Included as a General Principle | Neutral |
| 2015 | National Biodiversity Policy | Recognizes that increasing inequalities and persistent conditions of poverty in particular for women-headed households, marginal communities, and indigenous peoples are an obstacle to citizen commitment about the value of biodiversity and its services from a gender perspective. | Gender Sensitive |

Recognizes the contributions to conservation made by local communities and indigenous peoples and promotes their effective governance, favoring those groups that are more socially, economically, or culturally vulnerable than others in society.

## Forestry and REDD+ Policy Framework

Forest policies evolve from being gender-blind to becoming gender-sensitive, although the Forestry Law does not mention gender. Some of its articles can be interpreted to include the gender dimension. The National Foresty Development Plan recognizes that one of the functions of the National Forestry Office (ONF) is to promote the constitution and strengthening of associations and organized groups for the development of the forestry sector, with emphasis on women farmers and small producers so that they can organize themselves and take advantage of market and industrial forest products. These recommendations are also applicable in the context of the National Foresty Development Plan, which assigns the National Forestry Office the function to encourage programs aimed at rural communities in order to incorporate small landowners into reforestation programs.

## National REDD+ Strategy Costa Rica

* **Affirms its commitment to Decision LCF/16 and highlights parallel processes for the development and implementation to address gender considerations when developing and implementing their national strategy on climate change.**
* **Recognizes women as a marginalized group**

#### Gender-sensitive

## National REDD+ Implementation Plan Strategy Costa Rica

#### Gender-sensitive

## National REDD+ Benefit-sharing Plan Strategy Costa Rica

* **Mentions the description of monetary and non-monetary benefits which must be appropriate from the gender point of view**

#### Gender-sensitive

The National Forestry Development Plan 2011-2020 is the first forestry policy to recognize gender as a cross-cutting issue. The Plan recognizes that to ensure the conservation of biological diversity of forest lands (and their use), as well as cultural diversity and respect for the rights of indigenous peoples, it is necessary to include women in the participation of inhabitants in the decision-making. Although this is an important step in legislation, it is necessary to see it as a starting point, since the gender dimension must include actions beyond the forestry sector.

In 2011, Costa Rica began to integrate the gender approach into the Readiness phase of REDD+ (the FONAFIFO) with the support of UCN organized the first awareness-raising workshop on gender and forests. The workshop was attended by more than 20% of the institution's staff and during this event lessons learned, challenges, priority actions and the establishment of a work plan were discussed. In addition to the importance of gender recognition and sensitization, gender issues were discussed in several of the processes of Costa Rica's preparation process. For example, the workshop for the self-evaluation of the Relevant Stakeholders identifies that addressing the gender issues was a necessity.

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

During the process carried out at the national level with the Relevant Stakeholders for the Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA), it is recognized that the gender approach happens thanks to the active engagement of indigenous women's organizations such as CONADEP, ACOMUNITAS, ADIBITRIBI, ADIXEKOLODI, RIBICA, and others on REDD+ issues. However, the SESA document also mentions that the Relevant Stakeholders expressed their concern that the lack of mainstreaming of the issue will lead to the exclusion of women from decision-making processes unless there are a number of limitations to incorporating the issue into the country's REDD+ program.

In 2016, the Costa Rica Gender and REDD+ Road Map was prepared as part of the actions carried out by FONAFIFO to continue mainstreaming gender issues into REDD+ processes. The roadmap was developed through a multi-stakeholder participatory process when the workshop participants were asked to identify four main lines of action and 10 gender considerations relevant to the REDD+ process in Costa Rica.

The Roadmap defines 4 main lines of action and 10 gender considerations relevant to the REDD+ process in Costa Rica. To develop the roadmap, the workshop included a series of dynamics that allowed the identification of gender considerations relevant to the national REDD+ program based on the country context, gender inequalities, the situation of women in Costa Rica, and the experiences of indigenous women's organizations.

The Roadmap was developed through a participatory process with 32 participants. Ten gender considerations were identified, which were grouped into four key areas (Figure 3). The Road Map considered these four areas as general objectives that could be addressed by the national REDD+ program in terms of results, actions and organizations that could lead these actions. This Roadmap was used as a guide to propose the process for developing the Gender Action Plan for the National REDD+ Strategy.

The REDD+ Strategy reaffirms its commitment to Decision 1/COP16 and highlights paragraph 72, which calls on REDD+ countries to adopt gender considerations when developing and implementing their national strategies or action plans.

In order to comply with this mandate, Costa Rica defines the actions to develop the Gender Action Plan in the Emission Reduction Program to the Carbon Fund of the FCPF. Specifically, the Plan proposes that a gender strategy be developed during the first phase of preparation, along with other relevant groups in the National REDD+ Strategy (Action 6.4). With respect to gender, the Plan mentions that a gender strategy is planned to be developed during the second phase of preparation, in order to ensure that both the strategy and the ERP incorporate this dimension.

The following actions are proposed as part of the action plan to implement this action:

- Develop a strategy to mainstream the gender approach and the incorporation of other relevant groups in the National REDD+ Strategy based on the preliminary studies carried out (Activity 6.4.1)
- Develop information, training, outreach and financing activities to promote the participation of women in REDD+ actions (Activity 6.4.2)

Costa Rica's National REDD+ Strategy Implementation Plan and Benefit-sharing Plan are


---
**Page 185**
---

beginning to include specific actions to address gender considerations in the enabling conditions and benefit-sharing associated with the REDD+ Strategy. The case of the Implementation Plan is recognized as an enabling condition to carry out and operationalize a sub-strategy for group and gender participation. The Benefit-sharing Plan is more comprehensive, including indicators that measure equity and transparency in the design of the plan to ensure equitable distribution of monetary benefits among different stakeholders with rights. To assess whether the distribution is equitable, one of the indicators proposed is to include a description of monetary and non-monetary benefits, which should be culturally gender-appropriate.

NAMAs related to REDD+ address some gender considerations in the case of NAMA Livestock, it explicitly addresses the gender issue by recognizing the contribution that women make to productive activities on livestock farms. A recent study on dairy farms shows that a high percentage of women participate in the implementation of sustainable production practices identified as a priority in NAMA. Although the percentage of women who make decisions about the use of resources is lower than men, they are involved in decision-making processes related to the management and care of animals. In most cases, women have significant participation in decision-making. The establishment of fodder banks is noteworthy, where the percentage of women who decide on this initiative is higher than that of men. Women also participate more actively in the planting of trees in pastures, where the percentage of women participating and making decisions is the same. In addition, it includes as part of its MRY to measure and monitor gender equality, which is a key element for the implementation of NAMA Climate Intelligent Agriculture, as well as the social component in livestock families, including gender equity. NAMA mentions that its implementing agency (Fundación para el Desarrollo Rural) has committed to projects on sustainable agriculture, gender equity and energy efficiency, among others.

Gender Analysis of the Climate Change Policy Framework

Since 2014, Costa Rica has been leading the gender negotiations within the UNFCCC and is one of the managers of the Gender Action Plan for the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. This commitment has led to a change in policies and strategies related to climate change. For example, initially the national policies and communications related to climate change (the National Policy on Climate Change and the National Strategy on Climate Change), do not recognize or mention gender considerations. In the case of the communications, they only present data disaggregated by gender in terms of population or life expectancy data and the third National

The Regional Climate Change Strategy was developed by SIGA in 2010 and represents a significant change in climate policy by identifying and addressing gender considerations in a comprehensive manner. This change is due to the fact that the Strategy was developed under the framework of the Inter-American Mechanism for Gender Integration (IMCA) implemented the consultation process on gender and climate change, which aimed to compile the knowledge of women who are already experiencing climate change in their daily lives.

The Strategy adopts many of the ideas, concerns, and recommendations that were proposed by Central American women. For example, the principles of the ERCC are based on: (a) The recognition of the importance of gender equality as a cross-cutting theme; (b) Coherence of governance and solidarity policies; equity, gender equality and social justice; (c) Recognition that the region's vulnerability to climate change is unevenly distributed among women of African descent, rural and urban women, children and the elderly, and families living in poverty. The following (Table 6) summarizes the adaptation and mitigation actions identified in the Strategy that address gender considerations and are relevant to the REDD+ Strategy.

recommendations for actions to be considered, while in the agricultural sector, it includes a case study prepared by INAMU that describes women as agents of change. This case study provides data on:

- The time women invest in production: Women invest a very significant number of hours daily in domestic chores and farm work combined, and it is estimated around 50% of them spend more than four hours a day on agricultural and livestock tasks.

The Nationally Determined Predicted Contribution (NDC) reaffirms that climate policies, and the actions to be taken in Costa Rica, will be built on a framework that is consistent with universal principles of human rights and gender equity. This commitment reflects a significant change in Costa Rica's climate agenda that began four years ago when Costa Rica presented its rights and gender agenda at the international level in the climate change negotiations.

Costa Rica consolidates a regulatory framework related to gender-responsive adaptation based on human rights, by presenting its National Policy on Adaptation to Climate Change in 2018. The main points relate to adaptation risk management have a common framework that is consistent with universal principles of human rights and gender equity (see Text Box I). It should be noted that even the Strategy and Action Plan for the Adaptation of Costa Rica's Biodiversity Subject to Climate Change addresses gender considerations by recognizing that as its objective, it seeks to ensure that women are included in an efficient implementation of the strategy since, to the extent that groups of society can identify problems, solutions, necessary resources and implementation mechanisms, they more effectively contribute to the achievement of objectives set out in the short- and long-term. The definitions of these approaches show a robust and holistic conceptual framework that takes into consideration ideas proposed Agenda 2030 and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction. These are also the patterns found in most recent and innovative gender publications and analyses. These definitions can serve as a guide for future climate and environmental policies.
