# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### Test box 1: Law and Gender Approaches Included in Risk Management and Adaptation Policies

#### National Risk Management Policy 2010-2030

**Human Rights Approach:** Risk management with a human rights-based approach is a policy framework that supports human development. From a regulatory point of view, it is based on international standards on the subject of human rights. It aims at promoting conditions of security and sustainability under which the full enjoyment of human rights can be realized. The human rights-based approach to risk management seeks to identify the root causes of problems and development vulnerability to disasters, in order to correct the discriminatory practices and unfair distribution of resources that are often responsible for these vulnerabilities. This approach is based on a gender perspective, with risk management incorporated in development strategies and policies contributes to promote the sustainability of development projects and programs. The human rights-based approach is particularly relevant especially oriented to the most vulnerable groups, to participate in the preparation of policies and to hold accountability for their implementation obligation to the law as part of a system of rights and duties that is anchored in the concepts of international law.

**The Gender Equity Approach:** The application of the present National Risk Management Policy adapts the concept of gender equity. This approach recognizes the different needs, capacities, and vulnerabilities of men and women that people possess according to their gender. Gender characteristics imply differential reasons for more or less vulnerability to disasters. Women are often more vulnerable than men because they may be identified with respect to the condition of women, tending towards equality, recognizing the characteristics of women as a social group, and taking into account the different needs, capacities, and vulnerabilities of men and women in terms of their biological dimension and with respect to the demands of the patriarchal model. Under a new approach to maximizing the potential for gender equity, the human rights-based approach is not only relevant because it does not exclude the recognition that certain circumstances of social differentiation, different from the gender condition, may also be present as state of strength, and weaknesses that in the human rights perspective also deserve attention.

#### National Policy on Adaptation to Climate Change, Costa Rica

**Human Rights Approach:** The Convention on Human Rights recognizes that human rights are affected by human rights from health threats to damage on properties, livelihoods and even culture. Enhancing solidarity through affirmative action requires a gender perspective in the implementation of the National Plan on Adaptation to Climate Change. This approach, however, it can be included from the human rights approach to impact all the vulnerabilities that affect women and men differently. The Convention on Human Rights recognizes that women and men are affected by climate change in different ways due to their social or economic condition, such as indigenous peoples, must be considered.

**The Gender Equity Approach:** Gender equity is an inherent approach for the fulfillment of human rights. The differences between men and women in terms of their biological dimension and with respect to the demands of the patriarchal model are recognized by the Convention on Human Rights. Women and current inequalities and discrimination are expected to increase. The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) recognizes that women have a right to participate in the implementation of the National Plan on Adaptation to Climate Change will consider the interrelationship between climate change, gender equality, and human rights. This approach is particularly relevant for collectives, in particular those who suffer some disability or are senior female citizens, girls or adolescents among others. The Convention on Human Rights recognizes that women and men have different needs and vulnerabilities with respect to the effects of climate change. It also recognizes that there may be differences between men and women in order to face the adverse effects of the phenomenon, facilitating opportunities to ensure equal access to resources and services for both sexes. This approach is particularly relevant for collectives generate processes that promote and guarantee appropriate and make visible the management by women in adaptation in all areas: local, business, institutional, political, economic, etc.

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The National Policy on Adaptation to Climate Change proposes gender-responsive themes, guidelines, and indicators that can be taken into consideration when implementing the REDD+ Strategy. Several of the PAMs included in the REDD+ Strategy contribute to reducing emissions while increasing resilience and adaptive capacity. The Gender Action Plan represents a set of actions, including policies, strategies, programs, projects, and measures with the gender guidelines included in the National Adaptation Plan (Table 7).

### Table 7: Gender themes included in the National Adaptation Plan

- **Theme 1:** The development of local and institutional capacities for adaptation will focus on the most vulnerable groups, communities, groups and individuals, in accordance with human rights and gender equality approaches.
- **Theme 2:** Adaptation to climate change must be the starting point for land use planning. In this effort, it will consider human rights, gender equality and integrated adaptation approaches, and will enable adaptation measures that are consistent with human rights and gender equality.
- **Theme 3:** To provide public and private financial resources, from both existing and new sources, that effectively enable the implementation of adaptation measures and reduce losses and damages for vulnerable populations due to the adverse impacts of climate change. This should be done in a transparent and verified manner, taking into account the needs of women and men, especially vulnerable populations, particularly women, in the local economy through productive climate-resilient investments.
- **Theme 4:** Evaluation means are the contribution of indicators for monitoring the policy and its Action Plan, is a priority to ensure fair and equitable access.

### Indicators Theme 1: Technology platforms for the collection of gender-disaggregated data on adaptation measures and traditional knowledge.

The Ministry of Agriculture (MAG) is developing a Gender and Climate Change Strategy for the Agricultural Extension Service in 2015. To develop the strategy, MAG received support from the Climate Action Program, Low Emission Development Model Niedrigemissionsland, Costa Rica (NEL) and the German Development Cooperation Agency (GIZ). A situational diagnosis was carried out in three regional directorates of MAG’s Agricultural Extension Service, with the participation of the Gender Coordination Center. An analysis was made of the adaptation and mitigation measures that could be implemented.

### Gender analysis of the policy framework on Indigenous Peoples

In 2018, Costa Rica signed the Decree "General Mechanism for Consultation with Indigenous Peoples", and one of its principles is gender equality. The mechanism emphasizes the active participation of women in all stages of consultation as well as in the integration of representative and decision-making bodies included in this decree, the active, effective and equal participation of indigenous women must be guaranteed, as an indispensable condition to ensure the effectiveness of the mechanisms for their full participation, which motivated the General Mechanism for Consultation. The indigenous people must ensure that at least 50 per cent of indigenous women are appointed to their territorial councils.

During the REDD consultation process conducted by the Indigenous Peoples, women from all Indigenous Peoples in the country participated, representing the majority of the 24 Indigenous territories. Women expressed various concerns and ideas during these consultations of the mechanisms. This methodology was agreed upon in the decree for the Consultation with Indigenous Territories and Communities. The decree also states that "affirmative actions will be included in order to guarantee the effective participation of women, youth, people with disabilities and the elderly." Some of the actions proposed during this consultation were: • Establish an equitable distribution of IPEs benefits among indigenous peoples, considering their investment in community works, social support or individual benefits. • Establish organic agriculture and crops that are adapted to heat and droughts in agroforestry systems as a model of IPEs. • Integrate the participation of Indigenous peoples that can be considered as carbon producers into IPEs modalities.

* Promote participation and respect for the contributions of men, women and youth to be given by the communities.
* Train indigenous men and women to monitor protected areas, forests, rivers and mountains.
* Define a demand in the short-term, an internal protocol of open participation which includes a mechanism of evaluation and control of the processes of participation with the indigenous communities, which is evaluated by representatives from the communities (men, women, youth, children, senior citizens, etc.) and which works in coordination with the IDAs (Indigenous Development Associations); covering all the aspects necessary to guarantee benefits for future generations.

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### Text box 2: Viewing the REDD+ issue from the perspective of ACOMUITA

During the REDD+ process, the 24 territories convened to discuss the 5 special themes which were discussed by the IGAs of the territories. Indigenous women have expressed that they want their voices heard and that they are concerned about the lack of attention given to the rights of indigenous women that are binding on national policy and that this political approach should be supported by the IGAs.

To this end, the vision of indigenous women must be characterized by systematizing their cosmogonic vision and making the Sva Jaa operational within the national policies by defining strategic lines. 

For the 5 special themes, the gender theme should be displayed as follows:

1) **Indigenous PES**: PES designs focused on the valuation of traditional forms as Carbon filters and forest mediators. A PES that integrates women's farms is visualized; as cocoa farms have a comprehensive approach: water, medicine, water, etc. Currently, there are no studies or projects in this area. It is important to clarify that these efforts are not intended to compete with the ADI but rather complement it.

2) **Land tenure**: It is important to implement a safeguard for indigenous women and their matrilineal legacy as a mechanism for land sanitation and legal security.

3) **Forest management**: The Sva Jaa should be seen as an environmental policy to be applied in the indigenous territories.

4) **Protected areas**: The Sva Jaa could be seen as a regulatory mechanism also applicable to protected areas. It is important that it is women who define this, so it is very important to incorporate them.

5) **Monitoring and evaluation**: A participatory cultural approach for the participation and monitoring from the viewpoint of indigenous women as guardians and protectors of the forest. We would like to see a holistic vision where the collective good is promoted.

### Gender analysis of the policy framework for rural development

Equity and inclusion of the population in territorial rural development are recognized as one of the main themes in rural development policies and plans. The State Policy for Costa Rica's Rural Territorial Development (PEDRT) emphasizes that Act No. 9036 modified the institutional framework to include gender equality, which is a fundamental principle to incorporate a vision of equity and equality and to encourage the active participation of historically invisible groups. As for the State Policy for the Agroindustry Sector and Rural


---
**Page 195**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Development of Costa Rica 2010-2021: It contains inclusion with equity as one of its principles, which is defined as the broad access of people and social groups to resources and services without distinction of ethnicity, gender and creed. Furthermore, this state policy recognizes innovation and technological development as mechanisms to compensate for the lack of natural resources.

The main rural development policy for Costa Rica, the PEDRT, is gender-responsive as it recognizes women as a priority group for attention and addresses gender considerations in its principles as well as in its actions and indicators. In the PEDRT, women are recognized as one of the social groups that have been made invisible; so the policy tries to recognize women as an active subject (at the local level) and as active members of social organizations and associations. The PEDRT includes gender as one of its focuses and mentions that it "proposes affirmative actions aimed at promoting the empowerment of women, in order to improve their participation and incidence capacity of people, particularly women". In addition, its theme 2 focuses on Equity and inclusion of the Population in Territorial Rural Development and states that one of its strategic objectives is to "promote gender equality and the empowerment of women so that seeks to promote the integration and participation of the population in the management of its own development, including differentiated actions towards traditionally excluded groups such as indigenous people and Afro-descendants" and includes a gender indicator on the percentage of women agricultural producers.

The Central American Strategy for Territorial Rural Development 2010-2019 (ECADERT) of the Central American Integration System (SICA) develops the gender issue from a holistic and robust perspective, which demonstrates a high level of technical and practical knowledge. The ECADERT is considered to be one of the most important documents in this country and as a strategy it is one of the documents that best addresses the gender issue. ECADERT develops the gender issue from a holistic and robust perspective, which demonstrates a high level of technical and practical knowledge. It recognizes that "Territorial Rural Development raises the need to correct deep inequalities in gender relations" and that "the empowerment of women is vital, not only to improve socio-economic conditions but also for the construction of a new model of citizenship democratic citizenship". Furthermore, ECADERT proposes a novel vision on how to develop gender-responsive processes in the region, since it proposes that the generation of new productive activities and the development of new technologies and services through rural activities could lead to the transformation of gender relations traditionally established in the field. The strategy defines the gender perspective as the will to build public, governmental and civil policies that intervene in an unequal social situation and promote


---
**Page 196**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

the implementation of measures that allow men and women more equitable access to education, politics, the labor market and credit; among others; and recognizes that from the perspective of rural development, the gender perspective must influence institutional development to include the creation of regulations, laws, entities and organizations that respond to the needs of both men and women understand and transform the world, natural resources and their culture.

ECADERT highlights that rural women, in particular, play strategic roles in managing key resources such as water and land for their productive and reproductive responsibilities, but have limitations in access to and control of these resources. ECADERT addresses in detail issues related to the conservation and sustainable management of natural resources and mentions various lines of action that could be harmonized with the PANS of the REDD+ Strategy in Costa Rica.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Region | Territory | Actions
### Huerta North
- Expand access to credit on the basis of equal rights and opportunities, applying the principles of gender equity.
- Create productive projects such as the planting and processing of fruit trees, beekeeping, animal husbandry, forestry activities, tourism, with available markets and products for sale. This will allow women to participate in them to provide economic income to their homes.
- Programmes aimed at improving the environmental conditions of the territory in a sustainable manner, stimulating productive agricultural activities and favoring new sources of employment, especially for women, who are often employed on an equal basis.

### San Carlos, Peñas Blancas and Rio Grande
- Strengthen the role of Women's Land Trusts and Sanctuary Networks of Rural Women.
- Encourage actions that promote women's group organization in benefit of their rights. Strengthening of women's groups through training and capacity building manner. Train organized groups according to their needs.
- Promote the development of productive projects, expand the coverage of programs aimed at women, especially for initiatives and projects. Provide training in productive projects.

### Bruna
- Encourage actions that promote women's group organization in benefit of their rights. Strengthening of women's groups through training and capacity building manner. Train organized groups according to their needs.
- Promote the development of productive projects, expand the coverage of programs aimed at women, especially for initiatives and projects. Provide training in productive projects.

### Osa, Golfito, Corredores
- Coordinate technical and methodological training that strengthens the potential and entrepreneurship of women and youth. This will allow them to participate more effectively lead by the youth and women of the Territory.
- To insert the groups of women in the development of the Caminos de Osa to develop Communitarian Rural Tourism, products with identity, seal of Local Tourism (organic products).

### Chorotega
- Activities led by women's groups: land purchase, nursery and fruit and vegetable collection point.

### Limón and Marina
- Actions aimed at fostering protection, reforestation as well as sustainable production of forest resources. This will allow the improvement of living conditions for women in the community.

### Central Pacific
- Actions aimed at fostering protection, reforestation as well as sustainable production of forest resources. This will allow the improvement of living conditions for women and people with disabilities.

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### Institutional Analysis

To implement gender-responsive environmental initiatives, three enabling conditions are necessary: a) institutional capacities, political will and support platforms; b) organized women's groups working in forest systems; and c) gender experts with technical capacities to support the design, implementation and monitoring of gender-responsive activities. These enabling conditions are described below for Costa Rica.

## Institutional Capacities

One of the main activities of the gender analysis was the interviews with key actors from national governmental institutions, NGOs and Relevant Stakeholders. Each of these organizations has a gender focal point or focal points who were interviewed. The following sections summarize the institutional capacities that were discussed during the semi-structured interviews. The sections mention whether the institution or organization has a gender focal point (FP) or not.

If an organization has received training on gender and environment, and whether it has implemented gender-sensitive or responsive environmental projects. If a section includes a green checkmark (√), it means that the institution does have those capacities; if it includes a red × (X) it means that the institution does not have those capacities; and if it includes both a green √ and a red × (X) it means that the institution has some capacities, but there are some aspects that could be strengthened.

### Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINAE)

#### Gender Policy:
Many environmental policies do not include specific mandates on gender, but their mandates on social equity and inclusive participation can be interpreted to encompass the gender dimension. From the institutional point of view, Decree No. 2734-MINAE was published in 1998. To institutionalize gender equality in the Ministry, Decree No. 506-MINAE was issued in 1999, establishing the Office of Gender and Environment at MINAE. However, these decrees were not operationalized in the last government administrations. MINAE is currently developing a gender policy draft.

#### Gender and Environment Experts:
The gender focal point is not operating and the Ministry does not have a gender focal point. MINAE is currently defining the establishment of the Gender and Environment Network as


---
**Page 201**

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Annex XIII (c)

### Part of the functions of the Secretariat of Environmental Planning (SEPLASA) with the support of the Vice Ministry of Natural Resources.

#### Gender-sensitive staff:
The issue of gender and the environment has not been a priority for the last government administrations, so the institution has been losing its technical capacity to address the issue of gender. Currently, there is no person within the institution who has the capacity to address the issue of gender in environmental planning or policy making.

#### Capacity Building on Gender and Environment:
In the 1990s, MINAE was supported by the IUCN Global Gender Office. During this period, training sessions were held on different environmental topics and their articulation with gender. MINAE has not continued these trainings.

### Gender-Sensitive or Gender-Responsive Projects:
MINAE does not have a clear agenda on gender and environment, and its portfolio of initiatives does not include gender-sensitive or responsive projects.

## National Fund for Forestry Financing (FONAFIFO)

### Gender Policy: ✗/✓
The institution does not have a gender policy. However, the institution has implemented affirmative actions to increase women's participation in the Payment for Environmental Services Program. This measure began to be implemented at the beginning of 2006 as part of the Economic and Social Development Plan (PDES). The PES program was launched in 1998, and by that year, 230 women were incorporated into the program. Currently, the number of women participating is not known.

### Expertise on Gender and Environment: ✗/✓
The institution does not have a gender focal point, but the staff of the REDD+ Secretariat has increased its interest and capacities in issues related to social equity and gender. In addition, several of the members of the REDD+ Secretariat have extensive experience in gender, social equity and Indigenous Peoples' issues.

### Gender-Sensitive Staff: ✗/✓
The institution does not have a gender focal point, but the staff of the REDD+ Secretariat has increased its interest and capacities in issues related to social equity and gender. In addition, several of the members of the REDD+ Secretariat have extensive experience in gender, social equity and Indigenous Peoples' issues.


---
**Page 202**
---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Most of the institution’s staff considers the issue of gender to be important, but there are many doubts about how to incorporate a gender perspective into their projects or activities and in some cases the added value of developing gender-sensitive or responsive initiatives is not perceived. It should be noted that the institution has not yet appointed the director of the REDD+ Secretariat understand and support addressing the issue of gender.

## Capacity-Building on Gender and Environment

In 2013, FONAFIFO in collaboration with WWF organized the first awareness workshop on gender and forests. The workshop was attended by more than 20 of the institution’s staff and during this event topics such as gender equality, women’s rights, and the establishment of strategic alliances were discussed. In 2016, with the support of the REDD+ Secretariat - WISF/SES/FONAFIFO organized a small discussion panel on gender and REDD+ safeguards.

## Gender-Sensitive or Gender-Responsive Projects

To date, FONAFIFO has not implemented or funded projects that include objectives and results that promote gender equality. At the institutional level, equitable measures such as affirmative actions to ensure equal representation of women in decision-making positions were taken during the preparation process, the REDD+ Secretariat implemented several actions to (a) ensure that women’s rights are respected when developing REDD+ strategies; (b) the issue of gender was discussed during the Relevant Stakeholder Self-Assessment and Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) workshops, and (c) the Gender and REDD+ Roadmap was developed.

## National System of Conservation Areas (SNAC)

### Gender Policy

The institution does not have a gender policy. The issue of gender has been identified as one of the major gaps in the institution. The institution has just conducted a gender gap analysis to identify some of the gender-related issues and challenges that it faces.

Experts in Gender and Environment: ✅

The institution does not have a gender focal point at the time of the construction process of this plan.

### Gender Sensitive Staff: ✅/❌

As an initiative, the institution has appointed an environmental management coordinator who is an expert in human rights. However, most officials have not had the space to discuss and address gender issues.

Capacity Building on Gender and Environment: ✅/❌

The institution has not yet provided any training or workshops for its staff on the links between gender and environmental issues or how to identify and address gender considerations in their initiatives. It is expected that a module on gender and protected areas will be developed in the coming months.

### Gender-Sensitive or Gender-Responsive Projects: ✅/❌

SNAC does not have any projects focused on gender, protected areas and no gender-sensitive or responsive initiatives have been designed or implemented.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAG)

### Gender Policy

- Costa Rica has a Gender Policy under the Costa Rican Agricultural Sector approved in 2003.
- In addition, in 2014, the National Council for Women's Affairs created the National Gender Commission.
- It should be noted that rural development policies and strategies recognize women as potential agents of development and include mandates and concrete actions to promote gender equality and women's empowerment.

### Experts in Gender and Environment

- MAG has an expert in gender, agriculture and climate change who is the gender focal point for the institution.
- Gender-Sensitive Staff
  - The country has identified gender as a priority issue and has assigned one of its advisors to lead the efforts and promote social equity and gender equality.
  - In addition, MAG is part of the institutions that make up the Sectoral Network on Gender and Rural Youth, established in 2010, and serves as the focal point for this network.

### Capacity Building on Gender and Environment

- The Gender Commission holds meetings every month to discuss specific cases and share experiences, lessons learned, and strengthen the capacities of the officials of various institutions that make up the Network.
- **Gender Responsive Projects**
  - MAG participates in the implementation of the Emprende Project. The strengthening of women's entrepreneurial capacities is one of the objectives of this project. MAG is part of the Technical Team of the Entrepreneurship Unit.

### National Women’s Institute (MANU)

#### Gender Policy
- It has a National Policy for Gender Equality and Equity (PGEQ 2018–2030). The policy mentions some aspects related to women's development.
- MANU is currently developing the PEG Action Plan, which has proposed the inclusion of a section on gender and the environment.

#### Experts in Gender and Environment
- It does have experts working in gender and environment. Many of them have started working on the country’s climate agenda.
- **Gender-Sensitive Staff**
  - MANU has a wide range of initiatives to promote the women's agenda, however, many of the officials have not worked on initiatives that integrate the gender perspective into environmental issues. The Gender Commission is currently reflecting on the importance of expanding the women's agenda to address environmental issues.

#### Capacity Building on Gender and Environment
- In 2013, the National Women’s Institute staff at the National Meteorological Institute, the National Emergency Commission and the community water associations (ASADAS) in human rights and gender approaches and developed training processes on gender, climate change and disasters.

with organized groups of indigenous women in the Maleku community of Guatuso and in Zapotín, as well as with the municipal committee and local emergency committees in Cañas. INAMU is resuming these efforts in 2018 by organizing a workshop on gender analysis in climate action.

### Gender-Sensitive Fund for Women

The INAMU created a Fund for the Promotion of Productive Activities and the Organization of Women (FOMUJERES), around 1400 women entrepreneurs have benefited from this fund. Some of the beneficiaries work in agro-productive activities, and some even work on agro-forestry systems.

### Directorate of Climate Change (DCC)

#### Gender Policy: ✗/✓

The DCC does not have a gender policy for the institution. However, the NDC, the Action Plan of the National Climate Change Strategy (ENCC), the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan affirm the country's commitment to promote a transformational gender approach in climate public management and to ensure that women are involved as decision-makers in all climate actions. The Government of Costa Rica has also led efforts to incorporate gender considerations into the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change since 2015. In 2017, Costa Rica presented its first National Communication to the UNFCCC.

#### Gender experts:

The DCC does not have a gender expert or gender focal point, but is supported by one of the leading female experts on gender and environment worldwide during the UNFCCC negotiations.

### Gender-Sensitive Staff

DCC officials have supported the inclusion of a gender perspective in both national and international policies related to climate change. The DCC is currently working closely with INAMU to continue to mainstream gender considerations in climate actions and to have INAMU become part of the governing council on climate change.

#### Capacity Building on Gender and Environment

The DCC has been involved in several capacity building processes organized by INAMU. They have also received support and training from one of the world's leading experts on gender and the environment.

### Gender Mainstreaming and Gender-Responsive Projects

In 2017, the General Directorate of Foreign Policy of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Directorate of Climate Change of the Ministry of Environment and Energy organized the workshop entitled "human rights, gender and climate change: inputs for the construction of the National Adaptation Plan". The DCC has ensured the gender mainstreaming of climate policies; but one of the main challenges being faced is to ensure that these are implemented, designing and implementing gender-sensitive or responsive initiatives.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The National Biodiversity Policy 2015-2030 and the National Biodiversity Strategy 2016-2025 were developed incorporating a human rights and gender-based approach and include actions and indicators to promote gender equality. However, as an institution, there is no gender policy.

Gender capacity:
CONAGEBIO does have an expert in gender, social equity and biodiversity.
Gender Sensitive Staff:
The CONAGEBIO has several officials who consider the gender issue a central theme for the conservation of biodiversity in the country. At the management level, there is not only political will but also the necessary resources to incorporate a gender perspective in policies and initiatives to conserve biodiversity in Costa Rica.

Capacity Building on Gender and Environment:
CONAGEBIO has participated in several workshops and capacity building processes organized by IUCN's Gender Office in the 1990s and 2000s. Recently, they only participated in a small discussion panel on gender and REDD+ safeguards organized by FONAFIFO in 2016.

Gender Mainstreaming:
CONAGEBIO has guaranteed gender mainstreaming in biodiversity policies; but like other institutions of the main challenges it faces is to achieve the capacities to implement these policies and adopt and implement gender-sensitive or responsive initiatives.

## Institute of Rural Development

### Gender Policy:

The institution does not have a gender policy, but the regulatory framework of Act No. 9056 (Act transformed into INDER) modified the institutional framework for sustainable rural development in the country and incorporated the vision of equality and equity as part of the principles that should guide the institution's actions. It should be noted that article 15 of this Act identifies the need to promote gender equality, respect for human rights, social inclusion, and territories under the principles of participation, solidarity, generational and gender equity, establishing a framework for the promotion of women's empowerment (other issues), in order to promote the well-being and roots of the population in the country's rural territories, within a framework of equity and sustainability, while including gender criteria.

### Gender experts: ✅

The institution does not have a gender focal point, but it does have a person who follows up on the issue on different platforms and through various projects. During the period when the institution was called IDA, there was a focal point and a gender unit that worked with women's groups in small projects. However, later they decided that gender should be considered a cross-cutting issue, but the issue was not followed up.

### Gender Sensitive Staff:

A few years ago, efforts were made to create a gender commission with representatives from the different work units (credits, geo-referencing, promotion, computing), but the commission disappeared because many of the officials did not have the capacity and sensitivity to work on the

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### Capacity Building on Gender and Environment

Inder has been working with the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Youth Sector Network for 7 years, during which several training sessions have been developed. In addition, several efforts have been made to sensitize officials on gender issues, but no workshops have been implemented to address the issue of gender mainstreaming.

### Gender-Sensitive or Gender-Responsive Projects

Inder has developed several initiatives that address the gender perspective. For example, it has collaborated with the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock and has implemented various activities such as the creation of a module on the PEDT. In addition, in 2017 the 28 women representatives of the Territorial Councils were trained to develop their own projects. The objective is to sensitize them so that they can propose actions to unite the women's groups in the territory through several workshops. Currently, the "Gender Mainstreaming in Service Projects: Land and Rural Credit Funds" is being implemented in collaboration with UNICEF and FAO.

### Support Platforms

#### Sectoral Network on Gender and Rural Youth

Established in 2010 to promote the incorporation of the gender equality agenda into the services provided by the institution, it works up the agricultural sector. The Network is made up of the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAG), the State Plant Health Service (SFE), the National Animal Health Service (SENASA), and the National Council of 4-5 Clubs (CONAC 4-5), the Institute of Agricultural Research and Training (INATEC), the National Institute of Aquaculture (INCOPESCA), the National Production Council (CNP), the National Institute of Innovation and Transference in Agricultural Technology (INTA), the Integral Agricultural Marketing System (SIMA), the National Water Office, the National Groundwater, Irrigation and Drainage (SENASA) and the Executive Secretary of Agricultural Sector Planning (SEPSA).

#### Citizens' Climate Change Advisory Council (SCG)

It is a deliberative consultative body, which aims to collaborate with the design and implementation of national climate change policies. It was created on 29 September 2017 by Executive Decree No. DS/53/17 and was formed as such in 2018. The SC is composed of representatives of the following sectors: Community (ASADAS and Development Associations), Biodiversity-Ecosystems,

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### Background to the creation of the Gender Action Plan

In recent years, the Government of Costa Rica has recognized the importance of appropriately addressing gender issues in REDD+ processes[1]. The REDD Secretariat, comprising the National Commission for Forests (CNF), and the National System of Conservation Areas (SINAC) as the executive unit, has been involved in various processes related to the preparation of the National REDD Strategy (EN-REDD). In collaboration with women gender experts, civil society organizations, various indigenous women's groups, and other stakeholders.

Through the development of the Gender Action Plan (GAP), Costa Rica becomes one of a few countries to build a GAP for its National REDD+ Strategy. Costa Rica was one of the first countries to commit to the issue and began to integrate the gender approach in the readiness stage for REDD+ since 2011. As a result, the National REDD+ Strategy reaffirms its commitment to the implementation of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) that suggests that the national REDD+ strategies or action plans must address gender considerations.

The REDD Secretariat has recognized that working with a gender perspective implies not only mentioning this value as a principle, but also identifying relevant gender considerations and specific concerns actions to promote gender equality as part of the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy. To fulfill the mandate of the UNFCCC and harmonize it with this proactive vision, Costa Rica began to include specific actions to address gender issues in its National REDD+ Strategy. The Gender Action Plan is a document where it proposed as an enabling condition, to carry out and operationalize a strategy for the participation of gender and special population groups. The first step of this strategy was the development of a Gender and REDD Roadmap for Costa Rica in 2015.

[1] Access water for the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions through afforestation, forest degradation, and the conservation, sustainable management and enhancement of forest carbon stocks.


---
**Page 208**
---

Annex XIII (c)

Gender Assessment and Action Plan

participatory process that allowed the identification of gender considerations relevant to REDD+ based on the country context, as well as gender inequalities, the situation experienced by women, lessons learned from previous forestry projects and the experiences of Relevant Stakeholders (by its acronym in Spanish, PIR).

Based on this Roadmap, in 2017 the REDD+ Secretariat proposes to develop the REDD+ GAP through a multi-stakeholder process based on the reality and proposals of Costa Rican women. This document presents an analysis of the current situation of the country's situation in terms of forests, gender and climate change mitigation, which included field visits, case studies, analysis of inequalities, opportunities, challenges and lessons learned, as well as the identification of actions to be taken by the National REDD+ Strategy.

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a concrete and detailed proposal for actions related to gender and relevant to REDD+. The action proposed by the GAP are concrete and detailed since they are based on the National REDD+ Strategy Policies, Actions and Measures. In addition, the GAP includes an analysis of the current situation in terms of gender considerations relevant to the GAPs and their Implementation Plan were analyzed in order to ensure that the expected results are not only achievable, but that they address the needs of women and men equally.

Finally, it is important to mention that since 2015, Costa Rica leads the gender negotiations within the UNFCCC and is one of the administrators of the Gender Action Plan for this Convention. This commitment is translated into national policies, where Costa Rica's National REDD+ Strategy includes a gender-sensitive approach in its implementation.

The objective of this report is to present the Gender Action Plan for the Costa Rican National REDD+ Strategy. The report includes a summary of relevant information on gender and forests obtained during the analysis of the current country situation, the analysis of the gaps and opportunities identified by women and men, as well as an analysis of the actions and indicators proposed in the action plan and an analysis of the relationship of the GAP with the main international gender mandates. This action plan also reaffirms Costa Rica's commitment to implement its gender-sensitive climate policies through a gender-responsive climate strategy. However, the National REDD+ Strategy-GAP is not only a compliance instrument; it is a concrete and novel proposal for social and environmental transformation, based on

52

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Terminology used in this report

| Term | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Gender Considerations | It occurs when gender is recognized as a key factor in analyses. |
| Gender-Sensitive | It occurs when gender is recognized as an essential issue and the norms, roles and inequalities are considered as part of its objectives. |
| Gender-Responsive | It occurs when gender is considered as a component of its expectations and outputs and includes gender indicators in its monitoring and evaluation processes. |
| Gender-Transformative | It occurs when unequal gender relations are transformed to promote control over resources, equitable decision-making and empowerment. |

## 6. Methodology

The development of the GAP was carried out by the REDD+ Secretariat with the support of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) and the World Bank. The process for developing the GAP was based on five key principles: (a) to be based on an approach led by and adapted to country circumstances; (b) develop greater knowledge of the country's current context and successful experiences; (c) build on existing capacity and structures; (d) define a strategic approach to promote gender equality in national


---
**Page 210**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

environmental and climate strategies; and (e) promote partnerships to achieve the proposed strategy's outcomes.

The development process of the GAP proposes a conceptual framework with three lines of action that encompass gender considerations relevant to the country. Most of these gender aspects were evidenced when the Gender and REDD+ Road Map for Costa Rica was drawn up in 2015 and three specific lines of action with ten gender considerations were proposed (see Figure 1). These lines of action are: (a) strengthening institutional capacities; (b) closing gaps to consider before proposing specific actions related to the National REDD+ Strategy; (c) lack of recognition of women as forest conservation agents; (d) lack of information on gender and environmental issues; and (e) need to strengthen institutional capacities and awareness on gender and environmental issues.

Figure 1: Conceptual framework for the development of the National REDD+ Strategy GAP

| Análisis de Género e Intergeneracional | Fortalecimiento y sensibilización social para promover la igualdad de género | Diseño de un proceso integral para la implementación de la Estrategia REDD+ |
|---|---|---|
| Reconocimiento del rol de las mujeres en el manejo forestal y la conservación y su importancia en la intergeneracionalidad | Incorporación de la igualdad de género en la estrategia actual y propuesta de la Estrategia REDD+ | Diseño de un Mecanismo de Participación y Representación de los géneros intergeneracionales |

that the second axis for the development of the GAP was very important since one of the greatest challenges faced by personnel working on environmental issues, according to the interviews conducted, is to have the appropriate capacities and tools to incorporate the gender perspective in their initiatives, programs, and projects. ### Table 2: Lessons Learned: Characteristics of the Process for Developing the Costa Rica GAP

The REDD Secretariat led the GAP development process.
- The active involvement of the REDD Secretariat made it possible to strengthen the capacities of public officials from government institutions, establish a clear governmental ownership of the GAP at the national level, and the objectives and actions proposed in the GAP were more specific and harmonized with the National REDD Strategy PAM and the institutional operational plans (OP) of different government institutions.

The development of the GAP was based on strategic alliances with other institutions.
- For the development and implementation of the GAP, a strategic alliance was established with the National Women's Commission (CNE), which is responsible for the promotion and protection of women's human rights in conjunction with the Costa Rican State and civil society. Through this partnership, gender issues were represented as an integral part of the national policy on environmental issues as part of the women's agenda within the National Policy for Effective Equality between Men and Women.

The development of the GAP was based on a proposed theory of change.
- Identifying the gaps and proposing three interrelated lines of action to address them, the entire GAP design process led to the identification of two main lines of action: gender analysis and capacity building. These were progressively implemented before designing the action plan to address issues in forests and forests. The lessons learned from the explicit results of these actions of the first two lines of action were the necessary enabling conditions to design the GAP and implement its actions.

The GAP was developed using a methodology that included a combination of analytical and participatory approaches.
- Thanks to this methodology, very diverse data were collected which allowed for proposing actions in the GAP and their implementation. The methodology also allowed us to identify gender-related opportunities in diverse environmental sectors. Complementing the desk research with focus groups and consultations.

### 7. National REDD+ Strategy, Forests and Gender in Costa Rica

#### Forest condition

Costa Rica has made great strides in the creation of public policies that have promoted the creation of protected wilderness areas and payment programs for environmental services, which have made it possible to combat deforestation, the recovery of forest cover, sustainable forest management, the creation of support institutions and the development of financing mechanisms for the conservation and restoration of forests. Costa Rica has 3,218,468 ha of forest cover, of which 2,438,940 ha are mature forests. Between 1986-2013, annual gross anthropogenic deforestation has remained in the range of 23,255 to 54,474 hectares per year and the net regeneration area has increased substantially and the coverage of growing secondary forests has increased steadily over time.

As a result of these efforts, Costa Rica's forest cover shows a clear recovery trend. The country went from being a loser to a net winner of native forests. Between 1997 and 2013 the net deforested area fell steadily from the beginning of the period and the net regenerating areas increased towards the end of the period, as shown in Figure 2. This evidences the country's success in designing early forest policies that allowed it to reduce emissions in the sector and maintain vital critical ecosystem functions while improving its capacity to deliver shared benefits and access opportunities to key environmental and economic resources to local communities, especially in rural areas.

Figure 2. Change in the forest area of Costa Rica for the period between 1987-2013 (km²).

Source: EN-REDD+ COSTA RICA

However, the problem of deforestation and degradation in Costa Rica has not been eradicated. Although forest cover is growing, there are other areas where deforestation continues and land uses changes continue moving from forest to other uses. Analysis of the time series of land use change shows a gradient of deforestation in mature forests that is inversely related to their protection status. In unprotected areas, the recovery of forest cover from becoming forested is among the causes to which deforestation is attributed to. On the other hand, small forest producers and farmers have been excluded from the benefits of the state's environmental management of primary and secondary natural forests; the restriction of access to PES or recognition of the value of the standing tree to owners and possessors of natural forest; the lack of control over the use of forest use after alternative use; and the weakness of the State in the implementation of control mechanisms, are also elements that encourage deforestation and degradation.

### Gender Responsive

#### National Forest Development Policy

Currently, the official planning instrument for the use, management and protection of forest resources is the National Forest Development Plan (PNFD) 2011-2020. The PNFD proposes a policy framework organized into 1 superior policy and 12 specific policies, which in turn are divided into 35 operational policies. The Government of Costa Rica is promoting, through a program composed of several initiatives, to encourage the implementation of the current PNFD, called the Program on Forests and Biodiversity.

The National Forest Development Plan 2011-2020 is the first forest policy to recognize gender as a cross-cutting theme. The Plan recognizes that, in order to ensure the conservation of the biological diversity of forest lands and their use, as well as cultural diversity and respect for the rights of society, the gender perspective must be incorporated and full participation of women in decision-making processes is required.

In the case of forest policies, these evolve from being gender-blind to being gender-sensitive (see Figure 6). Although the Forestry Law does not mention gender, some of its articles can be interpreted as having a gender dimension. The National Forest Office (ONF) has as one of its functions, to promote the constitution and strengthening of associations and organized groups for the development of the forestry sector; with emphasis on the incorporation of women and small producers. Moreover, it is


---
**Page 220**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

key to strengthen the gender capacities of the ONF to promote, in an equitable manner, the constitution and strengthening of associations and organized groups of both women and men. In addition, the ONF should recognize the roles, capacities and needs of women farmers and small producers so that they can organize themselves and use, market and industrialize their products.

The ONF is also required to implement the gender-sensitive approach as provided for in Article 10 of the Parks Service Act, which assigns as one of the functions of the ONF, to encourage programs aimed at rural communities to incorporate small landowners in reforestation programs.

## National REDD+ Strategy

For more than two decades, Costa Rica has generated REDD+ policies and actions that have been the roadmap of governments of different political parties. Since their creation in 1986.


---
**Page 221**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The Forest Fertilizer Certificate programs and later the Payment for Environmental Services (PES), have been financed mainly with national resources, such as regular budgets and the single tax on fossil fuels, and with two loans from the World Bank, complemented with donations from bilateral and multilateral sources and with the development of public-private partnerships. These financing mechanisms have contributed to a greater level of sustained commitment and international recognition of the protection of its natural resources.

Costa Rica's National REDD+ Strategy was built from a long consultation process with the PIR between 2011 and 2017. Its Policies, Actions, and Measures (PAM) are the product of the systematization of a multiplicity of social, political, environmental, risks, and commitments that have been identified in the different processes of the National REDD+ Strategy. The PAMs were developed within the official planning framework of the Costa Rican forest sector (the PNDF 2011-2020), so that they complement each other. The six National REDD+ Strategy policies are presented in the figure below:

* Policy 1: Promotion of low-carbon production systems.
* Policy 2: Strengthen prevention and control programs for land-use change and fires.
* Policy 3: Incentives for conservation and sustainable forest management.
* Policy 4: Conservation of landscapes and forest ecosystems
* Policy 5: Participation of indigenous peoples
* Policy 6: enabling conditions.

Since 2011, Costa Rica began to integrate the gender approach in the Readiness phase of REDD+. In that year, FONAFIFO, with the support of the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN), organized a workshop on gender and forests. Thanks to this recognition and initial institutional sensitization, gender was discussed in several of the processes of the readiness phase in Costa Rica. For example, during the Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) workshop, gender was identified as a necessity, so during the Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) workshop, attendees discussed the issue during the sessions. The SESA document includes specific references to address gender issues, but it also recognizes the premise that not addressing the issue would lead to the exclusion and invisibility of women; it also recognizes that limitations prevail to incorporate gender issues in the country's REDD+ process.

As a result of these efforts, the National REDD+ Strategy reaffirms its commitment to Decision 1/CP.16 and highlights paragraph 72 calling on REDD+ countries to address


---
**Page 222**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

gender considerations when developing and implementing their national strategies or action plans. In order to fulfill this mandate, Costa Rica defined the actions to develop the Gender Action Plan in such a way that this plan is articulated with the MAPS included in the National REDD+ Strategy. In addition, the Costa Rica National REDD+ Strategy Implementation Plan, which was developed by the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MMAE) and the FCPF Carbon Fund, begin to include specific actions to address gender considerations.

The National REDD+ Strategy Implementation Plan recognizes that making and operating a component to address the participation of special groups and gender as an enabling condition.

As a first step towards addressing gender issues in 2016, the Road Map for Gender and Costa Rica REDD+ was developed. The roadmap was developed through a multi-stakeholder participatory process during the workshop Defining the design of the critical path for REDD+ in Costa Rica: A gender perspective. This roadmap is based on the following:

The overall objective of the workshop was to explore the gender considerations that should be considered in REDD+ processes in Costa Rica and to propose a plan to appropriately address these issues.

The workshop was attended by 30 participants, including representatives from government, NGOs, academia, international cooperation, indigenous women's organizations, community associations and rural women linked to the generation of the Carbon Fund. The results of this workshop were presented at the National REDD+ Strategy Implementation Plan Workshop in Costa Rica on March 28-29, 2016.

In this workshop, a roadmap was defined with 4 axes of action and 10 relevant gender considerations for the REDD+ process in Costa Rica. For each axis, expected results, actions and organizations that could lead them were proposed. In addition, this roadmap was used as a guide for the development of the Gender Action Plan for the National REDD+ Strategy.

## Analysis of gender roles, gaps and opportunities

This GAP is based on a comprehensive gender analysis that recognizes gender-differentiated roles, gaps and opportunities in the forest sector. Traditionally, gender analysis tends to focus only on gender gaps; but a comprehensive gender analysis goes beyond this by recognizing the different roles, responsibilities, constraints, opportunities and rights that exist between men, women, girls and boys in certain situations and contexts[1]. Conducting a holistic gender analysis allows for an understanding of the context within which REDD+ is implemented. This leads to more informed and more comprehensive actions. As a result, the objectives and actions proposed in the GAP have the potential not only to close the gaps identified but also to create new paths for


---
**Page 223**

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Costa Rican women and men who live in, depend on, and protect the country’s forests and biodiversity.

Gender analysis made it possible to better understand the reality of Costa Rican women and to obtain quantitative and qualitative data on gender roles, gaps and opportunities. At the local and national levels, women face gender inequalities in land tenure, participation in decision-making, training, and access to information, access to and control over economic resources, and access to services such as credit and extension.

Women from different regions are interested in various types of activities to reduce deforestation and forest degradation. The following is a summary of the data and information obtained from the interviews with women who participated in focus groups held during workshops. It should be noted that there is a wide range of gender inequalities at the national level, for example, those related to domestic violence or the care economy, which are not included in this document.

The following table summarizes some of the gaps and opportunities identified through focus group interviews with women who were mentioned during interviews, local focus groups and workshops held as part of its development.


| Gender Roles |
| --- |
| Rural women in Costa Rica are involved in various activities related to agricultural and forestry production. A recent study by the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAG) identified that women are involved in the production of agricultural products and other related goods and services such as the processing of agricultural products and administrative tasks. The MAG study also indicated that women participate in multiple activities in the production cycle on a permanent basis. However, during the focus groups it was mentioned on multiple occasions that women’s agricultural work and labour are not recognized by society or by the government, and they are often stereotyped as being responsible for domestic chores. |
| At the national level, there are specific gender roles associated with agricultural and forestry activities. On the farms of women producers, there is a smaller percentage of women doing agricultural work and a larger concentration in administrative and other work compared to men, who concentrate more on the development of agricultural activities that can be used for other purposes such as by-products, rural tourism, and waste treatment (Figure 6). |

In terms of crops, coffee is predominant in all regions of the country, both in the number of farms, as well as in the land area it occupies for both sexes of producers. However, apart from coffee, there are five main crops that stand out in terms of the land extension planted after coffee are rice, palm oil, beans, corn and sugar cane. In the case of women producers, the five crops that occupy most of the land area are: rice (51%), palm oil (38%), beans (20%), corn (17%) and sugar cane (Figure 8[1]). Women's farms are almost as diverse as men's in terms of crops, despite having fewer farms and less land to plant[6]. Farms run by women producers get a total of 278 crops, while those belonging to male producers have 359 crops. This diversity means that it is possible to establish agricultural systems for the development of specific products, especially farms with avocados, cocoa, coffee, heart of palm, plantain and banana crops where agroforestry systems could be established as programs to strengthen the productive system[7]. The information provided shows us that there are differences in the sizes of the farms, so the proposed models and practices should also be adapted to this diversity in this diversity of farms. This diversity also allows for the creation of more land areas managed by both women and men, even though they have fewer properties and are smaller in size.

Although the main land uses are similar between farms of female and male producers, there are differences related to preferences between different uses. When analysing the information on agricultural census (a disaggregated way)[8], it is observed that the main use of the land for women farmers is natural forests (20% of the total area), followed by permanent crops (15%), and then by natural fallows (9%). In contrast, men carry out their activities mainly in natural forests (34%) and permanent crops (28% of their farms). This reflects, to a certain extent, the involvement of women in livestock activities; however, the number of heads of cattle and the intensity with which women carry out these activities are lower than those carried out by men. The difference in land use can be seen in the percentage of hectares devoted to forests, permanent crops and improved pastures. In the case of male producers, the main uses are natural forests (23% of the total area), followed by permanent crops (20% of the total ha.), followed by improved pastures (15%). These data are consistent with the greater involvement of men in livestock activities. In the case of women producers, the order of importance varies since the main uses are natural forests (34% of the total of their farms),[9]

In contrast, women have a little more natural regeneration area (1% and 3% of the farms of the male and the female producers respectively) [Figure 9]. These data are also consistent with forest production systems in which men are more involved, since men are generally more interested in marketing timber and women in selling and using non-timber forest products such as seeds, medicinal plants, among others.

## Gender gaps

The gender gap analysis took as its starting point the conceptual framework on social equity recognition of the last decade of the work of the Commission on Biological Diversity (CBD) [1] as guidance on effective and equitable governance models and has been used to analyse REDD+ processes [2]. The conceptual framework proposes that social equity has three dimensions: recognition, values, and participation. In the context of the CBD, "recognition refers to the recognition of rights and the diversity of identities, values, knowledge systems and institutions of rights-holders and stakeholders; procedure refers to the process of decision-making in rules and procedures for making-and distribution-might imply that the costs and benefits resulting from natural resource management must be shared.


---
**Page 227**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

equitably among different actors[1]. These three dimensions are framed in a broader context, so the gender inequalities identified for each dimension must consider the pre-existing inequalities created by political, economic, and social conditions, which determine who can enjoy their rights, participate and benefit from benefit sharing. This conceptual framework is based on: (a) the principles of equity and non-discrimination; (b) international environmental mandates of various conventions (see Annex 3); (b) implementing novel methodologies that reflect the rights and social equity approach that the country has adopted in its national legislation, to ensure that the country has the flexibility to seek support to implement the proposed actions.

The summary of the gender gap analysis is presented below. The gaps are divided into the three dimensions of social equity mentioned above, and for each one the quantitative and qualitative data obtained during the analysis are included in the development of the GAP. Qualitative data come from focus group discussions and information obtained during interviews.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Annex XIII (c)

### Gender gaps related to recognition

#### Invizibilization of women in the agricultural and environmental sector

In Costa Rica there are about 12,598 women producers[1]; however, in many cases they themselves do not recognize their contributions and consider these activities as part of their domestic chores[4]. During the field visits it was verified that many women work in production and processing of crops and by-products. The fact that women have a greater participation in the development of by-products is also due to the fact that women have a greater participation in the development of by-products, surpassing men by twice as much (11,645 women and 5,637 men).

Women's farm work is generally considered informal and unpaid. Data from the 2014 Agricultural Census show that 72.8% of the women who work on the farms, in general, do not receive any type of payment or compensation. An analysis of these data shows that 17.2% of women have permanently paid labour, 48% of women have permanently unpaid labour[5], and 36% of men have permanently paid labour, while 45.8% of men have temporarily unpaid labour. In contrast, 27.6% of men have permanently unpaid labour, 9.3% of men have temporarily unpaid labour and 17.3% of men have temporarily unpaid labour (Figure 10)[6].

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Programs and projects generally consider the productive and environmental sector as a uniform sector. During several of the field interviews conducted, it was detected that one of the problems in the agricultural and environmental sector is the use of a language that is not inclusive and only refers to producers and does not evidence the diversity of actors and activities involved.

In Costa Rica, according to the 2014 Agricultural Census, women are more associated with domestic and care activities. Women, in many cases, are not considered producers with differentiated roles, characteristics and needs because they are associated with domestic and care activities. During the field visits, participants were asked to identify the activities carried out by men and those carried out by women. It was observed that women were more often associated with the domestic and care environment, while men were more often associated with economic activities[25]. When asked for more details about agricultural activities, participants said that in many cases they did not know what their wives or daughters did because these are activities that they considered to require strenuous physical effort such as cutting down trees. This pattern coincides with the findings of INAMU which emphasize that "rural women combine domestic and care work with agricultural production (e.g., processing coffee, making tortillas), making and selling products (cheese, breads, handicrafts), sowing and harvesting basic grains for subsistence, among others"[26]. The 2014 Agricultural Census also recognizes that a high percentage of women are involved in the production of food crops[27].

In addition, analysis of data on other agricultural activities shows that, on chicken farms, the highest percentages of women producers are found on subsistence agriculture farms[28].

Women's properties cover a smaller area and are smaller than men's properties. At the national level, women own only 7.1% of agricultural land in their names. In fact, 95.4% of the land (1,210,243.8 ha) is owned by men, and 4.4% (106,563.6 ha) is owned by women.

For the present gender analysis, a characterization was made according to the size of the farms and their extension in hectares. The results show that most of the farms are small: more than half of them have an area less than 5 hectares (51%). In fact, only 302 farms have more than 50 ha (3%) [7]. Compared to men's farms, the biggest difference is found with regard to male producers' farms tend to be larger, where 46% of farms are larger than 5 ha and 4,000 farms have more than 50 ha (7%).

Most of the spaces with agricultural production that are not farms belong to women[3]. A disaggregated analysis of this information shows that 53.8% of these spaces are run by women. This situation was evidenced in interviews and focus groups where it was determined that women have more difficulty formalizing their land tenure than men, especially when it is more difficult for them to formalize their land tenure. This pattern, where the number of productive spaces that are not farms is greater for women, is similar in all provinces except for Limón.

The difference between the number of productive spaces that are not farms and the number of productive spaces that are farms owned by men and women is Limón, which makes one presume that perhaps women from Limón have more barriers to formalize their land tenure than those of other provinces.

There are now regulations and policies to promote women's land tenure. In 1990, the Agrarian Development Institute (IDA, now INDER Rural Development Institute), changed the way in which titles were allocated for agricultural production. These changes were part of these efforts: one is to register title to property in the names of men and women when the applicants are married or are domestic partners; and [b] implement

[3] According to the Agricultural Census, spaces with agricultural production that are not farms are those properties where agricultural activities related to housing are carried out, such as the presence of poorly organized livestock for personal consumption, mainly for food consumption provided that they are maintained during the productive period.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Annex XIII (c)

Specific regulations for allocating land to female heads of household who apply for it. According to INDEF, between 2014-2016 a total of 303 farms have been allocated to women nationwide and during the same period 740 titles of ownership were also held throughout the country. During this period women had 30% more access to land than men. Despite these advances, however, Costa Rican women continue to experience enormous gender inequality in land tenure.

The land tenure gap between men and women in Indigenous Territories is much smaller than in lands that are not Indigenous Territories. In the country's 24 indigenous territories, 32.7% of the farms owned by indigenous producers are owned by women. The 2014 census identified a total of 5896 farms managed by indigenous men and women in these territories, which 3,051 are run by indigenous female producers (2,052 are run by men and 999 by indigenous women). It should be noted that the smallest differences in land tenure can be observed in the Cabécar ethnic territory. In this territory, 48% of farms are managed by indigenous women. The indigenous territory with the smallest difference in land tenure is Talamanca Bribrí, where 287 farms are managed by indigenous men and 275 farms by indigenous women.

The **Bribri Cabécar villages** are those where there is the largest number of indigenous women running farms. Map 1 shows that the indigenous territories where there is greater participation of indigenous women in agricultural production are Chirripó with 326 women, Cabécar and Talamanca Bribrí with 275 women. Other Cabécar ethnic territories also stand out for their high number of female producers: Talamancas with 140 women producers, and Cabarga territory of the Bribri ethnic group, with 44 women. These villages maintain a matrilineal system where women inherit the land and in turn succeed the ancestors' rights to it. During the ancestral assembly held in Talamanca Bribrí in the Cabécar territories of Talamanca, the communities reaffirmed that, for them, it is of utmost importance to value and maintain in force the ancestral practices and traditions, which include this matrilineal system. Despite this matrilineal structure and the support of the communities, many women claim that they still do not have formal recognition of property.

Gender-specific contributions and knowledge related to forest conservation and management are not recognized.

At the national level, women are actively involved in the conservation and sustainable management of forests, but their contributions have not been adequately recognized. In reviewing the literature during the gender analysis, it was observed that, despite the efforts of some institutions, much of the information on experiences of gender-sensitive forest management is not available. The majority of the information found as anecdotes, notes or newspaper articles. SINAC's institutional gender gap analysis11 showed that of the 216 officials surveyed, 78% said they were unaware of environmental initiatives that incorporate a gender perspective.

It should be noted that those public officials who are aware of environmental initiatives that incorporate the gender perspective mentioned at least 25 projects that contribute to the conservation and management of biodiversity and promote gender equality. During the

11 UNDP Costa Rica, 2018. Institutional gender gap analysis UNDP, GEF and SINAC.

77

**Page 234**
